SOAFAM Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C ($12,500, equal to the $15,000 loss minus the $2,500 deductible amount): Under an ordinary deductible, the insurer pays the loss amount minus the deductible whenever the loss exceeds the deductible. Here the loss of $15,000 exceeds the $2,500 deductible, so the insurer's payment is $15,000 minus $2,500, which equals $12,500. The policyholder retains the first $2,500 of the loss. No coinsurance or policy limit reduces this payment further in this scenario.
  2. The correct answer is A ($0, because the loss does not exceed the stated deductible amount): An ordinary deductible requires the loss to exceed the deductible before the insurer owes any payment. Since the loss of $1,800 is less than the $2,500 deductible, the insurer pays nothing and the policyholder bears the entire $1,800 loss. This illustrates how ordinary deductibles eliminate small claims from insurer payments entirely.
  3. The correct answer is E ($6,000, equal to the entire loss since it exceeds the deductible): Under a franchise deductible, once the loss exceeds the stated threshold, the insurer pays the full amount of the loss rather than subtracting the deductible. Since $6,000 exceeds the $5,000 franchise deductible, the insurer pays the entire $6,000 loss. This is the key feature that distinguishes a franchise deductible from an ordinary deductible.
  4. The correct answer is B ($0, because the loss does not exceed the franchise deductible amount): A franchise deductible pays nothing at all when the loss falls at or below the stated threshold. Since the $4,000 loss does not exceed the $5,000 franchise deductible, the insurer owes no payment and the policyholder bears the full loss. Had the loss exceeded $5,000, the insurer would have paid the entire loss amount instead.
  5. The correct answer is D ($40,000, equal to the policy limit since the loss exceeds it): A policy limit caps the maximum amount the insurer will pay on any single covered loss. Since the $50,000 loss exceeds the $40,000 policy limit, the insurer's payment is capped at $40,000 and the policyholder bears the remaining $10,000 of the loss. The policy limit therefore bounds the insurer's payment from above.
  6. The correct answer is B ($15,200, equal to 80% of the loss remaining after the deductible): First subtract the $1,000 deductible from the $20,000 loss to get $19,000. The 80% coinsurance provision then applies to this remaining amount, so the insurer pays 80% of $19,000, which equals $15,200. The policyholder is responsible for the $1,000 deductible plus the remaining 20% coinsurance share of $3,800.
  7. The correct answer is E ($80,000, equal to the policy limit, which caps the insurer's payment): After subtracting the $5,000 deductible, the remaining loss is $95,000. Comparing this to the $80,000 policy limit, the limit is the smaller amount, so the insurer's payment is capped at $80,000. The policyholder bears the $5,000 deductible plus the $15,000 of loss above the policy limit.
  8. The correct answer is A (0.40, computed as the limited expected value divided by the expected loss): The loss elimination ratio is defined as E[X wedge d] divided by E[X], representing the proportion of expected losses eliminated by the deductible. Dividing $4,000 by $10,000 gives 0.40. This means the deductible eliminates 40% of the expected loss amount from the insurer's payment obligation.
  9. The correct answer is D (A franchise deductible pays the full loss once the loss exceeds the deductible amount): Once a loss exceeds a franchise deductible, the insurer pays the entire loss with no subtraction, unlike an ordinary deductible which always subtracts the deductible amount from any loss that exceeds it. This is the defining structural difference between the two deductible types. Below the threshold, both deductible types produce a zero payment from the insurer.
  10. The correct answer is C ($40,000, equal to the maximum payment cap stated in the policy): Subtracting the $2,000 deductible from the $60,000 loss leaves $58,000, and 90% of that amount is $52,200. Since this raw coinsurance payment of $52,200 exceeds the stated $40,000 maximum payment, the insurer's actual payment is capped at $40,000. The maximum payment provision overrides the coinsurance calculation whenever the coinsurance result exceeds the cap.
  11. The correct answer is A (It decreases, because more of the loss distribution is now retained by the policyholder): As the deductible increases, E[X wedge d] increases toward E[X], so the insurer's expected payment per loss, equal to E[X] minus E[X wedge d], falls. A larger share of the loss distribution now falls below the deductible and is retained entirely by the policyholder. This relationship holds for any loss distribution, not just a specific family of distributions.
  12. The correct answer is D ($8,050, equal to the full loss since it exceeds the deductible): Because the $8,050 loss exceeds the $8,000 franchise deductible, however slightly, the franchise provision requires the insurer to pay the entire loss amount rather than subtracting the deductible. This all-or-nothing structure means even a small excess above the threshold triggers a payment equal to the full loss. If the loss had been $8,000 or less, the insurer would have paid nothing.
  13. The correct answer is B ($0, because the loss does not strictly exceed the franchise deductible amount): The franchise deductible provision requires the loss to strictly exceed the stated threshold before any payment is triggered. Since the loss of exactly $5,000 equals but does not exceed the $5,000 deductible, the insurer pays nothing on this claim. A loss of even one dollar more would have triggered a full payment of the entire loss amount instead.
  14. The correct answer is E ($95,000, equal to the full loss since it is below the limit): A policy limit only reduces the insurer's payment when the loss exceeds the limit. Since the $95,000 loss is below the $100,000 policy limit, the insurer pays the entire loss amount with no reduction. The policy limit only becomes binding once losses grow larger than the stated limit itself.
  15. The correct answer is C ($100,000, equal to the policy limit, which caps the insurer's payment): Subtracting the $10,000 deductible from the $150,000 loss leaves $140,000, but the policy limit of $100,000, equal to the maximum covered loss minus the deductible, caps the insurer's payment at $100,000. The policyholder therefore retains the $10,000 deductible plus the $40,000 of loss above the maximum covered loss.
  16. The correct answer is D (To give the policyholder an incentive to share in losses and insure adequately): A coinsurance provision requires the policyholder to bear a fixed percentage of each loss, which discourages moral hazard and encourages the policyholder to carry adequate coverage relative to the value of the insured property. Without such a provision, the policyholder would have little incentive to control losses or purchase sufficient coverage. This is distinct from the property coinsurance clause but serves a similar risk-sharing purpose.
  17. The correct answer is A ($16,000, computed as the expected loss minus the limited expected value shown): The insurer's expected payment per loss under an ordinary deductible equals E[X] minus E[X wedge d]. Subtracting $9,000 from $25,000 gives $16,000. This formula reflects that the insurer pays the full expected loss except for the portion retained by the policyholder through the deductible, captured by the limited expected value term.
  18. The correct answer is C ($19,000, computed by adding the franchise adjustment term to the ordinary result): The expected payment under a franchise deductible equals the ordinary deductible expected payment plus d times the probability that the loss exceeds d, since the franchise provision restores the deductible amount for every loss that triggers a payment. Adding $5,000 times 0.6, which equals $3,000, to the ordinary result of $16,000 gives $19,000. This confirms that a franchise deductible always produces an expected payment at least as large as the corresponding ordinary deductible.
  19. The correct answer is E ($18,500, equal to the limited expected value given in the problem): For a policy with no deductible and only a policy limit u, the insurer's expected payment per loss equals E[X wedge u] directly, since the limit simply caps any loss exceeding u while all losses below u are paid in full. Because E[X wedge 30,000] is given as $18,500, the insurer's expected payment per loss equals that same value without any further adjustment.
  20. The correct answer is B (It increases, since a higher deductible eliminates a larger share of expected losses): The loss elimination ratio equals E[X wedge d] divided by E[X], and E[X wedge d] increases as d increases, approaching E[X] in the limit. A larger deductible therefore eliminates a greater proportion of the expected loss from the insurer's payment obligation, raising the loss elimination ratio. This relationship does not depend on reinsurance arrangements at all.
  21. The correct answer is E ($33,750, equal to 75% of the full loss amount incurred): With no deductible in place, the coinsurance percentage applies directly to the entire loss amount. Multiplying $45,000 by 75% gives $33,750, which is the insurer's payment. The policyholder retains the remaining 25% coinsurance share, equal to $11,250, under the terms of this provision.
  22. The correct answer is C ($90,000, equal to the maximum payment cap stated in the policy): Subtracting the $1,000 deductible from the $200,000 loss leaves $199,000, and 70% of that remaining amount is $139,300. Because this raw coinsurance result of $139,300 exceeds the stated $90,000 maximum payment, the insurer's actual payment is capped at $90,000. The maximum payment provision therefore governs the final claim amount in this scenario.
  23. The correct answer is D (The amount of loss the ceding insurer keeps for its own account): Retention refers to the portion of any given loss or exposure that the ceding insurer does not cede to the reinsurer and instead retains for its own account. The remaining amount above the retention is transferred to the reinsurer under the terms of the treaty. Retention is a central concept in both proportional and non-proportional reinsurance arrangements.
  24. The correct answer is A ($24,000, equal to 30% of the total loss amount incurred): Under a quota share treaty, the reinsurer's share of every loss equals the fixed cession percentage applied to the total loss. Multiplying the $80,000 loss by the 30% ceded share gives $24,000. The ceding insurer retains the remaining 70%, equal to $56,000, for its own account under the terms of the treaty.
  25. The correct answer is B ($20,000, equal to 40% of the total loss amount incurred): Since the ceding insurer retains 60% of each risk under the quota share treaty, the reinsurer is responsible for the remaining 40%. Multiplying the $50,000 loss by 40% gives $20,000, which is the reinsurer's payment. The ceding insurer retains the remaining $30,000 for its own account.
  26. The correct answer is D ($50,000, equal to the surplus fraction of the insured value applied to the loss): The surplus ceded equals the insured value of $350,000 minus the $100,000 retention line, giving $250,000, so the ceded fraction of the risk is 250,000 divided by 350,000, or five sevenths. Applying this fraction to the $70,000 loss gives five sevenths of $70,000, which equals $50,000. The ceding insurer retains the remaining two sevenths of the loss, equal to $20,000.
  27. The correct answer is B (Surplus share cedes only the value exceeding the insurer's retention line): Under surplus share reinsurance, the ceding insurer keeps a fixed dollar retention line on every risk and cedes only the insured value above that line, so the ceded percentage varies by risk size. This contrasts with quota share, where the same fixed percentage is ceded on every risk regardless of its size. Larger risks therefore have a higher ceded percentage under surplus share than smaller risks do.
  28. The correct answer is A ($400,000, equal to the loss amount minus the ceding insurer's retention): Since the $900,000 loss falls between the $500,000 retention and the $1,500,000 top of the layer, the excess over retention has not exhausted the reinsurer's $1,000,000 layer limit. The reinsurer therefore pays the full excess of the loss over the retention, which is $900,000 minus $500,000, equal to $400,000. The ceding insurer retains the first $500,000 of the loss.
  29. The correct answer is C ($800,000, equal to the retention plus the amount above the reinsurance layer): The reinsurer's layer covers losses between $500,000 and $1,500,000, so it pays its full $1,000,000 limit since the loss exceeds the top of the layer. The ceding insurer retains the initial $500,000 plus the amount above $1,500,000, which is $1,800,000 minus $1,500,000, equal to $300,000. Adding these two retained pieces together gives a total retained amount of $800,000.
  30. The correct answer is E ($750,000, equal to the aggregate losses minus the stated retention amount): An aggregate stop-loss treaty pays the amount by which total aggregate losses across the whole book exceed the stated retention for the period. Subtracting the $2,000,000 retention from the $2,750,000 total aggregate losses gives $750,000, which is the reinsurer's payment. The ceding insurer retains the first $2,000,000 of aggregate losses for the year regardless of how those losses are distributed among individual claims.
  31. The correct answer is A (An unexpectedly high total of claims across the whole book of business): Aggregate stop-loss reinsurance protects the ceding insurer against adverse fluctuations in the total, or aggregate, losses across an entire portfolio over a defined period, rather than protecting against any single large claim. This is what distinguishes it from excess-of-loss reinsurance, which responds to individual large claims instead. The trigger for payment is the aggregate total exceeding the stated retention, not any one claim's size.
  32. The correct answer is D (The risk and volatility it can safely retain against the cost of reinsurance): A ceding insurer selecting a retention level under an excess-of-loss treaty weighs how much loss volatility its own surplus can absorb against the reinsurance premium it must pay for protection above that retention. A lower retention buys more protection but costs more in premium, while a higher retention reduces premium cost but exposes the insurer to greater volatility. This tradeoff is central to designing an effective reinsurance program.
  33. The correct answer is C (Surplus share lets the insurer keep small risks fully while limiting large ones): Because surplus share only cedes the insured value above a fixed retention line, small risks whose value falls below the line are kept entirely by the ceding insurer, while large risks are proportionally limited by ceding the excess. This produces more efficient risk retention across a book of widely varying risk sizes than a uniform quota share percentage would. Quota share cedes the same percentage of every risk regardless of size, which wastes reinsurance cost on small, low-risk policies.
  34. The correct answer is B (4 lines, computed by dividing the ceded surplus amount by the retention line): The surplus ceded on this risk equals the insured value of $400,000 minus the $80,000 retention line, giving $320,000. Dividing this ceded surplus by the $80,000 retention line, which defines one line of capacity, gives $320,000 divided by $80,000, equal to 4 lines of surplus accepted by the reinsurer. The ceding insurer retains one line, or $80,000, for its own account.
  35. The correct answer is E ($1,000,000, equal to Reinsurer B's full layer limit, since the loss exceeds it): Reinsurer A's layer runs from $500,000 to $1,000,000 and is fully exhausted since the loss far exceeds $1,000,000, so Reinsurer A pays its full $500,000 limit. Reinsurer B's layer runs from $1,000,000 to $2,000,000, and since the $2,200,000 loss exceeds the top of this layer as well, Reinsurer B also pays its full $1,000,000 limit. The remaining $200,000 above $2,000,000 reverts to the ceding insurer.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D ($18,000, equal to 50% of the full loss amount incurred here): With no deductible in place, the coinsurance percentage applies directly to the entire loss. Multiplying $36,000 by 50% gives $18,000, which is the insurer's payment. The policyholder retains the remaining 50% of the loss, also equal to $18,000, under this coinsurance arrangement.
  2. The correct answer is A (0.225, computed as the limited expected value divided by the expected loss): The loss elimination ratio equals E[X wedge d] divided by E[X]. Dividing $1,800 by $8,000 gives 0.225, meaning this $2,000 deductible eliminates 22.5% of the expected loss from the insurer's payment obligation. The remaining 77.5% of the expected loss, corresponding to option C, still falls on the insurer after the deductible is applied.
  3. The correct answer is E (The franchise deductible payment is always greater than or equal to the ordinary payment): For every loss below d, both deductible types pay nothing, but for every loss above d, the franchise deductible pays the full loss while the ordinary deductible pays only the loss minus d, which is a smaller amount. Since the franchise payment is never less than the ordinary payment for any realized loss, its expected value is also never less than the ordinary deductible's expected payment. This holds for any loss distribution, not a special case.
  4. The correct answer is C ($4,800, equal to 40% of the gross premium charged on the risk): Under a quota share treaty, the reinsurer receives the same fixed percentage of premium that it accepts in loss exposure. Multiplying the $12,000 gross premium by the 40% ceded share gives $4,800. The ceding insurer retains the remaining 60% of the premium, equal to $7,200, along with 60% of any losses on the risk.
  5. The correct answer is B ($50,000, equal to the maximum payment cap stated in the policy): Subtracting the $5,000 deductible from the $100,000 loss leaves $95,000, and 80% of that remaining amount is $76,000. Since this raw coinsurance result of $76,000 exceeds the stated $50,000 maximum payment, the insurer's actual payment is capped at $50,000. The maximum payment provision governs the final claim amount whenever the coinsurance calculation would otherwise exceed it.
  6. The correct answer is C (The insurer pays $7,500 after subtracting the ordinary deductible from the loss): an ordinary deductible reduces every covered loss dollar for dollar by the stated deductible amount. Here the loss of $8,500 minus the $1,000 deductible leaves $7,500 payable by the insurer. Ordinary deductibles never reduce the payment below zero, but since $8,500 exceeds $1,000, the full deductible applies here. No coinsurance or policy limit was stated, so no further adjustment is needed.
  7. The correct answer is A (The insurer pays $0 because the loss falls below the franchise threshold amount): a franchise deductible pays nothing at all when the loss stays below the stated franchise amount. Here the loss of $1,200 is less than the $1,500 franchise threshold, so the insurer has no payment obligation. This differs from an ordinary deductible, which would still apply arithmetically even below the threshold in some designs. Franchise deductibles are an all-or-nothing arrangement below the stated amount.
  8. The correct answer is E (The insurer pays $6,000, the full loss, since it exceeds the franchise threshold): once a loss exceeds the stated franchise amount, the insurer pays the entire loss with no reduction at all. Here $6,000 exceeds the $2,000 franchise threshold, so the deductible amount is not subtracted anywhere. This is the key distinction from an ordinary deductible, which would still subtract $2,000 even on a large loss. Franchise deductibles therefore behave like a trigger rather than a per-loss reduction.
  9. The correct answer is B (The insurer pays $40,000, the maximum covered loss reduced by the deductible): the maximum covered loss caps the loss amount before the deductible is applied, since $50,000 exceeds the $42,000 cap. The payment equals the minimum of the loss and the maximum covered loss, minus the minimum of the loss and the deductible, giving $42,000 minus $2,000 equals $40,000. The $50,000 raw loss is never used directly because the covered loss cap governs first. No coinsurance factor was stated, so it is not applied.
  10. The correct answer is D (The insurer pays $7,500, the coinsurance factor applied to the loss above the deductible): the deductible is subtracted first, leaving $10,500 minus $500 equals $10,000, and then the coinsurance factor of 0.75 is applied to that amount. This gives 0.75 times $10,000 equals $7,500 payable by the insurer. Applying coinsurance before the deductible, as in one distractor, reverses the standard order and produces an incorrect result. The insured retains the $500 deductible plus 25% of the remaining loss.
  11. The correct answer is A (The insurer pays $16,000, the coinsurance factor applied to the covered loss above the deductible): the maximum covered loss of $21,000 first caps the $30,000 loss, then the $1,000 deductible is subtracted, leaving $20,000, and finally the 0.80 coinsurance factor is applied to give $16,000. Skipping the deductible subtraction, as in one distractor, overstates the payment. Skipping the covered loss cap, as in another distractor, also overstates the payment. All three limitations must be applied in the correct order for the answer to be accurate.
  12. The correct answer is C (The loss elimination ratio equals forty percent, dividing the limited expected value by total expected loss): the loss elimination ratio is defined as the expected loss limited to the deductible divided by the overall expected loss, computed here as $4,000 divided by $10,000. That division produces exactly 0.40, or forty percent. This ratio represents the proportion of the insurer's total expected loss cost that the deductible eliminates. The remaining sixty percent of expected loss cost still falls on the insurer above the deductible.
  13. The correct answer is E (The loss elimination ratio increases monotonically, approaching one as the deductible grows larger): as the deductible size increases, the expected loss limited to the deductible captures a larger and larger share of total expected loss. This drives the loss elimination ratio steadily upward, approaching one as the deductible approaches the maximum possible loss. The ratio never decreases as the deductible increases, since a larger deductible can only eliminate an equal or greater share of expected loss cost. It also can never fall below zero or above one by definition.
  14. The correct answer is B (The loss elimination ratio equals thirty percent, dividing the limited expected loss by total expected loss): dividing the $750 expected loss limited to the deductible by the $2,500 overall expected loss gives 0.30, or thirty percent. This means the $1,000 deductible eliminates roughly thirty percent of the insurer's total expected loss cost on this policy. The remaining seventy percent of expected loss cost is still exposed to the insurer above the deductible. The seventy five percent distractor mistakenly inverts the numerator and denominator of the ratio.
  15. The correct answer is D (The insurer retains $72,000, sixty percent of the loss under this quota share treaty): a 40% quota share cession means the reinsurer takes 40% of the loss, leaving the ceding insurer with the remaining 60%. Applying 60% to the $120,000 loss gives $72,000 retained by the insurer. The distractor claiming forty percent is retained confuses the ceded share with the retained share. Quota share reinsurance always affects both parties proportionally, so the full loss is never retained alone.
  16. The correct answer is E (The reinsurer pays $27,000, thirty percent of the loss under this quota share treaty): applying the stated 30% cession percentage directly to the $90,000 loss gives $27,000 payable by the reinsurer. The ceding insurer keeps the remaining $63,000 as its retained share of the loss. The distractor using 18,000 mistakenly applies the retained 70% share as if it were 20%, which is inconsistent with the stated percentages. Quota share cessions apply the identical fixed percentage to every claim under the treaty.
  17. The correct answer is A (The reinsurer pays $30,000, seventy five percent of the loss under this surplus share cession): the cession fraction under surplus share equals the sum insured minus the retention line, divided by the sum insured, giving $150,000 divided by $200,000 equals 75%. That same 75% fraction applies proportionally to every loss on the policy, including this $40,000 loss, so the reinsurer pays $30,000. The insurer retains the remaining 25%, or $10,000, on this loss. The fraction is fixed at policy inception and applied consistently to all losses on that policy.
  18. The correct answer is B (The reinsurer pays $40,000, about two thirds of the loss under this surplus share cession): the cession fraction equals the sum insured minus the retention line, divided by the sum insured, giving $200,000 divided by $300,000, which is approximately 66.67%. Applying that fraction to the $60,000 loss gives approximately $40,000 payable by the reinsurer. The insurer retains the remaining one third, or $20,000, on this same loss. This fixed fraction applies uniformly to every loss on the policy regardless of the loss size.
  19. The correct answer is C (The reinsurer pays $250,000, the loss above the retention within the reinsured layer): under excess of loss reinsurance, the reinsurer pays the portion of the loss above the retention, up to the stated layer limit. Here the loss of $350,000 minus the $100,000 retention equals $250,000, which is fully within the $400,000 layer limit. The ceding insurer retains the first $100,000 of this loss for its own account. Since $250,000 does not exceed the $400,000 available capacity, the entire excess is ceded to the reinsurer.
  20. The correct answer is D (The reinsurer pays $0 because the loss is entirely below the retention amount): excess of loss reinsurance only responds to the portion of a loss that exceeds the stated retention. Since the $150,000 loss is smaller than the $200,000 retention, none of it reaches the reinsured layer, so the reinsurer owes nothing. The ceding insurer retains the entire $150,000 loss for its own account. This illustrates that excess of loss treaties provide no recovery at all for losses that never reach the attachment point.
  21. The correct answer is B (The insurer retains $100,000, combining the amount below retention and the amount above the layer): the insurer keeps the first $50,000 below the attachment point, and the reinsurer's payment is capped at the $200,000 layer limit, covering losses up to $250,000. Anything above $250,000, which here is $300,000 minus $250,000 equals $50,000, reverts back to the insurer since the layer is exhausted. Adding the $50,000 retained below attachment to the $50,000 retained above the layer top gives $100,000 total retained by the insurer. The reinsurer pays exactly $200,000, the full stated layer limit, in this scenario.
  22. The correct answer is E (The reinsurer pays $150,000, the aggregate loss above the retention within the layer): aggregate stop-loss reinsurance responds to total losses across the whole book once they exceed the stated aggregate retention. Here $650,000 minus the $500,000 retention equals $150,000, which is fully within the $300,000 layer limit. The ceding insurer retains the first $500,000 of aggregate losses for its own account. Because $150,000 does not exceed the available $300,000 layer capacity, the entire excess amount is ceded to the reinsurer.
  23. The correct answer is A (The reinsurer pays $0 because aggregate losses remain below the stated retention): an aggregate stop-loss treaty pays nothing until the ceding insurer's total losses for the covered period exceed the stated aggregate retention. Here the $450,000 in total losses never reaches the $500,000 retention, so no amount is ceded to the reinsurer. The ceding insurer retains the entire $450,000 in aggregate losses for its own account this year. This illustrates that aggregate stop-loss protection provides no benefit in a year with favorable overall loss experience.
  24. The correct answer is D (The insurer retains $550,000, combining the retention and the aggregate losses above the layer): the reinsurer's maximum payment under this layer is $200,000, covering aggregate losses between $400,000 and $600,000. Since total losses of $750,000 exceed the $600,000 top of the layer, the amount above $600,000, which is $150,000, reverts back to the insurer. Adding the $400,000 retention plus the $150,000 above the layer top gives $550,000 retained by the insurer overall. The reinsurer's payment is capped at exactly $200,000 despite the larger total loss.
  25. The correct answer is C (Quota share cedes one fixed percentage for every policy while surplus share varies by policy size): a quota share treaty applies one identical cession percentage across every policy the treaty covers, regardless of the individual policy's size. A surplus share treaty instead sets a cession percentage on each policy separately, based on how that policy's sum insured compares to the insurer's chosen retention line. Larger policies relative to the retention line therefore cede a higher percentage under surplus share than smaller ones do. This flexibility lets an insurer keep a more consistent net retained amount across policies of varying size.
  26. The correct answer is A (Proportional reinsurance shares premium and losses proportionally while non-proportional pays only above an attachment point): quota share and surplus share are proportional arrangements, ceding a set share of both premium and losses on every policy covered. Excess of loss and aggregate stop-loss are non-proportional arrangements, responding only once losses exceed a stated attachment point, with the reinsurer typically receiving a separate negotiated premium rather than a fixed share of the original premium. This distinction affects how each type of treaty allocates volatility between the insurer and the reinsurer. Non-proportional treaties concentrate protection on the tail of the loss distribution rather than spreading it evenly.
  27. The correct answer is D (Retention is the amount of loss the ceding insurer keeps for its own account before reinsurance recoveries apply): retention describes the portion of loss exposure the ceding insurer chooses to bear itself rather than transferring to a reinsurer. In excess of loss treaties this is the attachment point, while in surplus share treaties it is expressed as the retention line against each policy's sum insured. Setting the retention level determines how much volatility the insurer keeps versus transfers, and directly affects the reinsurance premium charged. A higher retention generally lowers the cost of reinsurance because the reinsurer bears less frequent exposure.
  28. The correct answer is B (It protects the insurer against an unexpectedly high total of losses across many claims during a period): aggregate stop-loss reinsurance is designed to respond to the combined effect of many claims over a defined period, such as a full policy year, rather than to any single large loss. This protects the insurer against a poor overall loss year even when no individual claim is unusually severe. It is a common complement to per-occurrence excess of loss coverage, which instead targets individual large claims. Aggregate stop-loss therefore addresses frequency-driven volatility rather than severity-driven volatility.
  29. The correct answer is E (Quota share reinsurance, which cedes a fixed percentage of every premium and loss): quota share reinsurance is a proportional treaty because it applies one fixed cession percentage to both the premium and the losses of every policy it covers. Excess of loss, catastrophe excess of loss, per-risk excess of loss, and aggregate stop-loss are all non-proportional structures, since each responds only once losses cross a defined attachment point rather than sharing a fixed proportion of every loss. This distinction matters for how volatility and premium are allocated between the ceding insurer and the reinsurer. Proportional treaties typically share expenses and profit commissions differently than non-proportional treaties do.
  30. The correct answer is C (Excess of loss responds per individual occurrence while aggregate stop-loss responds to total losses over a period): an excess of loss treaty is triggered separately by each individual claim once that claim exceeds the stated retention, making it a per-occurrence structure. An aggregate stop-loss treaty instead accumulates all losses over a defined period, such as a policy year, and is triggered only once that combined total exceeds the aggregate retention. Both are non-proportional structures, but they respond to different measures of loss experience. This is why insurers often purchase both types of coverage together to address severity and frequency risk separately.
  31. The correct answer is D (The insurer pays $45,000, the coinsurance factor applied to the covered loss above the deductible): the maximum covered loss of $52,500 first caps the $60,000 loss, then the $2,500 deductible is subtracted, leaving $50,000, and finally the 0.90 coinsurance factor is applied to give $45,000. Applying coinsurance before subtracting the deductible, as in one distractor, produces an overstated and incorrect result. All three adjustments, the covered loss cap, the deductible, and the coinsurance factor, must be applied in that order. The insured retains the deductible plus 10% of the remaining covered loss.
  32. The correct answer is A (The loss elimination ratio equals sixteen percent, dividing the limited expected value by total expected loss): dividing the $2,400 expected loss limited to the deductible by the $15,000 overall expected loss gives 0.16, or sixteen percent. This means the $3,000 deductible eliminates roughly sixteen percent of the insurer's total expected loss cost on this policy. The remaining eighty four percent of expected loss cost still falls on the insurer above the deductible. A larger deductible on the same distribution would produce a higher loss elimination ratio than this one.
  33. The correct answer is C (It represents the fraction of expected loss cost eliminated by imposing the deductible): the loss elimination ratio measures how much of the insurer's total expected loss cost is shifted to the insured by imposing a given deductible amount. It is computed as the expected loss limited to the deductible divided by the overall expected loss with no deductible. This ratio is commonly used to compare the impact of different candidate deductible amounts on the same loss distribution. It says nothing directly about reinsurance shares, profit margins, or claim probabilities.
  34. The correct answer is B (The insurer pays $3,000, the full loss, since it exceeds the franchise threshold): once a loss exceeds the stated franchise amount, the insurer must pay the entire loss with no reduction. Here the $3,000 loss exceeds the $2,500 franchise threshold, so the full amount becomes payable. If this had instead been an ordinary deductible of $2,500, the insurer would have paid only $500. This comparison highlights why a franchise deductible can be more generous to the insured than an ordinary deductible for losses just above the threshold.
  35. The correct answer is E (The insurer retains $60,000 after the ceded amount is limited by the per risk cap): the uncapped 50% quota share cession on the $100,000 loss would be $50,000, but the treaty's $40,000 per-risk cap restricts the reinsurer's payment to $40,000. The insurer must then absorb the remaining amount above what the reinsurer pays, leaving the insurer with $100,000 minus $40,000 equals $60,000 retained. This is a common feature added to quota share treaties to limit the reinsurer's exposure on unusually large individual risks. Without the cap, the insurer would have retained only $50,000 instead.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The reinsurer pays $40,000, eighty percent of the loss under this surplus share cession): the cession fraction equals the sum insured minus the retention line, divided by the sum insured, giving $100,000 divided by $125,000, which equals 80%. Applying that 80% fraction to the $50,000 loss gives $40,000 payable by the reinsurer. The insurer retains the remaining 20%, or $10,000, on this loss. This fraction is set once the policy's sum insured is known and applies consistently to every loss on that policy for the treaty year.
  2. The correct answer is B (The insurer retains $50,000, the amount below the first layer's attachment point): the insurer keeps only the first $50,000 of the loss, below the attachment point of the first reinsurance layer. The first layer covers the next $100,000, from $50,000 to $150,000, and is fully exhausted since the loss reaches $280,000. The second layer covers up to $200,000 excess of $150,000, so it pays the remaining $130,000 of the loss, well within its capacity. Adding the amounts ceded to both layers, $100,000 plus $130,000 equals $230,000 ceded, leaving exactly $50,000 retained by the insurer.
  3. The correct answer is A (A deductible reduces small claims and moral hazard by requiring the insured to share early losses): requiring the insured to bear the first portion of every loss discourages filing very small claims, which reduces administrative expense for the insurer. It also gives the insured a financial stake in preventing losses, which reduces moral hazard compared with a policy that pays every dollar of loss. Deductibles do not guarantee profit, transfer risk to a reinsurer, or increase catastrophic exposure, and they never remove the need for careful underwriting. Because of these effects, a deductible typically also lowers the premium charged for otherwise identical coverage.
  4. The correct answer is C (A policy limit caps the insurer's maximum payment obligation on a covered loss): a policy limit sets the maximum dollar amount the insurer will pay for a covered loss or set of losses, regardless of how large the actual loss turns out to be. This gives the insurer a known ceiling on its exposure, which is important for pricing and for managing capital requirements. Policy limits do not remove the deductible, guarantee full reimbursement, or automatically involve a reinsurer. The insured typically bears any loss amount above the stated policy limit.
  5. The correct answer is E (Coinsurance clauses share loss costs with the insured above the deductible to reduce moral hazard): a coinsurance factor requires the insured to continue bearing a fixed percentage of losses even after the deductible has been satisfied, rather than shifting the full remaining loss to the insurer. This ongoing cost sharing gives the insured a ongoing incentive to control losses even on claims well above the deductible. It does not eliminate the insurer's obligation entirely, and it has no direct connection to reinsurance treaty eligibility. Coinsurance factors are typically applied as a fixed percentage, such as 75% or 80%, to the loss above the deductible.
  6. The correct answer is B (The reinsurer pays $130,000, sixty five percent of the loss under this quota share treaty): since the ceding insurer retains 35% of every loss, the reinsurer must assume the remaining 65%. Applying 65% to the $200,000 loss gives $130,000 payable by the reinsurer. The distractor of $70,000 mistakenly applies the retained 35% share as if it were the ceded share. Quota share cessions apply this same fixed percentage split to every loss and every premium dollar under the treaty.
  7. The correct answer is D (The insurer retains $18,000, twenty percent of the loss under this surplus share treaty): the cession fraction equals the sum insured minus the retention line, divided by the sum insured, giving $320,000 divided by $400,000 equals 80%, so the insurer's retained fraction is the remaining 20%. Applying 20% to the $90,000 loss gives $18,000 retained by the insurer, with the remaining $72,000 ceded to the reinsurer. The retention line amount of $80,000 itself is not directly subtracted from the loss under surplus share, unlike an excess of loss treaty. This fixed retained fraction applies to every loss on this policy for the treaty period.
  8. The correct answer is A (The reinsurer pays $250,000, the aggregate excess capped at the stated layer limit): total aggregate losses of $2,400,000 minus the $2,100,000 retention give an excess of $300,000, but the treaty's stated layer limit caps the reinsurer's payment at $250,000. The ceding insurer therefore retains the $2,100,000 retention plus the remaining $50,000 above the layer limit, for total retained losses of $2,150,000. Setting the aggregate retention as a percentage of earned premium is a common way to size aggregate stop-loss treaties so protection scales with the insurer's book of business. The $300,000 figure without the cap applied overstates what the reinsurer actually owes.
  9. The correct answer is E (Non-proportional treaties let the insurer retain predictable losses while transferring volatile catastrophic tail risk): a non-proportional structure such as catastrophe excess of loss allows the insurer to keep the frequent, predictable, smaller losses for its own account while transferring only the volatile tail risk from major catastrophic events. This is generally more capital-efficient than a proportional treaty, which cedes and retains a fixed share of every loss regardless of its size. Proportional treaties also require sharing a proportional share of premium on every policy, which is unnecessary if the insurer only wants tail protection. Non-proportional treaties therefore concentrate reinsurance capacity where it is most valuable to the insurer's overall risk profile.
  10. The correct answer is C (A franchise deductible pays the full loss once the threshold is exceeded, while an ordinary deductible still subtracts the deductible): once a loss surpasses the stated threshold, a franchise deductible requires the insurer to pay the entire loss with no reduction, while an ordinary deductible continues to subtract the deductible amount from every loss regardless of size. For a loss only slightly above the threshold, this makes a franchise deductible more generous to the insured than an equivalent ordinary deductible. For losses far above the threshold, the dollar difference between the two structures becomes proportionally smaller. Insurers and reinsurers must account for this behavioral difference when pricing coverage that uses a franchise deductible.
  11. The correct answer is B (7.2): For a Poisson distribution, both the mean and the variance equal the parameter lambda, so a variance of 7.2 requires lambda equal to 7.2. This equal mean-variance property is a defining feature of the Poisson model. No other transformation of lambda is needed since mean and variance coincide exactly. This property distinguishes Poisson from negative binomial, where variance always exceeds the mean.
  12. The correct answer is D (3.36): For a binomial distribution with parameters m and q, the variance equals m times q times (1 minus q), so the variance here is 25 times 0.16 times 0.84, which equals 3.36. The mean of 4.00 is a different quantity computed as m times q. Binomial variance is always strictly less than its mean whenever q is between 0 and 1. This mean-exceeds-variance relationship is the opposite of the negative binomial case.
  13. The correct answer is A (22.50): The negative binomial variance formula is r times beta times the quantity one plus beta, so the variance equals 6 times 1.5 times 2.5, which equals 22.50. The mean of the same distribution is only r times beta, or 9.00, a smaller value. Because the multiplier one plus beta always exceeds one, negative binomial variance always exceeds its own mean. This overdispersion is why the negative binomial is preferred when observed claim variance is higher than the mean.
  14. The correct answer is E (8.16): The geometric distribution is the negative binomial with r set to one, so its variance formula reduces to beta times the quantity one plus beta, giving 2.4 times 3.4, which equals 8.16. The mean of a geometric distribution is simply beta, or 2.40, a much smaller number. Setting r equal to one in the general negative binomial variance formula confirms this reduction directly. The geometric distribution therefore always has variance exceeding its mean, just like every negative binomial.
  15. The correct answer is C (Poisson): Within the (a,b,0) class, the Poisson distribution is uniquely characterized by a equal to zero and b equal to the Poisson parameter lambda, so here lambda equals 4.5. Binomial requires a negative, and negative binomial and geometric both require a strictly between zero and one. Zero-truncated Poisson shares the same a and b values but is a conditional distribution excluding zero, not this case. Since a is exactly zero here, the ordinary Poisson is the only match.
  16. The correct answer is A (a=-0.429, b=5.571): For the binomial distribution, a equals negative q over one minus q, giving negative 0.3 over 0.7, or approximately negative 0.429. The parameter b equals m plus one times q over one minus q, which is 13 times 0.3 over 0.7, or approximately 5.571. These signed values are what distinguish binomial from the other (a,b,0) members, since a is negative only for binomial. Poisson always has a equal to zero, ruling out the remaining incorrect options.
  17. The correct answer is D (a=0.444, b=0.889): For the negative binomial distribution, a equals beta over one plus beta, so 0.8 over 1.8 gives approximately 0.444. The parameter b equals r minus one times beta over one plus beta, which is 2 times 0.8 over 1.8, or approximately 0.889. Both a and b depend on r and beta in this specific combined way for negative binomial. Only geometric, where r equals one, would force b down to exactly zero instead.
  18. The correct answer is B (a=0.545, b=0): The geometric distribution is the r equals one case of negative binomial, so a equals beta over one plus beta, giving 1.2 over 2.2, or approximately 0.545, while b always reduces to zero. This zero value of b is what makes the geometric distribution recursion depend only on the constant a, not on the claim count k. No other (a,b,0) member has b permanently fixed at zero this way. Recognizing b equal to zero is the fastest way to identify a geometric fit.
  19. The correct answer is E (350): For an exponential distribution, the variance equals theta squared, so the variance is 350 squared, which is 122500, and the standard deviation is the square root of that value, exactly 350 again. This equality between the mean and the standard deviation is a distinctive feature unique to the exponential distribution among common severity models. Gamma and Weibull distributions generally do not share this exact equality unless their shape parameters take special values. Recognizing this shortcut avoids needlessly computing the full variance formula.
  20. The correct answer is C (19200): For the gamma distribution, variance equals alpha times theta squared, so the variance here is 3 times 80 squared, which is 3 times 6400, giving 19200. The mean of this gamma distribution is only alpha times theta, or 240, a much smaller quantity. Increasing the shape parameter alpha raises both the mean and variance proportionally, while theta affects variance quadratically. Confusing the mean formula with the variance formula is the most common error on this type of question.
  21. The correct answer is D (1200): The Pareto mean formula is theta divided by the quantity alpha minus one, which here is 600 divided by 0.5, giving 1200. This mean formula only produces a finite, meaningful value when alpha exceeds one, and here alpha equals 1.5 satisfies that requirement. If alpha were less than or equal to one, the mean would not exist at all. The larger the theta parameter, holding alpha fixed, the heavier the average claim severity becomes.
  22. The correct answer is A (The variance does not exist because alpha must exceed 2): The Pareto distribution requires alpha strictly greater than two for its second moment, and hence its variance, to be finite. Since alpha equals 1.8 here, which is below that threshold, the variance is infinite and cannot be reported as a finite number. The mean alone can still exist because it only requires alpha greater than one, a weaker condition already satisfied. This distinction between the conditions for finite mean and finite variance is a frequently tested Pareto property.
  23. The correct answer is C (20000): The mean is theta over alpha minus one, giving 300 over 3, or 100, and the second moment is 2 theta squared over the product of alpha minus one and alpha minus two, giving 2 times 90000 over 6, which is 30000. Subtracting the squared mean of 10000 from the second moment of 30000 leaves a variance of 20000. Both alpha minus one and alpha minus two must be positive for these formulas to apply, and alpha equal to 4 satisfies both. Skipping the second-moment step and using only the mean would give an incorrect variance.
  24. The correct answer is B (178): The lognormal mean formula requires exponentiating mu plus sigma squared divided by two, so the exponent is 5 plus 0.18, or 5.18, and e raised to 5.18 is approximately 178. This mean is always larger than e raised to mu alone, since the sigma-squared term always adds a positive amount to the exponent. A common error is exponentiating only mu and ignoring the variance adjustment entirely. The larger the volatility parameter sigma, the more the mean is inflated above e to the mu.
  25. The correct answer is E (686): The variance formula first needs exp(sigma-squared) minus one, which is exp(0.64) minus one, or approximately 0.896, and exp(2mu+sigma-squared), which is exp(6.64), or approximately 765.10. Multiplying these two pieces together gives 0.896 times 765.10, approximately 686. A common mistake is computing exp(2mu) instead of exp(2mu+sigma-squared), which would produce 403 instead. The extra sigma-squared term in both factors is what makes lognormal variance grow so quickly as volatility increases.
  26. The correct answer is A (35.45): The Weibull mean formula multiplies theta by the gamma function evaluated at one plus one over tau, so with tau equal to 2 this is Gamma of 1.5, given as 0.8862. Multiplying 40 by 0.8862 gives approximately 35.45. Because Gamma of 1.5 is less than one, the Weibull mean here is actually smaller than the scale parameter theta itself. When tau equals one, the Weibull reduces to the exponential and the gamma factor becomes exactly one, restoring mean equal to theta.
  27. The correct answer is C (Poisson with mean 7.0): Thinning a Poisson process, where each loss independently exceeds the deductible with probability v, produces another Poisson process with parameter lambda times v, so the new mean is 20 times 0.35, or 7.0. This closure property under thinning is unique to the Poisson distribution among common frequency models. The deductible reduces the number of paying claims but never changes the underlying frequency family from Poisson. Reporting a value of 20.0 would incorrectly ignore the effect of the deductible on the observed claim count.
  28. The correct answer is D (12.00): For a binomial ground-up frequency, imposing a deductible with exceedance probability v produces a new binomial distribution with the same m but a modified probability qv, so the new probability is 0.5 times 0.6, or 0.3. The mean number of paying claims is then m times the new probability, which is 40 times 0.3, or 12.00. Only the success probability changes under this thinning, while the number of exposure units m stays fixed. Using the original probability of 0.5 without adjustment would overstate the mean at 20.00.
  29. The correct answer is B (5.00): Thinning a negative binomial frequency by a deductible-exceedance probability v keeps r fixed and multiplies beta by v, so beta becomes 4 times 0.25, or 1. The mean of the resulting negative binomial for paying claims is r times the new beta, which is 5 times 1, or 5.00. The original mean of the ground-up distribution was r times beta, or 20.00, considerably larger before the deductible was applied. This same thinning pattern, keeping r fixed and scaling beta, is why negative binomial is closed under deductible thinning just like Poisson and binomial.
  30. The correct answer is E (800): The exponential distribution has the memoryless property, meaning the distribution of the excess over any threshold, given that the threshold was exceeded, is exactly the same exponential distribution as the original. Therefore the expected payment per payment after the deductible remains theta, unchanged at 800. This is a special property of the exponential distribution and does not hold for most other severity distributions such as gamma or lognormal. Subtracting the deductible from the mean, giving 550, would be a memoryless-property error since the exponential excess is not simply the original mean minus the deductible.
  31. The correct answer is C (300): The Pareto distribution has the property that the excess over a deductible, conditional on exceeding it, is again Pareto with the same alpha but a shifted scale parameter equal to theta plus the deductible, so the new theta is 400 plus 200, or 600. The mean of this shifted Pareto is the new theta divided by alpha minus one, which is 600 divided by 2, or 300. This differs from the exponential case because Pareto is not memoryless, so the excess mean is not simply the original theta. Using the un-shifted theta of 400 in the mean formula would incorrectly give 200 instead of the correct 300.
  32. The correct answer is E (86.47): The limited expected value formula for the exponential distribution is theta times one minus e to the negative u over theta, so with u equal to 200 and theta equal to 100 this is 100 times one minus e to the negative 2, and e to the negative 2 is approximately 0.1353. Multiplying gives 100 times 0.8647, or 86.47. This value is always smaller than the unlimited mean of theta, since capping large claims at the policy limit reduces the expected payment. As the policy limit u grows very large, the limited expected value approaches the full unlimited mean of 100.
  33. The correct answer is A (44.44): The Pareto limited expected value formula multiplies theta over alpha minus one, here 100 over 2, or 50, by one minus theta over theta plus u raised to the power alpha minus one. That ratio is 100 over 300, or one third, squared to give one ninth, so one minus one ninth is eight ninths, or approximately 0.8889. Multiplying 50 by 0.8889 gives approximately 44.44. The unlimited Pareto mean of 50 would only apply if there were no policy limit at all. As u grows without bound, this limited expected value gradually approaches the full mean of 50.
  34. The correct answer is D (E[N] times E[X]): In the collective risk model, the expected aggregate loss is always the product of the expected claim count and the expected severity per claim, following directly from the law of total expectation applied to a random sum. Adding the two expectations instead of multiplying them, as in option A, is a common but incorrect shortcut. This multiplicative relationship holds regardless of which specific frequency or severity distributions are chosen, as long as N and the X_i are independent. It is the foundation formula used before layering in the more complex variance formula for aggregate losses.
  35. The correct answer is B (E[N]*Var(X) + Var(N)*(E[X])^2): The aggregate loss variance formula has two distinct pieces, the expected claim count times the severity variance, plus the frequency variance times the squared expected severity, reflecting variability from both random claim counts and random severities. Multiplying the two variances together, as in option A, ignores the correct decomposition entirely and produces a much smaller and incorrect result. This formula reduces to a simpler expression when N follows specific distributions, such as the compound Poisson case where it simplifies to lambda times the second moment of X. Both terms are always needed unless one of the two sources of randomness, frequency or severity, is degenerate.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (1200): The expected aggregate loss for the collective risk model is E[N] times E[X], so 8 times 150 gives 1200 for the expected annual aggregate loss. The value 9600 would result from squaring or otherwise misapplying the severity mean, which is a variance-style calculation, not the correct mean formula. The individual frequency mean of 8 and severity mean of 150 are only intermediate pieces, not the final aggregate answer by themselves. This straightforward product is the standard first step before computing the aggregate variance.
  2. The correct answer is A (291600): For a compound Poisson model, the aggregate variance simplifies to lambda times the second moment of the severity, E[X-squared]. Here gamma has variance 16200 and mean 180, so E[X-squared] equals 16200 plus 180 squared, which is 16200 plus 32400, giving 48600. Multiplying by lambda equal to 6 gives 291600. Using only the severity variance of 16200 without adding the squared mean would omit the second-moment adjustment that the compound Poisson formula requires.
  3. The correct answer is C (38880): With E[N] equal to 6 and Var(N) equal to 30 times 0.2 times 0.8, or 4.8, and the exponential severity having both variance and squared mean equal to 3600, the aggregate variance is 6 times 3600 plus 4.8 times 3600. That is 21600 plus 17280, which sums to 38880. Reporting only the first term, 21600, would ignore the additional variability contributed by the random frequency itself. Both pieces of the two-term formula are essential whenever the frequency distribution has variance different from its mean, as binomial does.
  4. The correct answer is D (480000): The negative binomial frequency has E[N] equal to 8 and Var(N) equal to 4 times 2 times 3, or 24, while the Pareto severity has mean 100 and variance 30000 after subtracting the squared mean from the second moment of 40000. The aggregate variance is 8 times 30000 plus 24 times 10000, which is 240000 plus 240000, totaling 480000. It is notable that both terms happen to contribute equally in this particular example, though that is a coincidence of these specific parameters. Omitting either term, such as reporting only 240000, would understate the true aggregate variance by half.
  5. The correct answer is B (a=0, b=3.5): In the Panjer recursion, the (a,b,0) class parameters used for a Poisson frequency are always a equal to zero and b equal to the Poisson parameter lambda, so here a is zero and b is 3.5. Setting a equal to lambda instead, as in option A, reverses the roles of the two parameters and gives an incorrect recursion. These same a and b values feed directly into the Panjer formula f_S(s), which builds up the aggregate loss distribution recursively from f_S(0) upward. Getting a and b correct is essential before any numeric recursion can be computed accurately.
  6. The correct answer is E (0.1624): The Panjer recursion for a compound Poisson model with a equal to zero and b equal to lambda equal to 2 gives f_S(1) equal to b times f_X(1) times f_S(0), which is 2 times 0.6 times 0.1353, or approximately 0.1624. The starting value f_S(0) equal to 0.1353 comes from e to the negative lambda, since the severity has no mass at zero. Only the y equal to 1 term contributes to this particular sum, since f_S(1) only involves f_S(1-1) equal to f_S(0). This recursive computation must proceed strictly in order from f_S(0) upward to reach any later value of s.
  7. The correct answer is B (0.2057): The Panjer recursion for f_S(2) sums two terms, one using y equal to 1 with weight b times y over s equal to 2 times 1 over 2, or 1, giving 1 times 0.6 times f_S(1), and one using y equal to 2 with weight 2 times 2 over 2, or 2, giving 2 times 0.4 times f_S(0). These evaluate to approximately 0.0974 and 0.1082, which sum to approximately 0.2057. Both terms must be included because the sum in the recursion runs from y equal to 1 all the way up to s. Stopping after only the first term would understate f_S(2) by nearly half.
  8. The correct answer is D (It is compound Poisson with lambda=11 and the same severity X): Independent compound Poisson random variables sharing the same severity distribution combine additively in their Poisson parameters, so the sum has lambda equal to 4 plus 7, or 11, with the identical severity distribution X unchanged. This additive closure property does not require the two lambda values to be equal beforehand. Multiplying the two lambda parameters instead of adding them, giving 28, is a common arithmetic error on this type of question. This property is extremely useful for combining separate lines of business into a single aggregate loss model.
  9. The correct answer is A (409.35): The expected payment per loss, which includes zero payments for losses below the deductible, equals the unlimited mean minus the limited expected value at the deductible, so first E[X and d] is computed as 500 times one minus e to the negative 0.2, giving approximately 90.65. Subtracting this from the full mean of 500 gives approximately 409.35 as the expected payment per loss. This quantity of 409.35 differs from the expected payment per payment, which by the exponential memoryless property would simply equal 500. The distinction between per-loss and per-payment expectations is a critical exam concept whenever a deductible is present.
  10. The correct answer is C (Per loss equals per payment multiplied by the probability of exceeding the deductible): Because the per-loss average includes all losses, including the ones below the deductible that pay nothing, it always equals the per-payment average scaled down by the probability that a loss actually exceeds the deductible. Since that probability is always between zero and one, the per-loss expectation is always smaller than or equal to the per-payment expectation, which is the opposite relationship stated in option A. Confusing these two averages is one of the most common sources of error when deductibles are involved in exam calculations. The per-payment average conditions only on the subset of losses that generate a nonzero payment.
  11. The correct answer is B (0.4557): The loss elimination ratio measures the fraction of expected total losses removed by imposing the deductible, computed here as E[X and d] divided by E[X]. The ratio theta over theta plus d is 180 over 270, or two thirds, and raising that to the power alpha minus one, or 1.5, gives approximately 0.5443, so one minus that value is approximately 0.4557. Since E[X and d] and E[X] share the identical theta over alpha-minus-one factor of 120, that factor cancels out of the ratio entirely, leaving exactly this one minus power-of-two-thirds expression. A deductible of 90 therefore eliminates roughly 45.6 percent of the total expected losses under this Pareto severity model.
  12. The correct answer is E (alpha=3, theta=270): Uniform inflation applied to a Pareto distribution multiplies the scale parameter theta by the inflation factor while leaving the shape parameter alpha completely unchanged, so the new theta is 250 times 1.08, or 270, with alpha remaining at 3. This is a key property that allows actuaries to trend loss distributions forward in time without needing to refit the entire distribution. Changing alpha in response to inflation, as several of the other options do, would be a fundamental misunderstanding of how the Pareto distribution transforms under scalar multiplication. Only the scale parameter theta responds to a multiplicative shift like inflation.
  13. The correct answer is A (The loss elimination ratio decreases because the fixed deductible becomes relatively smaller): For an exponential severity, the loss elimination ratio equals one minus e to the negative d over theta, and as inflation raises theta while the deductible d stays fixed in dollar terms, the ratio d over theta shrinks. A smaller ratio d over theta pushes the elimination ratio downward, meaning the fixed deductible eliminates a smaller proportion of losses than it did before inflation. This erosion effect is a well known reason insurers periodically raise deductibles to keep pace with inflation. Since exponential severity has no alpha parameter, option E does not apply to this distribution.
  14. The correct answer is D (Negative binomial, because variance always exceeds the mean): The negative binomial distribution has variance equal to r times beta times one plus beta, which is always strictly greater than its mean of r times beta whenever beta is positive, making it the natural choice for overdispersed count data. Poisson forces variance to exactly equal the mean, so it cannot capture data where variance is noticeably larger. Binomial actually has variance strictly below its mean, the opposite pattern from overdispersion. This is a major practical reason why negative binomial is frequently preferred over Poisson when fitting real claim count data.
  15. The correct answer is C (Its overdispersion relative to Poisson): When the Poisson parameter lambda varies randomly across policyholders according to a gamma distribution, the resulting mixed distribution is negative binomial, and this extra layer of parameter uncertainty is exactly what inflates the variance above the mean, producing overdispersion. If lambda were instead fixed and identical for every policyholder, the mixture would collapse back to an ordinary Poisson with equal mean and variance. Neither the exponential's memoryless property nor a symmetric or bounded shape has anything to do with this Poisson-gamma mixing construction. This mixture interpretation is a standard justification for why negative binomial is a natural model for heterogeneous risk populations.
  16. The correct answer is A (c-squared times Var(N)): When severity is degenerate at a fixed constant c, the aggregate loss S reduces to simply c times N, a constant multiple of the frequency random variable, so its variance is c squared times the variance of N by the standard scaling property of variance. This is a special case of the general two-term aggregate variance formula, where the severity variance term vanishes to zero since a constant has no variance, leaving only Var(N) times c squared, the squared mean of the degenerate severity. Option E incorrectly uses E[N] rather than Var(N), which would actually compute the mean of S, not its variance. This degenerate severity case is a useful sanity check for verifying that the general aggregate variance formula collapses correctly.
  17. The correct answer is E (The stop-loss premium): The expected value of the positive excess of the aggregate loss S over a retention level d is formally known as the stop-loss premium, since it represents the expected cost transferred to a reinsurer under a stop-loss or excess-of-loss aggregate reinsurance arrangement. This concept applies to the entire aggregate loss S, in contrast to the limited expected value E[X and d], which instead caps an individual severity X rather than the aggregate. The loss elimination ratio is a related but distinct concept measuring the fraction of expected loss removed by an individual policy deductible. Computing the stop-loss premium typically requires knowing or approximating the full distribution of S, often through Panjer recursion or a normal or translated gamma approximation.
  18. The correct answer is B (The negative binomial distribution, since its variance exceeds its mean): With both distributions sharing the same mean of 10, the negative binomial variance of r times beta times one plus beta, here 2 times 5 times 6, or 60, is much larger than the Poisson variance, which always equals its mean of 10. A larger variance at the same mean directly translates into a larger coefficient of variation, since coefficient of variation is the standard deviation divided by the mean. This comparison illustrates concretely why negative binomial is described as overdispersed relative to Poisson even when their central tendencies match exactly. No severity parameter theta is needed for this comparison since it concerns only the frequency distributions.
  19. The correct answer is C (S is generally right-skewed even though the severity is symmetric): Compound Poisson aggregate losses are typically right-skewed because the underlying Poisson claim count itself is right-skewed, especially at low to moderate values of lambda, and this asymmetry carries through to the aggregate sum even when the severity distribution itself is perfectly symmetric. As lambda grows very large, the aggregate distribution does approach a more symmetric, approximately normal shape by the central limit theorem, but for typical moderate lambda values in practice, right skewness remains. This is why symmetric severity does not guarantee a symmetric aggregate distribution, one of the more counterintuitive properties in aggregate loss modeling. The skewness of the aggregate genuinely does depend on lambda, contradicting option E.
  20. The correct answer is D (alpha=2, theta=120): Dividing the gamma variance formula by the gamma mean formula gives variance over mean equal to theta, so theta equals 28800 divided by 240, which is 120. Substituting this theta back into the mean formula gives alpha equal to the mean divided by theta, which is 240 divided by 120, or 2. This method of moments procedure, dividing variance by mean to isolate theta first, is the standard technique for fitting any two-parameter distribution where mean and variance both depend on the same two parameters. Reversing which formula to divide by which, or mismatching alpha and theta, is the most common error when applying this method under time pressure.
  21. The correct answer is E (4672.81): The probability a loss exceeds the deductible is e to the negative 0.25, or approximately 0.7788, so the reduced claim frequency after the deductible is 15 times 0.7788, or approximately 11.682 paying claims per year, still Poisson by the thinning property. Because the exponential distribution is memoryless, the expected payment per payment remains theta, unchanged at 400. Multiplying the reduced frequency of 11.682 by the unchanged severity of 400 gives approximately 4672.81 as the mean aggregate annual payment after the deductible. Ignoring the frequency reduction and using the full lambda of 15 would incorrectly inflate this answer to 6000.00.
  22. The correct answer is C (3738240): Since the post-deductible frequency remains Poisson with the reduced mean of approximately 11.682, the compound Poisson shortcut applies directly, requiring the second moment of the exponential severity, which is two times theta squared, or two times 160000, giving 320000. Multiplying the reduced lambda of 11.682 by 320000 gives approximately 3738240 for the variance of the post-deductible aggregate loss. This shortcut formula, lambda times E[X-squared], only works because the frequency remains exactly Poisson after thinning, a property that would not hold as simply for a non-Poisson base frequency. Using the original undiminished lambda of 15 instead of the reduced 11.682 would overstate this variance considerably.
  23. The correct answer is A (The zero-truncated mean is larger than the original Poisson mean): Removing the zero outcome and rescaling the remaining probabilities to sum to one necessarily shifts probability mass toward higher claim counts relative to the original distribution, which always raises the mean above the untruncated Poisson mean of lambda. This effect makes intuitive sense because a zero-truncated distribution is used specifically to model situations where at least one claim is known to have occurred, so very low counts become relatively more concentrated but the complete absence of zero pulls the average upward. The mean can never stay exactly the same after this kind of truncation, ruling out option C. Zero-truncated and zero-modified distributions are commonly used in reporting delay and IBNR count modeling contexts.
  24. The correct answer is B (N must be independent of the severities, and the severities must be independent and identically distributed): Both the mean and variance formulas for the collective risk model are derived under the assumption that the claim count N is independent of every individual severity amount, and that the severities themselves are independent and identically distributed random variables. Without independence between N and the severities, the simple multiplicative mean formula and the additive two-term variance formula would no longer hold in their standard forms. There is no requirement that N follow any particular named distribution such as Poisson, since the general formulas apply to any frequency distribution meeting the independence condition. Allowing severities to depend on N, as suggested in option D, would violate the identical-distribution assumption central to the standard collective risk model.
  25. The correct answer is D (Thinning and left-truncation and shifting, respectively): The frequency effect of a deductible, reducing the observed count of paying claims, is called thinning, since each ground-up loss independently either does or does not clear the deductible threshold. The severity effect is called left-truncation and shifting, since losses below the deductible are entirely excluded, and the remaining payment amounts are shifted downward by the deductible before being modeled. Right-censoring is instead the correct term for the effect of a policy limit, which caps large losses rather than excluding small ones, so it does not belong in this deductible-focused pairing. Keeping these three distinct terms, thinning, left-truncation and shifting, and right-censoring, straight is essential for correctly identifying which formula applies to which type of policy modification.

This estimator is also unbiased since the expected value of the sample mean equals theta.
