  22. The correct answer is D (The sample mean of the observed claim frequency count values.): Method of moments matches the first population moment, the mean lambda, to the first sample moment, the sample mean x-bar. Because the Poisson distribution has a single parameter, only one moment equation is needed, so lambda-hat equals x-bar. This happens to coincide with the maximum likelihood estimator for the Poisson distribution. The range, standard deviation, and zero-proportion statistics are not first-moment quantities.
  23. The correct answer is A (The maximum likelihood estimate of lambda equals three claims per period.): For a Poisson sample the maximum likelihood estimator equals the sample mean, so lambda-hat equals the sum of the ten claim counts divided by ten. Summing the values given in the stem produces a total of thirty claims. Dividing thirty by the sample size of ten gives a maximum likelihood estimate of three. This value uses every observation rather than a single data point.
  24. The correct answer is E (The method-of-moments estimate of alpha equals four for this sample.): Matching moments for the gamma distribution uses the mean equal to alpha times theta and the variance equal to alpha times theta squared. Dividing the variance by the mean gives theta-hat equal to twenty-five divided by ten, or two point five. Substituting back, alpha-hat equals the mean squared divided by the variance, which is one hundred divided by twenty-five, giving four. This two-equation approach is required because the gamma distribution has two unknown parameters.
  25. The correct answer is C (The method-of-moments estimate of theta equals eighteen for this data.): For a uniform distribution on zero to theta, the population mean equals theta divided by two. Setting this expression equal to the sample mean of nine and solving for theta gives theta-hat equal to two times nine, or eighteen. This method-of-moments estimator is unbiased, unlike the maximum likelihood estimator for the same parameter. Multiplying instead of doubling the mean would produce an incorrect result.
  26. The correct answer is B (The maximum likelihood estimate of theta equals eighteen for this sample.): The likelihood function for a uniform distribution on zero to theta is maximized by choosing the smallest theta consistent with every observation, which is the largest observed value. Among the six recorded losses, the largest value is eighteen, so theta-hat equals eighteen. Any theta smaller than eighteen would assign zero likelihood to that observation. This estimator differs from the method-of-moments estimator based on twice the sample mean.
  27. The correct answer is A (The bias of the maximum likelihood estimator equals negative two exactly.): The bias equals the expected value of the estimator minus the true parameter, which is n theta over n plus one minus theta, simplifying to negative theta over n plus one. Substituting n equal to nine and theta equal to twenty gives negative twenty over ten, or negative two. The negative sign confirms that the maximum order statistic systematically underestimates theta on average. This is why the method-of-moments estimator is often preferred despite its larger variance.
  28. The correct answer is D (The maximum likelihood variance estimate equals eight for this sample.): The sample mean of the five values five, seven, nine, eleven, and thirteen equals nine. Summing the squared deviations from nine gives sixteen plus four plus zero plus four plus sixteen, which totals forty. The maximum likelihood estimator divides this sum by n rather than n minus one, giving forty divided by five, or eight. This estimator is known to be biased downward relative to the unbiased sample variance.
  29. The correct answer is E (The bias of the maximum likelihood variance estimator equals negative two.): The bias equals the expected value of the estimator minus the true parameter, which is n minus one over n times sigma squared, minus sigma squared, simplifying to negative sigma squared over n. Substituting sigma squared equal to ten and n equal to five gives negative ten over five, or negative two. This confirms the well known result that the maximum likelihood variance estimator underestimates the true variance on average. The unbiased sample variance divides by n minus one instead to correct this.
  30. The correct answer is C (Mean squared error equals the variance plus the bias squared total.): Mean squared error is defined as the expected squared difference between the estimator and the true parameter, which algebraically decomposes into the variance of the estimator plus the square of its bias. This decomposition shows that an estimator can have a small mean squared error even with nonzero bias, provided its variance is sufficiently small. Squaring the bias rather than the variance is the key detail candidates often reverse. Subtracting the bias squared would understate the true mean squared error.
  31. The correct answer is D (The mean squared error of this estimator equals thirteen exactly.): Mean squared error equals variance plus bias squared, so this estimator has mean squared error equal to four plus three squared. Three squared equals nine, and adding this to the variance of four gives a total of thirteen. Simply adding variance and bias without squaring the bias would incorrectly give seven. Squaring only the variance or ignoring the variance entirely would also produce a wrong result.
  32. The correct answer is B (The mean squared error of this estimator equals three point five.): Bias squared equals negative one squared, which is one, and adding this to the variance of two point five gives three point five. Because this value is smaller than the thirteen computed for the first estimator, this second estimator is preferred under the mean squared error criterion despite having nonzero bias. Mean squared error can never be negative, since it is built from a squared bias term added to a variance. Comparing mean squared error values directly is the standard way actuaries rank competing estimators.
  33. The correct answer is E (The estimator converges in probability to the true parameter as n grows.): Consistency means that as the sample size increases without bound, the probability that the estimator differs from the true parameter by more than any fixed amount approaches zero. A consistent estimator need not be unbiased for finite samples, and its variance typically decreases rather than increases as n grows. There is no finite sample size at which convergence is guaranteed to be exact. Consistency is instead an asymptotic, large-sample property.
  34. The correct answer is A (The reciprocal of the total Fisher information evaluated at theta.): The asymptotic variance of the maximum likelihood estimator is given by the Cramer-Rao lower bound, which equals one divided by the total Fisher information for the sample, evaluated at the true parameter value. As the sample size grows, the total Fisher information grows proportionally, so this variance shrinks toward zero. The sample variance, the bias squared, and the sample range are unrelated quantities that do not describe this asymptotic behavior. This result underlies the construction of large-sample confidence intervals for maximum likelihood estimates.
  35. The correct answer is C (The approximate variance equals theta squared divided by n exactly.): The total Fisher information for a sample of size n equals n times the per-observation information, so it equals n divided by theta squared. The asymptotic variance of the maximum likelihood estimator is the reciprocal of this total information, giving theta squared divided by n. This matches the exact variance of the sample mean for the exponential distribution, since the sample mean is itself the maximum likelihood estimator. Multiplying rather than dividing by n would produce an estimator whose variance grows rather than shrinks with sample size.

The correct answer is D (The interval runs from about 0.24 to 0.36 for p.): The estimated variance of a Bernoulli proportion equals p-hat times one minus p-hat divided by n, giving 0.3 times 0.7 divided by 200, or about 0.00105. Taking the square root gives a standard error of about 0.0324. Multiplying by the critical value 1.96 gives a margin of about 0.0635. Adding and subtracting this margin from 0.3 produces an interval rounding to about 0.24 to 0.36.
  3. The correct answer is B (The interval runs from about 6.43 to 9.57 for theta.): Substituting theta-hat equal to 8 and n equal to 100 into theta squared over n gives an estimated variance of 64 over 100, or 0.64. The square root of this variance is a standard error of 0.8. Multiplying by the critical value 1.96 gives a margin of error of about 1.57. Adding and subtracting this margin from the point estimate of 8 produces an interval running from about 6.43 to 9.57.
  4. The correct answer is A (The maximum likelihood estimate of theta equals fifteen for this data.): For right-censored exponential data, the maximum likelihood estimator equals the total observed exposure time summed over all subjects, divided by the number of subjects whose claim was actually observed rather than censored. Dividing the total exposure time of one hundred twenty by the eight observed claims gives theta-hat equal to fifteen. Censored observations still contribute their exposure time to the numerator even though they contribute no death indicator to the denominator. This formula reduces to the ordinary sample mean when there is no censoring at all.
  5. The correct answer is C (The maximum likelihood estimate of theta equals one hundred fifty total.): The exponential distribution is memoryless, so losses observed above a truncation point behave like a fresh exponential random variable shifted by the truncation point. Subtracting the deductible of one hundred from the observed average of two hundred fifty gives an estimate of the mean excess loss above the deductible, which equals theta-hat. This produces theta-hat equal to one hundred fifty. Ignoring the truncation and using the raw average of two hundred fifty directly would overstate theta and bias the estimate.
  6. The correct answer is A (The probability that the loss falls between the interval boundaries, raised to the count power.): For grouped data, the exact loss amounts within an interval are unknown, so the likelihood contribution for that interval uses the true interval probability, the distribution function at the upper boundary minus the distribution function at the lower boundary. This probability is then raised to a power equal to the number of observations falling in that interval, since each contributes the same factor. Using the density at the midpoint is only an approximation and does not give the exact maximum likelihood estimator. The full grouped-data likelihood multiplies these interval-probability terms across all intervals.
  7. The correct answer is E (Maximum likelihood generally achieves greater asymptotic efficiency than method of moments.): Under standard regularity conditions, the maximum likelihood estimator attains the Cramer-Rao lower bound asymptotically, giving it the smallest possible large-sample variance among consistent estimators. The method-of-moments estimator is often easier to compute but typically has a larger asymptotic variance than the maximum likelihood estimator. Maximum likelihood estimators are not automatically unbiased in finite samples, and the two methods frequently produce different numerical estimates from the same data set. Some maximum likelihood problems also require numerical optimization, so that is not a distinguishing feature either.
  8. The correct answer is D (The sample mean is an unbiased estimator of the population mean mu.): The expected value of the sample mean equals the expected value of a single observation, which is the population mean mu, regardless of the specific distribution being sampled, as long as the mean exists. This unbiasedness property holds for any distribution with finite mean, not only the normal distribution. The variance of the sample mean is finite and equal to the population variance divided by n, not infinite. Because the expectation matches the target parameter exactly, the sample mean carries zero bias.
  9. The correct answer is C (The estimator is biased in finite samples but consistent for the parameter.): An estimator can have nonzero bias for any fixed, finite sample size while still being consistent, provided that both the bias and the variance converge to zero as the sample size grows without bound. The maximum likelihood estimator of the variance of a normal distribution is a classic example, since it is biased downward in finite samples yet remains consistent. Calling the estimator unbiased would contradict the stated nonzero finite-sample bias. Because both bias and variance vanish asymptotically, the mean squared error also converges to zero, ruling out an infinite mean squared error.
  10. The correct answer is B (The method-of-moments estimate of the mean equals ten for this data.): Method of moments matches the first population moment, the mean, to the first sample moment, the sample mean. Dividing the sum of the eight loss values, eighty, by the sample size of eight gives a sample mean of ten. This value serves directly as the method-of-moments estimate of the population mean for the normal distribution. The sample variance of eighteen relates instead to the second moment equation used to estimate the variance parameter.
  11. The correct answer is D (n divided by the sum of the natural logarithms of x over theta.): Setting the derivative of the single-parameter Pareto log-likelihood with respect to alpha equal to zero and solving produces alpha-hat equal to n divided by the sum, over all observations, of the natural logarithm of x sub i divided by theta. This estimator uses every observed loss amount above the known threshold theta. The sample mean and sample variance of the raw loss amounts do not appear in this maximum likelihood solution. Multiplying rather than dividing by the log-sum would produce an estimator that grows rather than shrinks as more extreme losses are observed.
  12. The correct answer is A (The method-of-moments estimate of alpha equals four for this sample.): The ratio r equals the sample variance divided by the sample mean squared, which is two hundred divided by ten squared, or two hundred over one hundred, giving r equal to two. Substituting r equal to two into the formula alpha-hat equal to two r over r minus one gives two times two divided by one, or four. This method-of-moments shape estimate can then be used with the sample mean to back out a scale estimate theta-hat. A ratio r greater than one is required for this formula to produce a valid, finite shape estimate.
  13. The correct answer is C (The tau-th root of the average of x-i raised to the tau power.): Because tau is known, transforming each observation by raising it to the tau power converts the Weibull model into an exponential model with mean theta to the tau power. The maximum likelihood estimator of that exponential mean is the average of the transformed values, x sub i raised to the tau power. Taking the tau-th root of this average recovers theta-hat on the original scale. This transformation trick only works because the shape parameter tau is assumed known rather than estimated simultaneously.
  14. The correct answer is E (The maximum likelihood estimate of theta equals twenty for this data.): For right-censored exponential data, theta-hat equals the total exposure time summed across all subjects divided by the number of subjects with an actually observed claim rather than a censored observation. Dividing the total exposure time of one hundred eighty by the nine observed claims gives theta-hat equal to twenty. The three censored insureds still contribute their full exposure time to the numerator of this formula. Ignoring the censored subjects entirely and dividing only among the nine observed claims by their own total would produce an incorrect, smaller theta.
  15. The correct answer is B (The maximum likelihood estimate of theta equals three hundred exactly.): The memoryless property of the exponential distribution means that losses observed above a truncation point are themselves exponentially distributed with the same mean theta as the untruncated distribution. Subtracting the deductible of two hundred from the observed average of five hundred isolates the mean excess above the deductible, giving theta-hat equal to three hundred. Using the raw average of five hundred without adjusting for truncation would overstate the true mean severity. This shortcut applies specifically because the exponential distribution, unlike most other severity distributions, is memoryless.
  16. The correct answer is C (The standard error decreases toward zero as the sample size grows.): The asymptotic variance of a consistent maximum likelihood estimator is inversely proportional to the total Fisher information, which itself grows proportionally with the sample size n. As n grows without bound, this variance, and therefore the standard error, shrinks toward zero, reflecting increasing precision with more data. The standard error does not increase with n, remain constant, or become undefined, and it does not oscillate under standard regularity conditions. This shrinking standard error is exactly what allows confidence intervals to narrow as more data become available.
  17. The correct answer is A (The estimator is biased in finite samples yet still consistent overall.): Dividing by n instead of n minus one produces an estimator whose expected value equals n minus one over n times the true variance, which is strictly less than the true variance for any finite n, making the estimator biased downward. As the sample size n grows without bound, the multiplier n minus one over n approaches one and the variance of the estimator also shrinks toward zero, so the estimator remains consistent. The estimator is therefore a textbook example of a biased but consistent estimator. Its variance is not fixed across sample sizes, since it depends on n.
  18. The correct answer is B (The method-of-moments estimate of theta equals eight for this sample.): For a single-parameter exponential distribution, the method-of-moments equation sets the population mean theta equal to the sample mean, which is the sum of the observations divided by the sample size. Dividing the sum of sixty-four by the sample size of eight produces theta-hat equal to eight. Because the exponential distribution has only one parameter, this method-of-moments equation is identical to the maximum likelihood equation for this model. Reporting the raw total of sixty-four instead of dividing by the sample size would be a common but incorrect shortcut.
  19. The correct answer is D (The maximum likelihood estimate of theta equals four for this sample.): When the shape parameter alpha is known, the gamma log-likelihood is maximized at the value of theta that satisfies the mean equation, alpha theta equal to the sample mean. Solving for theta gives theta-hat equal to the sample mean divided by alpha, which is twelve divided by three, or four. This result also coincides with the method-of-moments estimator in this special known-shape case. Multiplying the sample mean by alpha instead of dividing would produce an estimate far too large.
  20. The correct answer is E (The half-width of the confidence interval equals three point nine two.): The half-width of an approximate confidence interval based on asymptotic normality equals the critical value multiplied by the standard error of the estimator. Multiplying the critical value one point nine six by the standard error of two gives three point nine two. This half-width is then added to and subtracted from the point estimate to form the full confidence interval. Using the standard error alone, or the critical value alone, without multiplying the two together would not produce the correct half-width.
  21. The correct answer is A (The invariance property of maximum likelihood estimators under smooth transformations.): The invariance property of maximum likelihood estimation states that if theta-hat is the maximum likelihood estimator of theta, then any function g of theta-hat is the maximum likelihood estimator of that same function g applied to theta. This allows an actuary to plug theta-hat directly into a survival function or other transformation without re-deriving a separate likelihood. Unbiasedness and efficiency are not automatically preserved under nonlinear transformations, so those properties do not justify this shortcut. The invariance property applies broadly, not only to strictly linear transformations.
  22. The correct answer is D (The maximum likelihood estimate of this survival probability equals about zero point three seven.): By the invariance property, the maximum likelihood estimate of the survival probability at five is obtained by substituting theta-hat equal to five into the survival function, giving e raised to the negative one power. This quantity equals approximately zero point three seven, since e raised to the negative one is about zero point three six eight. There is no need to separately re-derive a likelihood function for this transformed probability. Plugging in a different, incorrect theta value would produce one of the other listed probabilities instead.
  23. The correct answer is C (The method-of-moments estimator is unbiased while the maximum likelihood estimator is biased downward.): The method-of-moments estimator, twice the sample mean, has expected value equal to twice the population mean, which equals theta exactly, so it is unbiased for any sample size. The maximum likelihood estimator, the largest order statistic, has expected value equal to n theta over n plus one, which is strictly less than theta, so it is biased downward. This tradeoff is a classic textbook example where the less efficient estimator is unbiased while the more efficient one is biased. The two estimators therefore do not share identical bias behavior.
  24. The correct answer is B (theta-hat plus or minus the critical value times the estimated standard error.): The general large-sample confidence interval formula adds and subtracts the appropriate standard normal critical value, multiplied by the estimated standard error of theta-hat, from the point estimate itself. The estimated standard error is typically obtained as the square root of the reciprocal of the estimated total Fisher information. Multiplying theta-hat by the critical value, or using raw sample variance or a parameter count directly, does not correspond to the correct asymptotic formula. This construction relies on the asymptotic normality of the maximum likelihood estimator established under standard regularity conditions.
  25. The correct answer is E (The interval runs from about 45.1 to 54.9 for theta.): The standard error equals the square root of the estimated variance, which is the square root of 6.25, or 2.5. Multiplying this standard error by the critical value 1.96 gives a margin of error of about 4.9. Adding and subtracting this margin from the point estimate of 50 produces an interval running from about 45.1 to 54.9. Using the variance directly instead of its square root would produce an incorrectly wide interval.
  26. The correct answer is D (Thirty-five times the natural log of F at three hundred minus F at one hundred.): The log-likelihood contribution from a grouped interval equals the observation count in that interval multiplied by the natural logarithm of the interval probability, which is the distribution function evaluated at the upper boundary minus the distribution function evaluated at the lower boundary. For the middle interval, this probability is F evaluated at three hundred minus F evaluated at one hundred, and the count is thirty-five claims. Using only the endpoint values or the interval width alone, without the count multiplier, would not give a valid log-likelihood term. Every one of the three intervals contributes an analogous term to the full grouped-data log-likelihood.
  27. The correct answer is B (The maximum likelihood estimate of p equals zero point three zero.): For a Bernoulli model, the maximum likelihood estimator of the success probability p equals the observed proportion of successes in the sample. Dividing the seventy-five policies that generated a claim by the total of two hundred fifty policies gives a proportion of zero point three zero. This proportion is also the sample mean of the zero-one indicator variables recorded for each policy. Any value other than this observed proportion would not maximize the Bernoulli likelihood for this data.
  28. The correct answer is A (The estimator p-hat is unbiased for p at every finite sample size.): The maximum likelihood estimator of a Bernoulli probability is the sample mean of the zero-one indicator observations, and the expected value of a sample mean always equals the true population mean, which here is p. This unbiasedness holds regardless of the specific value of p and for any finite sample size n, not only in large samples. Because bias is exactly zero rather than depending on p, the estimator is not merely unbiased for the special case of p equal to one half. Biased upward or downward would both require a nonzero expected difference between p-hat and p.
  29. The correct answer is C (The mean squared error equals five point five for this estimator.): Mean squared error equals variance plus bias squared, and since this estimator is unbiased its bias equals zero, so the bias squared term contributes nothing to the total. The mean squared error therefore reduces exactly to the variance of the estimator, which is given as five point five. This illustrates that for any unbiased estimator, mean squared error and variance are always numerically identical. Squaring the variance itself, or doubling it, would not reflect the correct decomposition of mean squared error.
  30. The correct answer is E (The approximate variance equals two sigma to the fourth power divided by n.): The asymptotic variance of a maximum likelihood estimator equals the reciprocal of the total Fisher information, and the total information for a sample of size n equals n times the per-observation information of one over two sigma to the fourth power. Taking the reciprocal of n divided by two sigma to the fourth power gives two sigma to the fourth power divided by n. This variance shrinks toward zero as the sample size n grows, consistent with the estimator's consistency. Multiplying rather than dividing by n would produce a variance that grows without bound.
  31. The correct answer is B (The interval runs from about 118.45 to 121.55 for mu.): The exact variance of the sample mean equals sigma squared divided by n, giving 25 divided by 40, or 0.625. Taking the square root gives a standard error of about 0.79. Multiplying by the critical value 1.96 gives a margin of error of about 1.55. Adding and subtracting this margin from the point estimate of 120 produces an interval running from about 118.45 to 121.55.
  32. The correct answer is D (The maximum likelihood estimate of theta equals ninety exactly for this data.): Because the exponential distribution is memoryless, losses recorded above a deductible are themselves exponentially distributed with the same mean as the original, untruncated severity distribution. Subtracting the deductible of fifty from the observed average of one hundred forty isolates the mean excess above the deductible, giving theta-hat equal to ninety. Using the raw average of one hundred forty directly, without subtracting the deductible, would overstate the true mean severity. This memoryless shortcut is specific to the exponential distribution and does not generally apply to other severity models.
  33. The correct answer is A (Sample moments converge in probability to population moments by the law of large numbers.): The law of large numbers guarantees that a sample moment, such as the sample mean, converges in probability to the corresponding true population moment as the sample size grows without bound. Since the method-of-moments estimator is a function of these sample moments, it inherits this convergence and is therefore consistent for the true parameter under mild conditions. There is no fixed sample size, such as ten, at which this convergence becomes exact rather than asymptotic. Method-of-moments estimators do not generally coincide with maximum likelihood estimators except in special cases like the exponential distribution.
  34. The correct answer is C (The law of large numbers applied to the average log-likelihood function.): Under standard regularity conditions, the average log-likelihood function converges in probability, by the law of large numbers, to its expected value, which is uniquely maximized at the true parameter value theta. This convergence implies that the value maximizing the sample log-likelihood, the maximum likelihood estimator, also converges in probability to theta. The central limit theorem instead explains the asymptotic normality of the estimator, a separate large-sample property from consistency. The binomial theorem, the Pythagorean theorem, and the fundamental theorem of calculus are unrelated to this consistency argument.
  35. The correct answer is E (The interval runs from about 64.68 to 85.32 for theta.): Multiplying the critical value 2.58 by the standard error of 4 gives a margin of error of 10.32. Adding and subtracting this margin from the point estimate of 75 produces an interval running from about 64.68 to 85.32. This wider interval, compared to a 95 percent interval with the same standard error, reflects the larger critical value needed for 99 percent confidence. Using the standard error alone as the margin, without multiplying by the critical value, would understate the true width of this interval.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The minimum expected claim count needed so observed experience is trustworthy on its own): Full credibility, under the classical limited fluctuation approach, is the threshold number of expected claims at which the observed experience alone is considered fully reliable, with no blending toward a prior estimate needed. Below that threshold, partial credibility is assigned instead. The other options describe unrelated insurance or reinsurance concepts. This standard is typically derived from a normal approximation to the aggregate claim count.
  2. The correct answer is D (Approximately 1,083 claims, computed as the square of 1.645 divided by 0.05): the full credibility standard equals (z/k) squared, where z is the standard normal quantile for 90% two-sided confidence, 1.645, and k is the accuracy parameter, 0.05. Squaring 1.645 divided by 0.05 gives 1,082.41, which rounds up to 1,083 claims since a fractional claim cannot occur. Options B and E use the wrong probability level, and option C mixes an unrelated probability with this accuracy level.
  3. The correct answer is A (Approximately 1,537 claims, computed as 1.96 divided by 0.05, then squared): the standard normal quantile for 95% two-sided probability is 1.96, so the full credibility standard equals (1.96/0.05) squared, which is 1,536.64, rounded up to 1,537 claims. Option B understates the required claim count by using the 90% quantile instead of 95%. Options C and E apply a stricter 99% probability level, and option D relaxes the accuracy requirement to 10%.
  4. The correct answer is E (Approximately 385 claims, computed as 1.96 divided by 0.10, then squared): doubling the accuracy parameter k from 0.05 to 0.10 quarters the full credibility standard, since n equals (z/k) squared. Computing (1.96/0.10) squared gives 384.16, which rounds up to 385 expected claims. Option A restates the original tighter standard, while options B, C, and D each substitute a different, incorrect probability level into the calculation.
  5. The correct answer is C (Approximately 2,655 claims, computed as 2.576 divided by 0.05, then squared): at 99% two-sided probability, the standard normal quantile is 2.576, so the full credibility standard equals (2.576/0.05) squared, which computes to 2,654.31, rounding up to 2,655 expected claims. Options A and D use lower probability levels, producing smaller and therefore incorrect standards, while options B and E each relax the accuracy parameter instead of using the stated 5%.
  6. The correct answer is A (Approximately 2,165, obtained by multiplying 1,082.41 by two and rounding up): the aggregate-loss full credibility standard equals the claim-count standard times one plus the squared severity coefficient of variation. Since the coefficient of variation is 1.0, the multiplier is 1 plus 1 squared, which equals 2. Multiplying 1,082.41 by 2 gives 2,164.82, rounding up to 2,165 expected claims. Option B wrongly skips the severity adjustment, and options C, D, and E use incorrect coefficients of variation.
  7. The correct answer is D (Approximately 1,776, obtained by multiplying 1,082.41 by 1.64 and rounding upward): the multiplier for aggregate-loss credibility is one plus the squared coefficient of variation, which here is one plus 0.8 squared, or 1.64. Multiplying 1,082.41 by 1.64 gives 1,775.15, which rounds up to 1,776 expected claims. Options A, C, and E each apply the multiplier for a different, incorrect coefficient of variation, and option B fails to adjust for severity variability at all.
  8. The correct answer is B (Z equals 0.707, the square root of the ratio of the two claim counts): the square-root rule for partial credibility sets Z equal to the square root of observed expected claims divided by the full credibility standard. Here that ratio is 500 divided by 1,000, or 0.5, and the square root of 0.5 is approximately 0.707. Option A uses the ratio directly without taking a square root, option D squares it instead, and options C and E do not reflect the actual claim counts given.
  9. The correct answer is E (Z equals 1.000, since the observed claim count already exceeds the full standard): whenever the expected claim count meets or exceeds the full credibility standard, the credibility factor is capped at 1.0, meaning the observed experience is used entirely on its own. Since 1,600 exceeds 1,537, no partial credibility calculation is needed. Option C computes a value above 1 without applying the required cap, and options A, B, and D describe values that do not correspond to full credibility being reached.
  10. The correct answer is C ($856, computed as 0.707 times $900 plus 0.293 times $750): the credibility premium formula weights the observed mean by Z and the prior mean by 1 minus Z. Multiplying 0.707 by 900 gives 636.30, and multiplying 0.293 by 750 gives 219.75, which sum to approximately $856. Options A and B represent the two extremes with no blending, option D uses an incorrect equal weighting, and option E reverses which mean receives the Z weight.
  11. The correct answer is D (Z decreases, because the observed claim count now represents a smaller share): since Z equals the square root of observed expected claims divided by the full credibility standard, increasing the denominator while holding the numerator fixed reduces the ratio inside the square root, which lowers Z. This reflects that a larger full credibility standard means more experience is needed before full weight is given. Options A, B, C, and E all misstate this inverse relationship between Z and the full standard.
  12. The correct answer is A (It raises the required standard, since severity variance is added into the formula): the full credibility standard for aggregate losses multiplies the claim-count-based standard by one plus the squared coefficient of variation of severity. Greater severity variability increases this coefficient of variation, which increases the multiplier and therefore the overall standard needed. Options B, C, D, and E each incorrectly describe severity variability as irrelevant or acting through the wrong part of the credibility framework.
  13.   14. The correct answer is B (Z equals 0.800, computed as n divided by the sum of n and k): the Bühlmann credibility formula sets Z equal to n divided by the quantity n plus k. Options A, B, and E misstate this relationship as division or subtraction, and option C does not reflect how variance actually decomposes.

  31. The correct answer is E (VHM equals 6.0, computed from each class mean's squared deviation from the overall mean): the squared deviation of Class 1's mean from 13 is 9, and of Class 2's mean is 4. Weighting these by 0.4 and 0.6 respectively gives 3.6 and 2.4, which sum to 6.0. Option A confuses VHM with EPV, options B and D use only one class's deviation, and option C applies an incorrect equal weighting.
  32. The correct answer is A (Z equals 0.603, computed as 8 divided by the sum of 8 and 5.267): the credibility parameter k equals EPV divided by VHM, which is 31.6 divided by 6.0, approximately 5.267. Applying the Bühlmann formula, Z equals 8 divided by the sum of 8 and 5.267, or 13.267, which is approximately 0.603. Options B, C, D, and E each misapply the underlying relationship between n, k, and Z.
  33. The correct answer is B ($56.11, computed by weighting each year's average by its own exposure units): the Bühlmann-Straub weighted average multiplies each year's per-unit average by its exposure and divides by total exposure. This gives (100 times 50, plus 150 times 55, plus 200 times 60), all divided by 450, which is 25,250 divided by 450, approximately $56.11. Option A wrongly treats all years as equally weighted, option C ignores the earlier years entirely, and options D and E use incorrect divisors.
  34. The correct answer is D (Z equals 0.862, computed as total exposure divided by exposure plus the parameter k): the Bühlmann-Straub formula applies the same structure as ordinary Bühlmann credibility, but using total exposure in place of claim count. Substituting exposure equals 50 and k equals 8 gives 50 divided by the sum of 50 and 8, or 58, which is approximately 0.862. Options A, B, C, and E do not correctly apply this exposure-based version of the formula.
  35. The correct answer is C (Policyholder 1 receives the higher credibility factor, since 50 exceeds 30 in exposure): because Z equals exposure divided by exposure plus k, a larger total exposure produces a larger credibility factor when k is held constant. Policyholder 1's factor is approximately 0.862, while Policyholder 2's is approximately 0.789, confirming that more exposure yields greater credibility. Options A, B, D, and E each misstate how exposure size relates to the resulting Bühlmann-Straub credibility factor.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Classical credibility uses a fixed standard for full credibility, while Bühlmann blends continuously via EPV and VHM): the classical approach sets a claim-count threshold beyond which full credibility is granted, with partial credibility scaled by a square-root rule below that point. Bühlmann credibility instead derives a continuously varying weight from the ratio of expected process variance to variance of hypothetical means, never formally reaching full credibility. Options B, C, D, and E each describe relationships that do not generally hold between the two frameworks.
  2. The correct answer is E (Z approaches 1, meaning the observed experience receives essentially full credibility weight): since Z equals n divided by n plus k, letting k shrink toward zero while n stays fixed and positive makes the denominator approach n itself, so the ratio approaches 1. A small k reflects little variation between hypothetical means relative to process variance, which justifies weighting observed data heavily. Options A, B, C, and D all misdescribe this limiting behavior of the Bühlmann formula.
  3. The correct answer is B (Z is capped at exactly 1.000, and no further increase is possible beyond that): the classical square-root credibility formula, Z equals the square root of observed expected claims divided by the full standard, would otherwise produce values above 1 once the standard is exceeded, so the convention caps Z at 1.000 to represent full reliance on observed experience. Options A, C, D, and E all describe outcomes that contradict this standard convention.
  4. The correct answer is D (The variability in outcomes for a single risk class, holding its true mean fixed): the expected process variance averages, across all risk classes, the variance that exists within a single class due to random fluctuation around that class's own true mean. It is distinct from the variance of hypothetical means, which instead measures how much the true means differ across classes. Options A, B, C, and E each describe a different or unrelated source of variability.
  5. The correct answer is C (The variability among the true underlying means of the different risk classes): the variance of hypothetical means measures how much the true, long-run average claim experience differs from one risk class to another across the portfolio. It complements the expected process variance, which instead captures fluctuation within a single class around its own fixed mean. Options A, B, D, and E each describe sources of variability unrelated to what VHM is defined to measure.
  6. The correct answer is B ($300, representing the premium for the three remaining months of unexpired coverage): The unearned premium reserve is the portion of premium corresponding to coverage not yet provided. With nine of twelve months elapsed, three months of the policy term remain unexpired. Applying the pro-rata fraction of 3/12 to the $1,200 annual premium yields $1,200 times 0.25, or $300.
  7. The correct answer is D ($400, reflecting the four remaining months of the six month policy term): Two of the six months have elapsed, leaving four months of unexpired coverage. The unearned premium reserve equals the premium multiplied by the unexpired fraction of the term. Here that is $600 times four sixths, which equals $400.
  8. The correct answer is A (Because premium corresponding to future coverage periods has not yet been earned as revenue): Premium is earned gradually as the insurer provides coverage over the policy term. The unearned premium reserve represents the liability for coverage the insurer still owes on policies already written. Recognizing all written premium as revenue at issuance would overstate current income and understate the insurer's remaining obligation. This reserve is unrelated to claims reporting, investment performance, or refund rights.
  9. The correct answer is E ($800, representing the four elapsed months out of the twelve month policy term): Earned premium accrues proportionally to the coverage period that has already passed. Four of the twelve months have elapsed by September 30. Multiplying the $2,400 annual premium by four twelfths gives $800 of earned premium, leaving $1,600 unearned.
  10. The correct answer is C (It covers claims from events that occurred but have not yet been reported to the insurer): The incurred but not reported reserve, or IBNR, provides for losses from covered events that have already happened as of the valuation date, but which the insurer has not yet learned about through a claim filing. It is distinct from case reserves, which cover claims already reported. It has nothing to do with premium refunds or future renewals.
  11. The correct answer is B (Case reserves plus the incurred but not reported reserve for unreported claims): The total loss reserve combines case reserves, which are estimates for claims already reported and open, with the incurred but not reported reserve for losses that occurred but have not yet been reported. This total excludes the unearned premium reserve, which is a distinct liability for future coverage. Paid losses represent completed transactions, not a component of the outstanding reserve.
  12. The correct answer is A (Workers compensation generally carries a proportionally larger incurred but not reported reserve): Longer-tailed lines like workers compensation take more time for claims to be reported and to fully develop, so a larger share of ultimate losses remains unreported at any given valuation date. Short-tailed lines like automobile physical damage report and close claims quickly, leaving a smaller incurred but not reported component. Premium volume alone does not determine the relative size of this reserve.
  13. The correct answer is D (1.500, obtained by dividing the summed 24 month losses by summed 12 month losses): The volume weighted age to age factor sums the losses at the later age across all available accident years and divides by the sum of losses at the earlier age for those same years. Here the sum of 24 month losses is $3,150,000 and the sum of 12 month losses is $2,100,000. Dividing $3,150,000 by $2,100,000 gives a factor of exactly 1.500.
  14. The correct answer is E (1.067, obtained by dividing the 36 month loss by the 24 month loss): An age to age factor divides the loss amount at the later evaluation age by the loss amount at the earlier evaluation age for the same accident year. Dividing $1,600,000 by $1,500,000 gives approximately 1.0667, which rounds to 1.067. This factor captures the modest additional development expected between 24 and 36 months for this line.
  15. The correct answer is C (1.600, obtained by multiplying the two individual age to age factors together): The cumulative development factor to ultimate is the product of every age to age factor from the given age through maturity. Multiplying the 12 to 24 month factor of 1.500 by the 24 to 36 month factor of 1.067 gives approximately 1.600. This single factor converts a 12 month loss evaluation directly into a projected ultimate loss estimate.
  16. The correct answer is A ($1,920,000, obtained by multiplying the reported losses by the cumulative development factor): The chain ladder method projects ultimate losses by multiplying reported losses at a given age by the cumulative development factor from that age to ultimate. Multiplying $1,200,000 by 1.600 produces a projected ultimate of $1,920,000. This estimate assumes future development will mirror the historical pattern observed in the triangle.
  17. The correct answer is B ($720,000, the difference between the projected ultimate and the reported losses to date): The incurred but not reported reserve equals the projected ultimate loss minus losses already reported. Subtracting the $1,200,000 already reported from the $1,920,000 projected ultimate leaves $720,000. This reserve provides for losses from accident year 2025 that have occurred but have not yet emerged in the insurer's reported data.
  18. The correct answer is E ($1,760,550, obtained by multiplying the reported losses by the remaining development factor): Since accident year 2024 is already at 24 months, only the 24 to 36 month development factor remains to be applied. Multiplying $1,650,000 by 1.067 gives $1,760,550. This reflects the smaller amount of remaining development expected for a more mature accident year.
  19. The correct answer is D ($830,550, obtained by adding the two individual accident year reserves together): The combined incurred but not reported reserve across accident years equals the simple sum of each accident year's individually estimated reserve. Adding $720,000 for accident year 2025 to $110,550 for accident year 2024 gives $830,550. This combined figure represents the insurer's total unreported liability across both open accident years in the triangle.
  20. The correct answer is C (Historical development patterns will repeat consistently across current and future accident years): The chain ladder method assumes that the pattern of loss development observed in older, more mature accident years will continue to apply to younger, less mature accident years. This allows historical age to age factors to be used for projecting immature years to their ultimate value. It does not assume equal ultimate dollar amounts or that later claims are inherently larger.
  21. The correct answer is B (The volume weighted average, because it gives appropriate influence to larger, more credible years): The volume weighted average sums numerators and denominators across all accident years before dividing, so years with larger loss volumes naturally receive more influence over the resulting factor. This reduces distortion from a single small accident year having an atypical ratio. The simple average treats every accident year's ratio equally regardless of size, which can overweight noisy, low volume years.
  22. The correct answer is A (Apply a tail factor beyond 60 months to capture the remaining expected development): When a triangle does not extend far enough to observe complete maturity, actuaries apply a tail factor representing expected development beyond the last observed age. Assuming development simply stops would understate the true ultimate loss for lines that continue to develop. The tail factor is typically estimated from industry benchmarks or a curve fit to the observed pattern.
  23. The correct answer is C (Incurred triangles typically develop faster and more stably since case reserves capture known information sooner): Incurred losses combine paid amounts with case reserve estimates set by adjusters, so they often reflect emerging claim information earlier than payments alone. Paid triangles rely solely on completed cash disbursements, which can lag behind actual claim severity as cases remain open. Neither triangle type is inherently prohibited, and the two do not necessarily produce identical development factors.
  24. The correct answer is D (20 percent, computed as one minus the reciprocal of the development factor): The percentage of losses reported at a given age equals one divided by the cumulative development factor to ultimate. With a factor of 1.25, the percentage reported is 1 divided by 1.25, or 80 percent. Subtracting this from 100 percent leaves 20 percent of ultimate losses still expected to emerge as unreported.
  25. The correct answer is E ($2,320,000, obtained by adding reported losses to expected losses times the unreported share): The Bornhuetter-Ferguson ultimate combines actual reported losses with a portion of the a priori expected losses corresponding to the unreported share. Multiplying the $2,600,000 expected ultimate by 20 percent gives $520,000, which is added to the $1,800,000 already reported. The result, $2,320,000, blends actual experience with prior expectations rather than relying on either source alone.
  26. The correct answer is D ($520,000, the difference between the Bornhuetter-Ferguson ultimate and the reported losses): Under this method, the incurred but not reported reserve equals the Bornhuetter-Ferguson projected ultimate minus losses already reported. Subtracting $1,800,000 from $2,320,000 leaves $520,000. Notably, this equals the expected ultimate losses of $2,600,000 multiplied by the 20 percent unreported share, since the Bornhuetter-Ferguson IBNR formula reduces directly to that product.
  27. The correct answer is A (Yes, since the weighted combination algebraically simplifies to the identical standard Bornhuetter-Ferguson formula result): Setting Z equal to one divided by the cumulative development factor represents the percentage of losses reported. Algebraically, Z times the chain ladder ultimate, which is reported losses times the development factor, plus one minus Z times expected losses, reduces exactly to reported losses plus expected losses times the unreported percentage. This confirms Bornhuetter-Ferguson is a credibility blend of the chain ladder and expected claims approaches.
  28. The correct answer is B ($750,000, obtained by averaging the chain ladder ultimate and the expected ultimate equally): With a development factor of 2.0, Z equals one divided by 2.0, or 0.5. The chain ladder ultimate is $300,000 times 2.0, or $600,000. Weighting this equally with the $900,000 expected ultimate, since Z and one minus Z both equal 0.5, gives 0.5 times $600,000 plus 0.5 times $900,000, which equals $750,000.
  29. The correct answer is E (Chain ladder can be highly volatile when reported losses are small and development factors are large): At early maturities with sparse reported data, small changes in reported losses get multiplied by a large development factor under the chain ladder method, producing unstable ultimate estimates. Bornhuetter-Ferguson dampens this volatility by anchoring part of the estimate to a stable, independently derived expected loss figure. This makes it more suitable than pure chain ladder for immature or thin experience.
  30. The correct answer is C (It never incorporates actual emerging experience, so it cannot adjust to a true trend): Because the expected claims technique relies entirely on an assumed a priori loss ratio, it ignores whatever pattern is actually emerging in the reported claims data. If true underlying loss experience is worse or better than assumed, this method fails to reflect that shift until the assumption itself is revised. This is precisely the limitation that Bornhuetter-Ferguson addresses by blending in reported experience over time.
  31. The correct answer is A ($2,100,000, computed by applying the assumed loss ratio directly to earned premium): The expected claims technique sets ultimate losses as the a priori expected loss ratio multiplied by earned premium, without reference to reported losses at all. Multiplying 70 percent by $3,000,000 gives $2,100,000. This figure is used as the ultimate estimate regardless of how much has actually been reported so far in the accident year.
  32. Subtracting $500,000 from $2,100,000 leaves $1,600,000. Because this technique never adjusts for actual emerging experience, this reserve stays purely a function of the assumed loss ratio and premium volume.
  33. Practice Test 11 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B ($485.71, applying a rate change factor of 0.9714 to the current rate): The loss ratio method computes a rate change factor as the experience loss ratio divided by the permissible loss ratio. The permissible loss ratio here is one minus 0.22 minus 0.08, or 0.70. Dividing 0.68 by 0.70 gives approximately 0.9714, and multiplying the current $500 rate by this factor produces a new indicated rate of about $485.71.
  2. The correct answer is E ($857.14, applying a rate change factor of approximately 1.0714 to the current rate): The permissible loss ratio equals one minus 0.20 minus 0.10, which is 0.70. Dividing the experience loss ratio of 0.75 by this permissible loss ratio of 0.70 gives a rate change factor of approximately 1.0714. Applying this factor to the current $800 rate produces a new indicated rate of about $857.14, reflecting an indicated rate increase.
  3. The correct answer is A (An increase of approximately 10.77 percent, since the experience loss ratio exceeds the permissible loss ratio): The indicated percentage rate change equals the experience loss ratio divided by the permissible loss ratio, minus one. Dividing 0.72 by 0.65 gives approximately 1.1077, and subtracting one leaves an indicated increase of about 10.77 percent. This reflects that actual losses are running higher relative to premium than the rate structure was designed to permissibly support.
  4. The correct answer is D (One minus the variable expense ratio and the profit and contingency provision combined): The permissible loss ratio represents the portion of premium available to pay losses after accounting for variable expenses and the targeted profit provision, expressed as one minus those two components. It serves as the benchmark against which the actual experience loss ratio is compared. It is not a regulatory mandate independent of expense structure, nor is it based on paid losses versus case reserves.
  5. The correct answer is C (The pure premium method builds a new rate, while the loss ratio method adjusts one): The pure premium method constructs an indicated rate directly from pure premium and expense components, independent of any prior rate level. The loss ratio method instead starts from the insurer's current rate and adjusts it up or down based on how the experience loss ratio compares to the permissible loss ratio. Both methods do incorporate expense provisions, just through different mechanics.
  6. The correct answer is E (Fixed expenses do not vary with premium size, while variable expenses scale proportionally): Fixed expenses, such as a flat policy issuance cost, stay constant regardless of the premium charged, so they are added as a dollar amount alongside pure premium. Variable expenses, such as commissions, scale proportionally with premium, so they are more naturally expressed as a percentage in the denominator. This distinction ensures the formula correctly reflects how each expense type actually behaves.
  7. The correct answer is B (It builds in an allowance for a reasonable underwriting profit and unexpected adverse deviations): The profit and contingency provision compensates the insurer for the risk of writing business and provides a margin for a reasonable underwriting return, while also cushioning against unexpectedly adverse loss experience. It is distinct from agent commissions, which fall under variable expenses, and from investment income, which is a separate consideration in overall rate adequacy analysis.
  8. The correct answer is A (A premium deficiency reserve may be required to supplement the inadequate unearned premium reserve): When the unearned premium reserve is expected to be insufficient to cover anticipated future claims, expenses, and other costs on unexpired policies, a premium deficiency reserve is established to make up the shortfall. This is a recognized accounting response rather than automatic cancellation or an automatic increase in an unrelated reserve like the incurred but not reported reserve.
  9. The correct answer is D (Exclude or cap the outlier and rely more on a multi year average factor): When a single large claim distorts an age to age factor, actuaries often exclude or cap that outlier and instead rely on an average computed across multiple years to smooth out the effect of unusual, non-recurring events. Blindly applying the inflated factor going forward would systematically overstate future ultimate losses. Switching entirely to the expected claims technique discards useful triangle information rather than addressing the specific distortion.
  10. The correct answer is C (Reserving estimates liabilities for losses already incurred, while ratemaking sets adequate future premium levels): Loss reserving is backward looking, estimating the insurer's liability for claims from events that have already occurred, whether reported or not. Ratemaking is forward looking, using loss development and other data to set premium rates expected to be adequate for future policy periods. Both draw on similar development concepts, such as loss triangles, but they answer fundamentally different financial questions.
  11. The correct answer is B (1200): under the pro rata method, the unearned portion of a premium equals the total premium multiplied by the fraction of the policy term remaining. Six months have elapsed on the annual policy by December 31, leaving six of twelve months still to be covered. The unearned premium reserve is therefore 2,400 times 6/12, which equals 1,200. This reserve represents the future coverage obligation the insurer still owes the policyholder.
  12. The correct answer is D (1800000): IBNR reserves cover claims that have occurred but have not yet been reported, plus any expected future development on reported claims. Subtracting the 3.2 million already reported from the 5.0 million ultimate estimate leaves 1.8 million. This 1.8 million must be booked separately from the case reserves to fully reflect the insurer's true liability.

Dividing by one minus the variable expense and profit percentage correctly grosses up the rate so that after those percentage-based costs are deducted, the fixed expenses and pure premium are still fully funded. Treating both expense types the same way would either overcharge or undercharge the policyholder for one of the two cost categories.

Averaging these two individual factors, 1.35 and 1.32, produces a simple average of 1.335. This simple average approach weights each accident year's factor equally regardless of the dollar volume of losses underlying it, which differs from a volume-weighted average that would give more influence to the larger accident year. Actuaries choose between simple and volume-weighted averaging based on the credibility and stability of each accident year's data.
  33. The correct answer is D (2150000): under the Bornhuetter-Ferguson method, the percentage of losses expected to be reported equals one divided by the cumulative development factor, so one divided by 2.00 gives 50 percent reported and 50 percent unreported. Multiplying the 2,200,000 a priori expected losses by the 50 percent unreported factor produces 1,100,000 of expected unreported losses. Adding this 1,100,000 to the 1,050,000 already reported yields an ultimate loss estimate of 2,150,000. This method's reliance on the a priori estimate for the still-unreported portion reduces the volatility a pure chain-ladder projection would exhibit at this maturity.
  34. The correct answer is A (2030000): the expected claims technique bases the ultimate loss estimate entirely on the a priori expected loss ratio applied to earned premium, ignoring the accident year's own reported loss development. Multiplying the 3,500,000 in earned premium by the 58 percent a priori loss ratio produces an ultimate loss estimate of 2,030,000. This technique is most appropriate for very immature accident years where reported losses would provide an unreliable basis for projection. As the accident year matures, the actuary would typically shift toward a Bornhuetter-Ferguson estimate that begins incorporating the emerging reported experience.
  35. The correct answer is C (202.6): dividing the 9,000,000 in losses and ALAE by 60,000 earned exposures produces a pure premium of 150 per exposure. Adding the 8 fixed expense per exposure brings that figure to 158. Dividing 158 by one minus the 22 percent variable expense and profit provision, which is 0.78, produces an indicated rate of approximately 202.6 per exposure. This calculation ensures the collected premium is sufficient to cover losses, fixed and variable expenses, and the insurer's target profit margin.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (933): the loss ratio method calculates an indicated rate change factor by dividing the experience loss ratio by the permissible loss ratio, and here that is 70 divided by 60, or approximately 1.1667. Applying this rate change factor to the current average rate of 800 produces a new indicated rate of approximately 933. This 16.67 percent increase reflects that actual losses have been running higher relative to premium than the rate structure's permissible loss ratio assumed. The insurer would apply this same rate change factor uniformly across the rating structure unless further rating variable analysis was performed.
  2. The correct answer is C (22.5%): the combined variable expense and profit ratio used in the pure premium method's rate denominator is the sum of every expense provision that is expressed as a percentage of the final premium, plus the profit provision. Adding the 12 percent commission, 2.5 percent premium tax, 3 percent variable general expense, and 5 percent profit together produces a combined ratio of 22.5 percent. This combined ratio is subtracted from one to produce the denominator used to gross up the pure premium and fixed expense sum into a final indicated rate. Fixed expenses are deliberately excluded from this combined percentage since they are already added separately as a flat dollar amount.
  3. The correct answer is E (2500000): when policies within a block are written uniformly throughout the year, the average policy is approximately halfway through its term at year end, meaning roughly half of the total annual premium remains unearned in aggregate. Multiplying the 5,000,000 in total annual premium in force by 50 percent produces an estimated aggregate unearned premium reserve of 2,500,000. This simplified assumption is a common approximation for annual policies written uniformly across the year, though more precise methods such as the daily or monthly pro rata approach are used when exact policy effective dates are available. The aggregate reserve will decline over the following year as each individual policy's remaining term is earned.
  4. The correct answer is A (500000): total reported losses are the sum of payments made to date and held case reserves, which here is 2,100,000 plus 1,400,000, or 3,500,000. The IBNR reserve is the difference between total ultimate losses and this reported total, so subtracting 3,500,000 from the 4,000,000 ultimate estimate leaves 500,000. This 500,000 reflects both claims that have occurred but not yet been reported to the insurer and any expected redundancy or deficiency in the currently held case reserves. The insurer must book this 500,000 in addition to the 1,400,000 in case reserves to reflect its full estimated liability.
  5. Multiplying the 1,200,000 reported at 36 months by the 1.08 tail factor produces a projected ultimate of 1,296,000. Tail factors are typically smaller than earlier age-to-age factors since most development has already occurred by later maturities. The loss reserve for this accident year equals this 1,296,000 ultimate estimate minus the losses already reported.
  6. The correct answer is C (the chain-ladder method applied directly to reported losses): the Bornhuetter-Ferguson estimate is built from two pieces, reported losses to date and the expected unreported portion of a priori losses, with the unreported piece weighted by the percentage of losses still unreported. As the accident year matures, that unreported percentage shrinks toward zero, so the a priori-based piece of the estimate becomes negligible and the ultimate estimate converges toward reported losses alone. This is effectively the same result the chain-ladder method would produce, since chain-ladder projects ultimate losses directly from reported losses using development factors. At very early maturities, by contrast, the Bornhuetter-Ferguson estimate instead resembles the expected claims technique, since the unreported percentage is close to 100 percent.
  7. The correct answer is A (241.2): adding the 25 fixed expense per policy to the 180 pure premium per exposure produces a subtotal of 205. Dividing by this factor rather than simply adding the percentage ensures that after variable expenses and profit are deducted from the final premium, the pure premium and fixed expenses remain fully funded. The number of policies in force does not directly enter this per-exposure rate calculation once the pure premium and fixed expense per exposure are already known.

This positive result indicates that actual loss experience is running higher relative to premium than the 66 percent permissible loss ratio assumed when rates were last set.   9. Summing the 24-month values gives 270,000 plus 473,000 plus 364,000, or 1,107,000, and summing the 12-month values gives 200,000 plus 350,000 plus 280,000, or 830,000. Dividing 1,107,000 by 830,000 produces a volume-weighted factor of approximately 1.334. This approach gives more influence to the accident years with larger loss volumes, which is generally preferred over a simple average when accident years differ significantly in size.
  10. The correct answer is B (the portion of premium associated with coverage still remaining on policies in force): the unearned premium reserve exists because insurers collect premium in advance for coverage that extends into the future, and accounting principles require that revenue be recognized only as the coverage is actually provided. As time passes and the insurer provides the promised coverage, the corresponding portion of premium moves from unearned to earned. The reserve is therefore a liability reflecting the insurer's ongoing obligation to provide coverage for the remainder of each policy's term. This concept is distinct from IBNR, which instead reflects an estimate of losses that have occurred but not yet been fully reported or paid.
  11. This approach avoids the instability that could result from relying on limited or unreliable reported claims data at an early maturity. As reported experience becomes more credible over time, the actuary would typically move toward methods that give increasing weight to that emerging data.
  12. Subtracting the 165 pure premium from this 200 leaves a fixed expense of 35 per exposure. This kind of reverse calculation is useful for checking whether an insurer's assumed fixed expense provision is consistent with its published rate and other stated pricing assumptions.
  13. Any inconsistency between the two would prompt the actuary to investigate whether the development factor or the ultimate estimate needs revision.

Salvage, subrogation, unearned premium, and policyholder dividends are each separate concepts entirely unrelated to this particular reserve component.
