Practice Test 13 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (To capture the further loss development still expected to occur beyond the last observed age): A tail factor extends the observed development pattern past the final age actually included in the triangle, in order to account for loss development that is still expected to continue after that last observed point, which matters whenever claims are known to remain open beyond the triangle's stated maturity. It is not used to convert paid losses to incurred, to strip out inflation, to shift losses between accident years, or to restate premium to the current rate level.
  2. The correct answer is D (1.51, the selected volume weighted 12 to 24 month development factor): The volume weighted average sums all three 24 month values and divides that total by the sum of all three 12 month values, so (450 plus 620 plus 511) divided by (300 plus 400 plus 350) equals 1581 divided by 1050, which is 1.51. This approach gives larger accident years correspondingly more influence than a simple average of the three individual ratios would, since it weights by total loss volume rather than treating every year identically.
  3. The correct answer is C (165.00 dollars per exposure, the indicated rate for this line): Pure premium equals losses divided by exposures, so 480000 divided by 4000 equals 120.00 dollars. Adding the fixed expense of 12 dollars gives 132.00, and dividing that sum by 1 minus the variable expense ratio of 0.2 correctly accounts for variable expenses that scale directly with the final rate, producing 165.00 dollars. Failing to divide out the variable expense percentage in this way would understate the true indicated rate.
  4. The correct answer is D (3,750 earned exposures underlying this particular indicated rate): The rate must cover pure premium plus fixed expenses, all divided by 1 minus the variable expense ratio, so pure premium equals the rate times 1 minus the variable ratio, minus the fixed expense: 150 times 0.8 minus 20 equals 100 dollars. Exposures then equal total losses divided by this pure premium, so 375000 divided by 100 equals 3750. This simply reverses the usual pure premium calculation to solve for the previously unknown exposure count.
  5. The correct answer is B (An increase of about 10.8 percent applied to the filed rates): The loss ratio method computes the indicated rate change as the experience loss ratio divided by the permissible loss ratio, minus one, so 0.72 divided by 0.65 minus 1 equals 10.8 percent. This reflects that actual losses are running higher, relative to premium, than the level the currently filed rates were designed to support, and a rate increase near this percentage would restore the loss ratio back to its permissible level.
  6. The correct answer is E (63 percent, the permissible loss ratio implied by this structure): The permissible loss ratio is simply the share of premium left over once all expense and profit provisions have been removed, so 1 minus 0.24 minus 0.08 minus 0.05 equals 0.63, or 63 percent. This figure represents the loss ratio the currently filed rates are designed to sustain, and it serves as the benchmark against which the experience loss ratio is compared under the loss ratio ratemaking method.
  7. The correct answer is E (The pure premium method can be applied even without any existing set of current rates in place): The pure premium method builds an indicated rate directly from losses and expenses stated per exposure, so it does not depend on comparing anything against a preexisting current rate level. The loss ratio method, by contrast, expresses experience relative to current premium and therefore necessarily requires an existing rate level to measure against. The remaining statements each reverse or otherwise misstate this basic relationship between the two methods.
  8. The correct answer is A (Fixed expenses are added directly per exposure while variable expenses instead scale with the final premium): Fixed expenses, such as a flat policy issuance cost, do not vary with the size of the premium ultimately charged, so they are simply added as a flat dollar amount per exposure. Variable expenses, such as commissions or premium taxes, are instead a percentage of the final premium itself, so they must be handled by dividing the rate by one minus that percentage rather than by adding a flat dollar figure.
  9. The correct answer is B (22.7 percent, the variable expense percentage implied by these figures): Since rate equals pure premium plus fixed expense, all divided by 1 minus the variable expense percentage, the variable percentage equals 1 minus the sum of pure premium and fixed expense divided by the rate, so 1 minus (150 plus 20) divided by 220 equals 22.7 percent. Checking the reverse direction, 150 plus 20, divided by 1 minus 0.227, does return the original rate of 220 dollars.
  10. The correct answer is D (880, the projected cumulative loss level at 36 months): Multiplying the known 24 month cumulative loss by the selected age to age factor projects the next point along the development pattern, so 800 times 1.1 equals 880. This is the fundamental mechanic of the chain ladder method, applied here one age interval at a time using a factor selected directly from historical development observed on this same triangle.
  11. The correct answer is E (An estimate exactly equal to reported losses, matching using reported losses alone): When the cumulative development factor equals 1.00, the unreported percentage, computed as 1 minus 1 divided by the factor, equals zero exactly, so the Bornhuetter Ferguson IBNR contribution vanishes and the resulting ultimate estimate reduces to reported losses alone. This demonstrates that the method converges smoothly to the chain ladder result as an accident year matures fully, rather than ever producing a higher estimate, a lower estimate, or an undefined one.
  12. The correct answer is E (Incurred triangles already reflect case reserve changes, which usually dampens the reported development pattern): Because incurred losses combine payments already made with the claim adjuster's outstanding case reserve estimate, much of the eventual growth toward the ultimate value is already reflected earlier through that case reserve, so incurred development factors tend to be smaller and less volatile than paid development factors, which only grow as cash is actually disbursed over time. Paid losses are not systematically larger, incurred triangles are actually quite sensitive to reserving practice changes, and paid data can certainly be arranged into a triangle.
  13. The correct answer is A (63.4 percent, the combined experience loss ratio for this line): The combined experience loss ratio should be built from the aggregate dollar amounts rather than from an average of the two separate yearly ratios, so total losses of 450000 divided by total premium of 710000 equals 63.4 percent. Aggregating the dollars first in this way gives more weight to the year carrying greater premium volume, which is the standard approach whenever blending multiple years of experience together.
  14. The correct answer is A (Ultimate loss projections for the other accident years will be systematically overstated): Selecting a development factor that is inflated by a single unusually large claim, rather than excluding or otherwise adjusting for that outlier, causes the method to apply too high a multiplier to every other accident year's reported losses, pushing their projected ultimates above what the underlying true pattern would otherwise support. It would not leave every projection unaffected, and it has no direct bearing on the unearned premium reserve or on already reported historical losses from prior years.
  15. The correct answer is D (It avoids amplifying the effect of one single large claim through a development factor): Because the expected claims technique ignores reported losses entirely and relies solely on the a priori expected loss ratio, it is not distorted the way the chain ladder method would be if a single large claim in an immature accident year were multiplied by a large development factor. It does not eliminate the need for future review, it does not guarantee a lower estimate in every possible case, it still requires earned premium as an input, and it does not automatically account for any shift in business mix.
  16. The correct answer is D (The reserve would trend steadily upward, tracking the ongoing growth in written premium): Because unearned premium is simply a fraction of written premium tied to the remaining policy term, steady growth in written premium each year, combined with an unchanged earning pattern, naturally increases the pool of premium that has not yet been earned as of any given valuation date. The reserve would not stay flat, would not trend downward under sustained growth like this, does not ever turn negative from growth alone, and is fundamentally a premium based reserve rather than a loss based one.
  17. The correct answer is A (177.50 dollars per exposure, the indicated rate for this line): Combining losses and unallocated loss adjustment expense gives 330000 dollars, and dividing that sum by 2500 exposures produces a pure premium of 132.00 dollars. Adding the fixed expense of 10 dollars and then dividing by 1 minus the variable expense ratio of 0.2 gives 177.50 dollars. Loss adjustment expense that varies directly with claim volume is commonly folded into the loss figure this way before the fixed and variable expense loadings are applied on top.
  18. The correct answer is E (The chain ladder method, since it multiplies reported losses directly by a single factor): The chain ladder method always projects the ultimate directly from the actual reported losses through a single multiplicative factor, so reported losses carry full weight no matter how immature the accident year happens to be at that point. The Bornhuetter Ferguson method instead adds reported losses to a separate IBNR component derived from the a priori expected losses, so reported losses are effectively given comparatively less weight at earlier, more immature ages.
  19. The correct answer is A (A decrease of roughly 11.3 percent applied to current rates): The indicated rate change equals the experience loss ratio divided by the permissible loss ratio, minus one, so 0.55 divided by 0.62 minus 1 equals -11.3 percent, a decrease. Because actual losses are running below the level the currently filed rates were built to support, a rate decrease is indicated to bring the loss ratio back into line with the permissible level, rather than an increase of any size or no change at all.
  20. The correct answer is E (750.0, the additional incurred but not reported reserve to book): The unreported percentage under the Bornhuetter Ferguson method equals 1 minus 1 divided by 1.60, which is 0.3750, or 37.5 percent. The additional reserve to book beyond the case reserves already embedded in reported losses equals expected ultimate losses times that percentage, so 2000 times 0.3750 equals 750.0. This amount is booked on top of the reported incurred figure of 1450, producing a total ultimate estimate of 2200.0, though the question asks only for this incremental reserve.
  21. The correct answer is C (The larger of zero and S_T minus K at expiration): a European call gives the holder the right, not the obligation, to buy the stock at K, so the payoff is max(S_T minus K, 0). If the stock finishes below K the holder simply lets the option expire worthless rather than exercising at a loss. This asymmetric payoff is exactly what distinguishes an option from a forward contract, which has no floor at zero. The other choices either describe a put, a forward, or an arbitrary average with no basis in the contract terms.
  22. The correct answer is A (The larger of zero and K minus S_T at expiration): a put gives the holder the right to sell at K, which is valuable only when the market price S_T has fallen below K, so the payoff is max(K minus S_T, 0). When S_T exceeds K the put simply expires worthless since selling at K would be worse than the market. Option B describes a call's payoff instead, and the remaining choices ignore the optionality entirely. Recognizing this floor-at-zero structure is essential before any pricing formula can be applied.
  23. The correct answer is D (C minus P equals S_0 minus K times v to the power T): put-call parity follows because a long call plus a short put replicates a forward purchase of the stock at K, whose present value cost equals S_0 minus the present value of K. This relationship must hold or an arbitrageur could combine the four instruments to lock in a riskless profit. Options A, B and C misstate the sign or the operation joining C and P. Every put-call parity numeric question on this exam ultimately reduces to correctly applying this single identity.
  24. The correct answer is B (About 0.5042): the risk-neutral up probability is p* equals (e to the r minus d) divided by (u minus d), giving (e^0.05 minus 0.90) divided by (1.20 minus 0.90), which is (1.05127 minus 0.90) divided by 0.30, or 0.15127 divided by 0.30. That division equals 0.5042. This probability is a pricing device implied by no-arbitrage, not a forecast of the real chance the stock rises. Every downstream binomial price in this scenario depends on getting this ratio exactly right.
  25. The correct answer is E (About 4.80): the call payoffs are max(60 minus 50, 0) equals 10 in the up state and max(45 minus 50, 0) equals 0 in the down state, so the discounted expected payoff is e to the negative 0.05 times (0.5042 times 10 plus 0.4958 times 0). That product is e^-0.05 times 5.042, which equals 0.95123 times 5.042, or about 4.80. The down-state payoff drops out entirely because the option is out of the money there. This backward-induction step is the core mechanic tested throughout the binomial portion of the exam.
  26. The correct answer is B (About 2.36): the put payoffs are max(50 minus 60, 0) equals 0 in the up state and max(50 minus 45, 0) equals 5 in the down state, so the price is e to the negative 0.05 times (0.4958 times 5) plus zero from the up branch. That equals 0.95123 times 2.479, or about 2.36. Notice this put is cheaper than the call from the prior question even though both are struck at the current price, because the up move of 20 percent is larger than the down move of 10 percent. Backward induction always applies the same discounted risk-neutral expectation regardless of which payoff function is used.
  27. The correct answer is D (About 2.44, closely matching call minus put): S_0 minus K times v to the power T equals 50 minus 50 times e to the negative 0.05, which is 50 minus 47.56, or about 2.44. The call minus put difference from the two prior questions is 4.80 minus 2.36, which is 2.44 as well, confirming the parity relation holds within rounding. This kind of consistency check is a fast way to catch an arithmetic slip in either the call or put calculation. A meaningful mismatch between the two sides would signal a computational error rather than a genuine arbitrage opportunity on an exam problem.
  28. The correct answer is A (Pricing under p* makes the discounted stock price a martingale, so no-arbitrage pricing does not require knowing investors' true risk preferences): replacing the real-world probability with p* lets the replicating-portfolio argument price the derivative purely from observable inputs like S_0, u, d, and r, sidestepping unobservable risk premia entirely. This is the fundamental theorem of asset pricing applied to a simple binomial setting. Option E is a common misconception, since p* generally differs from the true probability whenever investors demand a risk premium. The other choices invent facts that play no role in the actual justification.
  29. The correct answer is C (The growth factor e to the r must lie strictly between d and u): if e to the r were at or above u, borrowing at the risk-free rate to buy the stock would never lose money and often profit, an arbitrage. If e to the r were at or below d, shorting the stock and investing at the risk-free rate would dominate, also an arbitrage. Requiring d less than e to the r less than u keeps the risk-neutral probability p* strictly between 0 and 1. This condition is exactly what the earlier probability calculation of about 0.5042 relied on implicitly.
  30. The correct answer is E (121.00): after two up moves the stock price is S_0 times u squared, which is 100 times 1.10 squared, or 100 times 1.21, giving 121.00. This top node represents the best possible outcome across both periods in this tree. The other listed values correspond to the up-then-down node, the single-up node, and the down-squared node respectively, so a careless mix-up of exponents produces exactly those wrong answers. Getting every node price correct is essential before any payoff or backward-induction step can proceed.
  31. The correct answer is A (21.00, 4.50, and 0.00): applying max(S minus 100, 0) at each terminal node gives max(121 minus 100, 0) equals 21.00, max(104.50 minus 100, 0) equals 4.50, and max(90.25 minus 100, 0) equals 0 since that node is below the strike. Listing the payoffs correctly in the right order matters because backward induction feeds each one into the node directly above it. Option D confuses the stock price with the option payoff, a common early mistake. Correct terminal payoffs are the foundation every later step in a multi-period binomial problem depends on.
  32. The correct answer is D (About 13.92): the value at the up node is the discounted risk-neutral expectation of its two successors, e to the negative 0.04 times (0.6054 times 21.00 plus 0.3946 times 4.50). That is e^-0.04 times (12.71 plus 1.78), or 0.96079 times 14.49, which equals about 13.92. This intermediate node value will itself become one of two successors feeding the time-zero price. Skipping straight to the terminal payoffs without computing this intermediate step is the most common source of error in multi-period binomial problems.
  33. The correct answer is C (About 2.62): the down node's value is e to the negative 0.04 times (0.6054 times 4.50 plus 0.3946 times 0.00), which equals e^-0.04 times 2.724, or 0.96079 times 2.724, giving about 2.62. The down-down successor contributes nothing because its payoff of 0.00 vanishes from the expectation. This node value is noticeably smaller than the up node's 13.92, reflecting that the option is far less likely to finish in the money after a down move. Both intermediate node values must be computed correctly before the time-zero price can be found.
  34. The correct answer is B (About 9.09): the time-zero price is e to the negative 0.04 times (0.6054 times 13.92 plus 0.3946 times 2.62), which is e^-0.04 times (8.43 plus 1.03), or 0.96079 times 9.46, giving about 9.09. This final step folds the whole two-period tree back to a single present value using the same discounted risk-neutral expectation applied at every node. The result can be cross-checked by expanding the tree directly over the three terminal probabilities, which gives the same answer within rounding. Backward induction, one node at a time, is the method the exam expects even when a shortcut formula exists.
  35. The correct answer is E (Each node's value equals the discounted risk-neutral expectation of its two immediate successor values): at every node, a replicating portfolio of stock and a risk-free bond can match the option's two possible next-period payoffs exactly, and that portfolio's cost is the discounted risk-neutral expectation of those payoffs. Repeating this one step at a time, moving backward, ultimately produces a consistent time-zero price without ever needing the real-world probability. Option C describes a naive averaging that ignores discounting and the tree's branching structure entirely. This is precisely the mechanic used across the two-period example worked through the prior four questions.

Practice Test 14 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Out of the money, since the stock trades below the strike): a call is out of the money whenever the current stock price is below the strike, because immediate exercise would mean buying at 50 an asset worth only 45, a losing trade. It is in the money when the stock exceeds the strike and at the money when the two are equal. Moneyness is a statement about the current relationship between spot and strike, not a prediction about what will happen by expiration, so option E is incorrect. This terminology is used constantly when describing binomial payoffs like those computed in the earlier tree questions.
  2. The correct answer is A (Out of the money, since the stock trades above the strike): a put is valuable to exercise only when the stock trades below the strike, since it lets the holder sell above the market price. Here the stock at 70 is above the strike of 60, so exercising the put would mean selling at 60 an asset worth 70, a losing trade, making the put out of the money. Moneyness definitions are mirrored between calls and puts, which is a frequent source of confusion on this exam. Option E is simply false since moneyness is a standard concept applied to both option types.
  3. The correct answer is E (N(d1) equals the hedge ratio, or delta, showing shares of stock needed to replicate the call): in the Black-Scholes formula, the coefficient multiplying the stock price is N(d1), and this same quantity is the number of shares a market maker must hold to delta-hedge a short call position. It is not itself a probability the option finishes at the strike, which rules out option A. Option C confuses the risk-neutral distribution embedded in the formula with the real-world probability, which the formula does not use directly. Understanding d1 as tied to delta connects the closed-form Black-Scholes formula back to the replicating-portfolio logic used throughout binomial pricing.
  4. The correct answer is B (The risk-neutral probability that the call finishes in the money at expiration): N(d2) is the probability, under the risk-neutral measure, that the terminal stock price S_T exceeds the strike K, so that exercising the call is worthwhile. This is why the second term looks like the discounted strike times the risk-neutral chance of having to pay it, mirroring the expected-payoff structure used in binomial pricing. Option A confuses this probability term with volatility itself, which only enters through the computation of d1 and d2. Option C actually describes N(d1) rather than N(d2), a distinction tested repeatedly across this exam.
  5. The correct answer is C (About 0.35): d1 equals the natural log of S_0 over K, plus (r plus sigma squared over 2) times T, all divided by sigma times the square root of T. Here the log term is zero since S_0 equals K, so d1 equals (0.06 plus 0.045) times 1 divided by 0.30, which is 0.105 divided by 0.30, giving 0.35. This value will be used directly to find d2 and then N(d1) in the following two questions. Because S_0 equals K exactly, the entire numerator reduces to the drift-adjustment term alone, simplifying the arithmetic considerably.
  6. The correct answer is E (About 0.05): d2 is always found as d1 minus sigma times the square root of T, so here it is 0.35 minus 0.30 times 1, which equals 0.05. This small positive value indicates the risk-neutral probability of finishing in the money, N(d2), is only slightly above one half, consistent with an at-the-money call under a modestly positive drift assumption. Confusing the sign of the subtraction, which would produce 0.65, is a frequent computational slip. Both d1 and d2 must be carried forward correctly to obtain the correct call price in the next question.
  7. The correct answer is B (About 7.36): the call price is S_0 times N(d1) minus K times e to the negative rT times N(d2), which is 50 times 0.6368 minus 50 times e^-0.06 times 0.5199. That first term is 31.84, and the second term is 47.09 times 0.5199, which is 24.48, giving a call price of 31.84 minus 24.48, or about 7.36. This is noticeably above the intrinsic value of zero for this at-the-money option, reflecting the time value from thirty percent volatility over a full year. The next question uses put-call parity to find the matching put price from this same call value.
  8. The correct answer is A (About 4.45): put-call parity gives P equals C minus S_0 plus K times e to the negative rT, which is 7.36 minus 50 plus 50 times e^-0.06. Since 50 times e^-0.06 is about 47.09, the put equals 7.36 minus 50 plus 47.09, or about 4.45. This can be checked directly with the put form of Black-Scholes, K e^-rT N(negative d2) minus S_0 N(negative d1), which gives the same value within rounding. Using parity to derive the put from an already-computed call is often faster than recomputing the full formula with negated d1 and d2.
  9. The correct answer is D (The call price rises, since greater dispersion of outcomes increases the value of the upside): because the call payoff is floored at zero, the holder benefits from larger upside swings without bearing a symmetric penalty from larger downside swings, so higher volatility strictly increases the call's value for any moneyness. This positive relationship between volatility and option value is one of the standard Greeks tested on this exam, called vega. Options A, C, and E all wrongly suggest the relationship could reverse or vanish depending on moneyness, when in fact vega is positive for both calls and puts across all strikes. This effect appears consistently whether the option is priced by Black-Scholes or by a binomial tree with wider up and down factors.
  10. The correct answer is C (The put price rises, for the same dispersion-of-outcomes reasoning that raises the call price): the put payoff is also floored at zero, so wider dispersion increases the chance of a large favorable downside move without a matching penalty on the upside, raising the put's value just as it raises the call's. This symmetry follows directly from put-call parity, since C minus P equals S_0 minus K v to the T, a quantity that does not depend on volatility at all, so if C rises with volatility then P must rise by the same amount. Option A is a common but incorrect intuition that volatility somehow favors only one option type. This vega symmetry is a fast way to check that a pair of call and put valuations are mutually consistent.
  11. The correct answer is B (The price generally increases, since more time allows greater dispersion in the terminal stock price): longer time to maturity widens the standard deviation of the terminal log stock price, which is sigma times the square root of T, producing the same dispersion benefit that higher volatility provides. For a call on a non-dividend stock this effect is unambiguous, since more time can only help the unlimited upside while the downside stays floored at zero. Option C incorrectly focuses only on strike discounting while ignoring the larger and generally dominant volatility effect. This time-value relationship is why longer-dated options trade at a premium to otherwise identical shorter-dated ones.
  12. The correct answer is E (The call price falls, since dividends reduce the stock's expected growth path available to the option holder): a continuous dividend yield lowers the forward price of the stock, since S_0 is effectively discounted by e to the negative delta T inside the pricing formula, reducing the expected terminal stock price the call's holder benefits from. This is why the dividend-adjusted Black-Scholes formula multiplies S_0 by e to the negative delta T rather than using the raw spot price directly. Option B is incorrect because although dividends are paid to shareholders, the option holder never receives them, which is precisely why the pricing formula must adjust downward for this lost benefit. This dividend adjustment becomes especially important later when embedded insurance options are modeled against an account value that behaves like a dividend-paying stock.
  13. The correct answer is D (About 0.245): with dividends, d1 equals the log of S_0 over K, plus (r minus delta plus sigma squared over 2) times T, all divided by sigma times the square root of T. Since S_0 equals K, the log term vanishes, leaving (0.05 minus 0.02 plus 0.03125) times 1 divided by 0.25, which is 0.06125 divided by 0.25, giving 0.245. This account behaves exactly like a dividend-paying stock in the pricing formula, with the fee drag playing the role of the dividend yield. This d1 value feeds directly into d2 and then the embedded call and put values in the following questions.
  14. The correct answer is A (About negative 0.005): d2 equals d1 minus sigma times the square root of T, so here it is 0.245 minus 0.25 times 1, which is negative 0.005. Because d2 is now just barely negative, N(d2) will sit just under one half, meaning the risk-neutral probability the account value ends above the guaranteed floor is only slightly less than fifty percent. This is a useful sanity check whenever the fee drag nearly offsets the risk-free rate's positive drift. The next two questions use this d2 value along with d1 to price both the embedded call-like upside and the embedded put-like guarantee.
  15. The correct answer is C (About 11.12): the dividend-adjusted call value is S_0 times e to the negative delta T times N(d1) minus K times e to the negative rT times N(d2). Here S_0 e^-delta T is 100 times e^-0.02, or 98.02, times N(d1) of 0.5968 gives 58.49. K e^-rT is 100 times e^-0.05, or 95.12, times N(d2) of 0.4980 gives 47.37. Subtracting gives 58.49 minus 47.37, or about 11.12. This call-like value represents the present worth of any account growth above the guaranteed floor, a component actuaries must value alongside the guarantee itself.
  16. The correct answer is D (About 8.22): the dividend-adjusted parity relation gives P equals C minus S_0 e^-delta T plus K e^-rT, which is 11.12 minus 98.02 plus 95.12, or about 8.22. This put-like value represents the present cost of the guarantee itself, the amount an insurer would need to hold or hedge to cover shortfalls if the account value ends below the floor. Deriving this from the already-computed call via parity avoids needing to separately evaluate N of negative d1 and N of negative d2 from scratch. This exact structure, an embedded put funded by fee income, underlies how guaranteed minimum benefits are valued in modern actuarial practice.
  17. The correct answer is E (A European put struck at the guaranteed amount, owned by the policyholder): paying the greater of the account value or the guarantee is equivalent to paying the account value plus max(guarantee minus account value, 0), which is exactly the payoff of a put option struck at the guarantee. The insurer has effectively written this put to the policyholder, and the death trigger determines when the payoff is realized rather than a chosen exercise date. This mirrors the embedded put valued numerically in the previous two questions, where the guarantee's cost came out to about 8.22 per unit of account value. Recognizing a guaranteed benefit as a disguised put or call is the key conceptual link between option pricing theory and insurance product valuation.
  18. The correct answer is B (A put option, priced using the same dividend-adjusted framework applied to fee-drag equity accounts): because the benefit only pays extra when the account value ends below the guarantee, its payoff is max(guarantee minus account value, 0) at maturity, the defining shape of a European put. This is precisely the structure valued numerically two questions earlier, where fee drag played the role of a dividend yield reducing the account's effective growth rate inside the pricing formula. The insurer, having implicitly sold this put to the policyholder, must reserve for or hedge a liability whose value rises with volatility and with the size of the guarantee relative to the account. Modeling these guarantees using standard option pricing tools is now a core actuarial technique for equity-linked insurance products.
  19. The correct answer is A (The policyholder chooses whether to elect the guaranteed rate only if it is more valuable than the market alternative): this selective exercise, taking the better of two possible outcomes, is exactly the optionality that gives any derivative its value, since the policyholder never elects the guarantee when the market rate would be more favorable. This asymmetry, favorable outcomes captured while unfavorable ones are simply avoided, is the same structural feature that makes a call or put valuable rather than a simple forward obligation. Because interest rates and mortality experience both affect the relative attractiveness of the guarantee, actuaries value this benefit using option pricing techniques adapted to interest rate and longevity risk rather than pure equity risk. Recognizing this optionality is what separates a true embedded option from a fixed guaranteed cash payment.
  20. The correct answer is C (The option's delta changes as the account value and time to maturity change, requiring rebalancing to keep the hedge effective): just as N(d1) in the Black-Scholes formula moves whenever the stock price, volatility, or remaining time changes, the hedge ratio for an embedded guarantee shifts continuously, so the insurer must periodically buy or sell the underlying assets to keep the replicating portfolio matched to the liability. This is the same dynamic hedging logic that underlies the derivation of the Black-Scholes formula itself, extended from a traded option to an insurance liability. Option B is incorrect because a static, buy-and-hold hedge would drift away from the true liability value as market conditions evolve. This dynamic hedging approach is now standard practice for insurers managing large blocks of variable annuity guarantees.
  21. The correct answer is E (Delta ranges from 0 to 1, approaching 1 deep in the money and 0 deep out of the money): because N(d1) is a cumulative standard normal probability, it is bounded between 0 and 1 by construction, with values near 1 when the call is deep in the money and values near 0 when it is deep out of the money. This range reflects that a deeply in-the-money call behaves almost like owning one full share of stock, while a deeply out-of-the-money call barely moves at all with small stock price changes. Option B actually describes the range for a put's delta rather than a call's. This bounded range is what makes N(d1) usable directly as a hedge ratio for dynamic replication.
  22. The correct answer is D (Delta ranges from negative 1 to 0, approaching negative 1 deep in the money and 0 deep out of the money): since put delta equals N(d1) minus 1 and N(d1) is bounded between 0 and 1, put delta must be bounded between negative 1 and 0. A deeply in-the-money put behaves almost like a short position in one full share, explaining the delta near negative 1, while a deeply out-of-the-money put barely responds to stock price changes, explaining the delta near 0. This negative sign makes sense because a put's value moves opposite to the underlying stock price, unlike a call's value which moves in the same direction. Recognizing this sign difference is essential when constructing a delta-hedged portfolio containing both calls and puts.
  23. The correct answer is B (About 0.6015): the risk-neutral up probability is (e to the r minus d) divided by (u minus d), which here is (e^0.03 minus 0.85) divided by (1.15 minus 0.85). Since e^0.03 is about 1.03046, the numerator is 1.03046 minus 0.85, or 0.18046, and dividing by 0.30 gives about 0.6015. This probability will feed directly into both the call and put valuations computed in the next two questions using this same tree. Note this up probability is somewhat higher than in the earlier 50-stock example, since the down move here is proportionally milder relative to the up move.
  24. The correct answer is A (About 3.50): the call payoffs are max(46 minus 40, 0) equals 6 in the up state and max(34 minus 40, 0) equals 0 in the down state, so the price is e to the negative 0.03 times (0.6015 times 6 plus 0.3985 times 0). That is e^-0.03 times 3.609, or 0.97045 times 3.609, which equals about 3.50. Since the option is out of the money in the down state, only the up branch contributes to the expected payoff before discounting. This price will be used together with the put price in the next two questions to confirm put-call parity.
  25. The correct answer is C (About 2.32): the put payoffs are max(40 minus 46, 0) equals 0 in the up state and max(40 minus 34, 0) equals 6 in the down state, so the price is e to the negative 0.03 times (0.3985 times 6 plus 0.6015 times 0). That equals e^-0.03 times 2.391, or 0.97045 times 2.391, giving about 2.32. As before, the up-state payoff contributes nothing since the put would be worthless there. This put price, together with the call price of about 3.50, should satisfy put-call parity exactly, which the next question confirms.
  26. The correct answer is B (About 1.18, closely matching call minus put): S_0 minus K times v to the power T equals 40 minus 40 times e to the negative 0.03, which is 40 minus 38.82, or about 1.18. The call minus put difference from the two prior questions is 3.50 minus 2.32, which equals 1.18 as well, confirming the parity relationship holds. This kind of cross-check is a fast way to verify both option prices were computed correctly without redoing the full binomial calculation from scratch. A discrepancy larger than rounding error here would indicate an arithmetic mistake in one of the two prior questions.
  27. The correct answer is D (It is never optimal to exercise early, so the American call has the same value as the European call): exercising early forfeits the remaining time value of the option and forces the holder to pay the strike sooner than necessary, while holding the call until expiration always weakly dominates immediate exercise when there are no dividends to capture. This classic result means American and European call prices coincide exactly on a non-dividend-paying stock, so the more complex American pricing machinery is unnecessary in that case. Once dividends are introduced, however, early exercise can become optimal just before an ex-dividend date, since the dividend payment lowers the stock price the option holder would otherwise benefit from. This asymmetry between calls and puts, covered in the next question, is a frequently tested distinction on this exam.
  28. The correct answer is E (Exercising early lets the holder invest the strike proceeds at the risk-free rate sooner, which can outweigh the lost time value when the put is deep in the money): a deep in-the-money put holder who exercises receives the strike price immediately and can begin earning interest on it right away, an advantage that grows as the stock price falls further toward zero, since the maximum possible payoff is bounded by the strike itself. This time-value-of-money benefit has no equivalent for a call, since a call holder who exercises early must pay out cash rather than receive it. Because of this asymmetry, American puts are generally worth more than otherwise identical European puts, while American and European calls coincide absent dividends as shown in the prior question. This distinction matters for insurance applications where certain embedded guarantees behave more like American-style options with discretionary policyholder timing.
  29. The correct answer is A (p* must lie strictly between 0 and 1): since p* equals (e to the r minus d) divided by (u minus d), the condition d less than e to the r less than u guarantees the numerator is strictly positive and strictly smaller than the denominator, which forces p* to fall strictly between 0 and 1. If p* fell outside this range, it could no longer be interpreted as a valid probability, and the discounted expected payoff formula would produce prices inconsistent with no-arbitrage. This is exactly why the earlier examples, with p* values like 0.5042, 0.6054, and 0.6015, all satisfied this bound. Verifying that a computed p* falls strictly between 0 and 1 is a useful check that the tree's inputs were set up correctly.
  30. The correct answer is C (The binomial price converges toward the corresponding Black-Scholes price as the number of periods grows large): as the number of subintervals increases and each period's up and down moves are scaled down consistently with the assumed volatility, the discrete binomial distribution of terminal stock prices approaches the continuous lognormal distribution underlying Black-Scholes. This convergence is exactly why the binomial model is often described as a discrete-time approximation to the continuous-time Black-Scholes framework. In practice, actuaries often use a modest number of periods for hand calculation and rely on Black-Scholes directly for continuous-time applications. This relationship also explains why the two-period example earlier in this set produced a price reasonably close in spirit to what a Black-Scholes calculation with matching total volatility would give.
  31. The correct answer is D (It makes the discrete binomial tree's volatility of returns match the assumed continuous volatility, enabling convergence to Black-Scholes): choosing u and d this way calibrates the variance of the log stock price change over each subinterval to sigma squared times h, exactly matching the variance implied by the continuous-time lognormal model. Without this specific choice, the tree could still be arbitrage-free but would not converge to the correct Black-Scholes price as the number of periods grows large. Setting d equal to 1 over u also keeps the tree recombining, meaning an up move followed by a down move returns to the original stock price, which keeps the number of distinct nodes manageable. This parameterization is the standard bridge connecting the discrete binomial method to the continuous Black-Scholes formula discussed in the prior question.
  32. The correct answer is B (Long the put, long the stock, and borrow the present value of the strike): rearranging put-call parity, C equals P plus S_0 minus K times v to the power T, which shows a long call is equivalent to holding a long put, owning the stock outright, and borrowing the present value of the strike to fund part of the purchase. At maturity, if the stock finishes above the strike the put expires worthless and the stock position alone matches the call's payoff after repaying the loan, while if the stock finishes below the strike the put's payoff exactly offsets the stock's shortfall relative to the strike. This kind of synthetic construction is useful whenever a call is unavailable or mispriced relative to its component parts. Recognizing these synthetic relationships reinforces why put-call parity is treated as a fundamental identity rather than just a computational shortcut.
  33. The correct answer is E (Long the call, short the stock, and lend the present value of the strike): rearranging put-call parity gives P equals C minus S_0 plus K times v to the power T, which corresponds to holding a long call, shorting the stock, and investing the present value of the strike at the risk-free rate. At maturity, if the stock finishes below the strike the call expires worthless and the short stock position plus the invested strike proceeds reproduce the put's payoff, while if the stock finishes above the strike the call's gain exactly offsets the loss on the short stock position beyond the strike. This mirrors the synthetic call construction from the previous question but with the stock and bond positions reversed in sign. Both synthetic relationships together fully characterize the four-instrument no-arbitrage structure behind put-call parity.
  34. The correct answer is A (About 95.48): rearranging put-call parity gives S_0 equals C minus P plus K times v to the power T, which is 8.00 minus 3.00 plus 100 times e to the negative 0.05 times 2. Since e to the negative 0.10 is about 0.90484, the strike's present value is 90.48, so S_0 equals 5.00 plus 90.48, or about 95.48. This approach lets an actuary back out an implied stock price directly from observed option prices whenever the spot price itself is not directly quoted or needs verification. The two-year discounting factor must be applied to the full two years, not just one, a detail that is easy to overlook.
  35. The correct answer is C (About 4.40 percent): put-call parity gives C minus P equals S_0 minus K times e to the negative rT, so 6.50 minus 4.20, or 2.30, equals 98 minus 100 times e to the negative r. Rearranging, 100 times e to the negative r equals 98 minus 2.30, or 95.70, so e to the negative r equals 0.9570. Taking the natural log gives negative r equals the log of 0.9570, which is about negative 0.0440, so r is about 4.40 percent. This technique of backing out an implied risk-free rate from observed option, stock, and strike prices is a direct application of solving put-call parity for whichever single unknown quantity is missing.

Practice Test 15 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Whole life insurance providing permanent coverage with a guaranteed cash value component): Whole life insurance is a permanent product that remains in force for the insured's entire life as long as premiums are paid. It builds a guaranteed cash value that grows over time and can be accessed through policy loans or full surrender. Term insurance, by contrast, provides coverage only for a limited period and typically has no cash value.
  2. The correct answer is D (Convertibility, which lets the policyholder exchange term coverage for permanent coverage): A convertible term policy allows the insured to switch to a permanent product, typically whole life, without demonstrating continued insurability. This protects policyholders whose health has declined since the term policy was issued. Renewability is a separate feature that only extends the same type of term coverage.
  3. The correct answer is A (Endowment insurance, which pays on death during the term or at maturity): Endowment insurance combines death protection with a savings element, guaranteeing payment of the face amount whether the insured dies within the term or survives to the maturity date. A pure endowment differs because it pays nothing on death, only on survival. This dual payout structure makes endowment insurance both a protection and accumulation vehicle.
  4. The correct answer is E (Pure endowment, which pays a benefit only if the insured survives to the date): A pure endowment provides no death benefit whatsoever, paying the stated amount only to policyholders who survive to the maturity date. Surviving policyholders benefit from the mortality of those who die before maturity, since their share of the pooled funds is redistributed. This is different from an endowment insurance contract, which pays on both death and survival.
  5. The correct answer is C (Deferred insurance, where the coverage period begins after a waiting period): Deferred insurance postpones the start of the insurer's death benefit obligation until a specified future date rather than the policy's issue date. This structure is often used to align coverage with an anticipated future need, such as a child reaching adulthood. It differs from immediate insurance, where protection begins as soon as the policy is issued.
  6. The correct answer is A ($22,500 per year of retirement income under the plan's formula): Multiplying the benefit rate by years of service and final average salary gives 0.015 times 25 times $60,000, which equals $22,500. This unit credit style formula rewards longer service and higher final pay with a proportionally larger annual benefit. Each additional year of service under this formula adds another 1.5% of final average salary to the annuity.
  7. The correct answer is D (A defined contribution plan, where the final benefit depends on account growth): A defined contribution plan specifies the contribution amount rather than the eventual benefit, and the account balance at retirement reflects contributions plus accumulated investment gains or losses. Because the benefit is not guaranteed, the participant rather than the employer bears the investment risk. A defined benefit plan works in the opposite direction, promising a specified benefit regardless of investment performance.
  8. The correct answer is B (Vesting, the point at which the accrued benefit becomes nonforfeitable): Vesting establishes the participant's ownership of the accrued benefit, protecting it from forfeiture upon termination of employment before retirement. Before a participant is vested, leaving the employer can mean losing some or all of the accrued benefit. Vesting schedules are typically structured as either a single cliff date or a gradual graded schedule.
  9. The correct answer is E (Cliff vesting, which grants zero benefit until a threshold, then full vesting at once): Cliff vesting has an all-or-nothing structure, where a participant has no vested right at all until reaching the required years of service, at which point the full accrued benefit becomes nonforfeitable immediately. Graded vesting instead increases the vested percentage in steps across several years. Both approaches encourage employees to remain with the employer long enough to earn ownership of their benefit.
  10. The correct answer is C (Normal retirement age, the age at which the unreduced benefit is payable): Normal retirement age is the age specified by the plan at which a participant can retire and receive the full accrued benefit without any early retirement reduction. Retiring before this age under an early retirement provision typically results in a reduced benefit to reflect the longer expected payment period. Retiring after normal retirement age may increase the benefit under some plan designs.
  11. The correct answer is D ($25,500 per year after applying the plan's early retirement reduction): Retiring three years before age 65 triggers a reduction of 3 times 5%, or 15%, applied to the unreduced benefit. Multiplying $30,000 by 0.85 gives a reduced annual benefit of $25,500. This reduction compensates the plan for the longer period over which benefits will be paid to an employee retiring earlier than normal retirement age.
  12. The correct answer is A (60 percent of the employee's final salary before retirement): The replacement ratio is calculated by dividing total retirement income by final pre-retirement salary, so $45,000 divided by $75,000 equals 0.60, or 60%. This ratio measures how well retirement income sources replace an individual's working income. Financial planners commonly compare this figure to a target range to assess whether retirement income appears adequate.
  13. The correct answer is B (Final average salary, averaging pay from a short period near retirement): Final average salary formulas typically use the highest three or five consecutive years of earnings near the end of a career, which usually produces a higher benefit than averaging over the entire career. This approach rewards employees whose pay increased substantially over time. Career average formulas instead spread the calculation across all years of service, often producing a lower benefit for long-tenured employees.
  14. The correct answer is E (Career average pay, which averages earnings across the entire period of participation): A career average formula bases the benefit on average earnings over the whole span of plan participation rather than only the final years before retirement. This design tends to produce a lower benefit for employees whose pay rises significantly late in their careers compared to a final average formula. Some career average plans periodically update or index past pay to help offset this effect.
  15. The correct answer is C (The plan sponsor, who must fund any shortfall to pay promised benefits): In a defined benefit plan, the employer promises a specific benefit amount and therefore must absorb the investment risk, contributing additional funds if plan assets fall short of what is needed to pay promised benefits. This is a defining difference from defined contribution plans. Because the benefit formula does not change with investment performance, participants receive their promised benefit regardless of how plan investments perform.
  16. The correct answer is B (The individual participant, whose account balance reflects actual investment performance): In a defined contribution plan, the retirement benefit is simply whatever the account balance has grown to, so investment gains and losses flow directly to the participant. Unlike a defined benefit plan, there is no employer promise of a specific dollar benefit at retirement. This shift of investment risk from employer to employee is one of the key differences between the two plan designs.
  17. The correct answer is E (Level term insurance, where the death benefit and premium both stay constant): Level term insurance keeps both the face amount and the premium unchanged for the full length of the specified coverage period, such as a ten or twenty year term. This predictability makes budgeting simpler for policyholders compared to products with changing premiums. Decreasing term insurance instead lowers the death benefit over time while often keeping the premium level.
  18. The correct answer is D (Decreasing term insurance, structured so the death benefit declines over time): Decreasing term insurance is specifically designed so its declining death benefit tracks an amortizing obligation such as a mortgage balance, keeping the coverage aligned with the outstanding debt. Because the insurer's risk decreases over time, the premium can often remain level even as the benefit falls. This makes decreasing term a cost effective choice for covering a shrinking financial obligation.
  19. The correct answer is A (The nonforfeiture provision, guaranteeing access to accumulated cash value on surrender): Nonforfeiture provisions protect the policyholder's equity in a permanent life insurance policy, ensuring that surrendering the contract still produces a cash payment reflecting the accumulated value built up over time. Without this protection, a policyholder who stopped paying premiums could lose the entire accumulated value. Whole life policies commonly rely on this provision because they are designed to build meaningful cash value.
  20. The correct answer is C (The full $100,000 face amount, paid because the insured survived to maturity): Endowment insurance guarantees payment of the full face amount either on the insured's death during the term or on survival to the stated maturity date. Because the insured survived the entire twenty year term in this scenario, the insurer pays the complete $100,000 at maturity. This dual guarantee is what distinguishes endowment insurance from a pure endowment or from term insurance.
  21. The correct answer is E (Pure endowment insurance, since it pays nothing at all if death occurs first): A pure endowment provides no protection against death, paying its benefit only to those who survive to the maturity date, with the interests of those who die earlier effectively supporting the survivors' benefit. Endowment insurance, in contrast, guarantees payment in both the death and survival scenarios. This distinction makes pure endowments a purely survival contingent product rather than a protection product.
  22. The correct answer is A (A deferred annuity delays income payments, while deferred insurance delays death coverage): A deferred annuity accumulates funds and postpones the start of periodic income payments until a later date chosen by the purchaser or specified in the contract. Deferred insurance instead postpones the start of death benefit protection, meaning no death claim would be paid if the insured died during the deferral period. These are distinct product categories serving different financial purposes despite sharing the word deferred.
  23. The correct answer is D (Unit credit method, which builds the accrued benefit from separate yearly credits): Under the unit credit method, each year of service generates its own separate unit of accrued benefit, and the total accrued benefit is simply the accumulation of these units earned to date. This approach directly ties the accrued benefit to service already performed, making it straightforward to determine a participant's benefit at any point in time. Many traditional final average pay formulas are built on this unit credit structure.
  24. The correct answer is B (60 percent of the accrued benefit under the graded vesting schedule): The schedule grants 20% at 2 years and adds 20 percentage points for each further year, so at 3 years vesting is 40%, and at 4 years vesting is 60%. This graded structure gradually increases the participant's nonforfeitable right rather than granting it all at once. By year 6 the participant reaches full 100% vesting under this same schedule.
  25. The correct answer is C (Early retirement provision, which permits a reduced benefit before normal retirement age): An early retirement provision sets a minimum age and service combination at which a participant may elect to begin benefits before normal retirement age, typically accepting a reduced monthly or annual amount. The reduction compensates the plan for the longer expected payment period compared to waiting until normal retirement age. Not all plans offer early retirement, and those that do may set different eligibility requirements.
  26. The correct answer is A (Approximately 70 percent to 80 percent of pre-retirement income): This commonly cited range reflects the assumption that retirees generally have reduced expenses, such as no longer saving for retirement or commuting to work, and may face lower income tax burdens. Individual circumstances such as health costs or debt levels can shift the appropriate target higher or lower. Replacement ratio targets are a useful benchmark rather than a guaranteed or universally applicable figure.
  27. The correct answer is E (Flat dollar formula, paying a fixed amount per year of service regardless of pay): A flat dollar benefit formula is unaffected by a participant's salary, applying the same dollar credit for every year of service no matter how much the employee earned. This design is common in some union negotiated plans where benefits are set through collective bargaining rather than tied to individual pay. Salary based formulas such as final average pay instead vary the benefit with earnings history.
  28. The correct answer is B (Approximately $17,908 after 10 years of compound growth): Applying the compound interest formula, $10,000 multiplied by 1.06 raised to the tenth power equals approximately $17,908. Because this is a defined contribution account, the eventual balance depends entirely on this kind of investment growth rather than a guaranteed benefit formula. A small change in the assumed interest rate would meaningfully change the projected future balance over such a long period.
  29. The correct answer is D (Level premium structure, keeping payments constant despite rising underlying mortality cost): Whole life insurance uses level premiums that remain unchanged for the life of the policy, even though the true cost of insurance rises with age. Early in the policy the level premium exceeds the actual cost of insurance, and this excess builds the policy's cash value, which is later drawn upon as mortality costs exceed the level premium. This structure allows lifetime coverage without ever increasing premiums.
  30. The correct answer is C (Renewability, which guarantees renewal is available without new evidence of insurability): A renewable term policy guarantees the policyholder the right to renew coverage for additional periods without submitting new medical evidence, though the premium generally increases to reflect the insured's higher age at each renewal. This protects policyholders whose health may have deteriorated from losing coverage entirely. Convertibility is a related but distinct feature that allows a change to a different, permanent, type of policy.
  31. The correct answer is D (Endowment insurance, which pays the guaranteed sum on either death or survival): Endowment insurance is well suited to this savings and protection goal because it guarantees payment of the stated amount whether the insured dies during the term or survives to the maturity date. This dual guarantee makes it a common tool for funding a future need such as education while still protecting against the parent's early death. A pure endowment could not meet this goal because it pays nothing on death.
  32. The correct answer is C (Mortality gain, the benefit to survivors from the deaths of other policyholders): In a pure endowment pool, the amounts that would have been paid to policyholders who die before maturity remain in the pool and effectively increase the benefit available to those who survive, a phenomenon known as mortality gain or survivorship benefit. This pooling mechanism is what allows a pure endowment to offer a larger survival benefit than would be possible without pooling. It is a defining structural feature of pure endowment products.
  33. The correct answer is A (To align the start of full coverage with a future point of need): Deferred whole life insurance delays the start of full coverage to match a future point when protection becomes more relevant, such as when an insured child grows older and takes on financial responsibilities. This design can also make the policy more affordable during the deferral period. The coverage still becomes permanent life insurance once the deferral period ends and full benefits begin.
  34. The correct answer is E (The plan sponsor, who remains obligated to fund promised benefits adequately over time): Because a defined benefit plan promises a specific benefit formula, the sponsor bears ultimate responsibility for ensuring sufficient assets exist to pay those promised benefits, contributing additional amounts whenever actuarial assumptions are not met by actual experience. The actuary calculates recommended contributions based on assumptions, but does not personally guarantee plan funding. This funding obligation is a central distinguishing feature of defined benefit plans compared to defined contribution arrangements.

Practice Test 16 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Portability, the ability to transfer a vested account balance between plans): Defined contribution accounts are generally quite portable, since the vested balance belongs to the individual participant and can typically be rolled over into a new employer's plan or an individual retirement account without loss of value. Defined benefit plans are usually far less portable, since their promised benefit formulas are tied to a single employer's plan design. This portability advantage is one reason defined contribution plans suit workers who change employers frequently.
  2. The correct answer is C (100 percent of the accrued benefit is vested under this cliff schedule): Under a cliff vesting schedule, the vested percentage jumps directly from 0% to 100% once the required years of service are completed, with no partial vesting in between. Since the employee has completed exactly the required 5 years, the entire accrued benefit is now nonforfeitable. This all-or-nothing structure differs from a graded schedule, which increases the vested percentage gradually across several years.
  3. The correct answer is D (Normal retirement, the point at which the full unreduced benefit becomes payable): Reaching normal retirement age and service requirements entitles the participant to begin receiving the full benefit calculated under the plan's formula without any reduction for early commencement. This contrasts with early retirement, which allows benefits to begin sooner but at a reduced amount. Plans specify normal retirement requirements in the plan document, and these requirements can vary considerably between different employers.
  4. The correct answer is A ($34,000 per year after applying the required early retirement reduction): Retiring 5 years before age 65 produces a total reduction of 5 times 3%, or 15%, so the reduced benefit equals $40,000 multiplied by 0.85, which is $34,000. This reduction reflects the additional number of years over which the plan expects to pay benefits when a participant retires earlier than normal retirement age. Different plans may apply different reduction rates or reduction methods for early commencement.
  5. The correct answer is E (Social Security integration, coordinating the plan formula with Social Security's own progressivity): Social Security integration adjusts a defined benefit plan's formula to account for the fact that Social Security already replaces a higher percentage of income for lower paid workers, aiming to produce more consistent total replacement ratios across the workforce. This can be done by providing a higher plan benefit rate on pay above a specified level, among other approaches. Integration rules are subject to specific limits intended to prevent excessive favoring of higher paid employees.
  6. The correct answer is B (The defined benefit plan places investment risk on the employer, not the employee): Because a defined benefit plan promises a specific benefit determined by a formula, the employer sponsor bears the investment risk and must contribute additional funds if plan assets underperform. A defined contribution plan reverses this allocation, since the eventual benefit is simply whatever the individual account has grown to, placing investment risk on the employee. This fundamental difference in risk allocation is one of the most important distinctions between the two plan types.
  7. Option A describes the number who have died, not a probability. Option C multiplies two counts together, which produces a number far outside the [0,1] range required of a probability. Option D and E confuse S(x) with the death probability qₓ or with an unrelated ratio.
  8. This identity also equals negative the derivative of ln S(x) with respect to x. Option A omits the necessary sign and would give a negative force of mortality. Options B, C, and E do not correspond to any standard actuarial identity relating μₓ to S(x).
  9.   11.   16.   17.   18.   19.   20.   21. Option C incorrectly keeps the rate frozen at the original age of selection. Options A, D, and E all contradict the basic structure of a select-and-ultimate mortality table.

Option E incorrectly assumes attained age alone determines mortality, ignoring the selection effect entirely. Options A, C, and D each describe unrelated policy features that do not drive the observed mortality gap.

Practice Test 17 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (μₓ equals Bcˣ, an increasing exponential function of age with parameters B and c greater than one): Gompertz's law models the force of mortality as growing exponentially with age, capturing the observed pattern that mortality accelerates rapidly at older ages. Option A describes Makeham's simpler linear predecessor without the exponential term, which is a different classical law. Options B, D, and E do not correspond to any standard parametric mortality law used in actuarial practice.
  2. The correct answer is E (μ₆₀ is approximately 0.0304, computed as 0.0001 multiplied by 1.1 raised to the power 60): raising 1.1 to the power 60 gives approximately 304.48, and multiplying this by B equal to 0.0001 gives 0.030448, which rounds to 0.0304. Options A, B, C, and D all state values inconsistent with this direct exponential computation. This same value can be checked by taking sixty times the natural logarithm of 1.1 and exponentiating before multiplying by B.
  3. The correct answer is A (μ₇₀ is approximately 0.0790, computed as 0.0001 multiplied by 1.1 raised to the power 70): raising 1.1 to the power 70 gives approximately 789.7, and multiplying by B equal to 0.0001 gives 0.07897, which rounds to 0.0790. Options B, C, D, and E all state values that do not match this exponential computation using the same parameters as the prior age-60 calculation. The ten-year jump reflects the rapid exponential growth characteristic of Gompertz mortality.
  4. The correct answer is A (The ratio equals approximately 2.5937, computed as c raised to the power 10): since μₓ equals Bcˣ, the ratio μ₍ₓ₊₁₀₎ over μₓ simplifies to c raised to the power 10 regardless of x, because the Bcˣ factor cancels, and 1.1 raised to the power 10 equals approximately 2.5937. Options B, C, D, and E all misstate this constant multiplicative factor. This age-independence of the ratio is a defining feature of the Gompertz exponential form.
  5. The correct answer is C (ₜpₓ equals exp of negative Bcˣ multiplied by the quantity cᵗ minus one, all divided by the natural log of c): integrating μₛ equal to Bcˢ from s equal to x to s equal to x plus t and taking the negative exponential yields exactly this closed form, which is the standard Gompertz survival function. Options A, B, and E omit or misplace essential pieces of the required integral. Option D replaces the exponential structure with an invalid linear approximation.
  6. The correct answer is A (₅p₆₀ is approximately 0.8228, computed from the closed-form Gompertz survival formula): evaluating negative Bc⁶⁰ times the quantity c⁵ minus one, divided by the natural log of c, gives an exponent of approximately negative 0.19502, and exponentiating this gives approximately 0.8228. Options B, C, D, and E all state values that do not match this exact evaluation of the Gompertz formula. This result is consistent with the earlier computed value of μ₆₀ under the same parameters.
  7. The correct answer is B (μₓ equals A plus Bcˣ, where A represents an age-independent component of mortality): Makeham's law adds a constant hazard, often interpreted as capturing accident and other causes of death that do not depend strongly on age, on top of the age-dependent Gompertz exponential term.
