The correct answer is B (An effective annual interest rate of exactly five percent per year): The Standard Ultimate Life Table is conventionally defined alongside an effective annual interest rate of 5%, used consistently in worked examples and practice problems across actuarial study materials. This paired assumption allows candidates to compute annuity and insurance values directly from the table without needing an additional interest rate specification. The 5% rate is a fixed convention associated specifically with this table.
  14. The correct answer is E (A radix value of exactly one hundred thousand lives at the starting age): The Standard Ultimate Life Table is conventionally set up with a radix of l0 = 100,000 lives, a round number chosen for computational convenience in producing the tabulated lx values. This starting quantity is arbitrary in the sense that it cancels out in most probability calculations, but it remains the standard convention. All subsequent lx entries in the table are scaled relative to this radix.
  15. The correct answer is D (Approximately 0.01174, computed directly as B times c raised to the power 50): Under Gompertz's law, mu_x = B c^x, so mu_50 = 0.0001 times 1.1^50. Since 1.1^50 is approximately 117.39, multiplying by 0.0001 gives approximately 0.011739, which rounds to 0.01174. This reflects the exponentially increasing force of mortality characteristic of Gompertz's model.
  16. The correct answer is A (tpx = exp[-B c^x (c^t - 1)/ln c], the standard Gompertz survival formula): Integrating the Gompertz force of mortality from x to x+t and negating gives the exponent needed for tpx, resulting in tpx = exp[-B c^x (c^t - 1) / ln(c)]. This is the standard closed-form survival formula derived specifically for Gompertz's law. Option E includes an extra term involving a constant A, which belongs to Makeham's law rather than pure Gompertz's law.
  17. The correct answer is C (Approximately 0.00901, adding the constant term A to B times c to the 60): Under Makeham's law, mu_x = A + B c^x, so mu_60 = 0.0002 + 0.00005 times 1.09^60. Since 1.09^60 is approximately 176.09, the second term is approximately 0.008805, and adding A gives approximately 0.009005, which rounds to 0.00901. This reflects both the age-independent and exponentially increasing components of mortality.
  18. The correct answer is B (A constant hazard component independent of age, such as risk from accidents): Makeham added the constant A to Gompertz's original exponential term specifically to represent mortality risks that do not depend on age, such as accidents or other external causes. The remaining term B c^x still captures the age-dependent, exponentially increasing biological aging component. Together the two terms produce a more realistic mortality curve than Gompertz's law alone at younger ages.
  19. The correct answer is E (Recently selected lives experience lower mortality than the general population until the select period ends): Lives that have just passed underwriting, such as a medical exam for life insurance, tend to be healthier on average than the general population of the same attained age. This selection effect fades over time, and after the select period the mortality rates converge to the ultimate rates that apply regardless of underwriting history. This is why select tables show lower rates than ultimate tables at the same attained age during the select period.
  20. The correct answer is B (Approximately 0.9905, multiplying the two complementary one-year survival probabilities together directly): The two-year survival probability is 2p[58] = (1 - q[58]) times (1 - q[58]+1) = 0.9960 times 0.9945. Multiplying these gives approximately 0.990522, which rounds to 0.9905. This correctly combines both select mortality rates over the two-year period for this recently selected life.
  21. The correct answer is D (It equals q_{x+3}, the ultimate mortality rate for attained age x plus 3): With a select period of 3 years, durations 0 through 2 after selection use select rates, and by duration 3 the select effect has fully worn off. At that point the rate converges to the ultimate rate for the corresponding attained age, so q[x]+3 = q_{x+3}. This is the defining property of where the select and ultimate columns merge in the table.
  22. The correct answer is A (Exactly 950, found by subtracting l46 directly from the given l45 value): By definition, dx = lx minus l(x+1), so d45 = l45 - l46 = 95,000 - 94,050 = 950. This represents the number of lives from the original cohort of 95,000 that are expected to die between exact ages 45 and 46. No rounding or adjustment is needed since both life table values are given exactly.
  23. The correct answer is C (Approximately 0.01531, dividing the difference between l40 and l43 by l40): By definition, nqx = (lx minus l(x+n)) divided by lx, so 3q40 = (98,000 - 96,500) / 98,000 = 1,500 / 98,000 = 0.015306, which rounds to 0.01531. This represents the probability that a life aged exactly 40 fails to survive the full three years to age 43.
  24. The correct answer is E (Approximately 0.00392, multiplying the five year survival probability by the one year mortality rate): By definition, n|qx = npx times q(x+n), so 5|q30 = 5p30 times q35 = 0.98 times 0.004 = 0.00392. This represents the probability that a life aged 30 survives to age 35 and then dies within the following year, combining the survival probability and the subsequent one-year mortality rate.
  25. The correct answer is A (Exactly 40 years, computed as omega minus 20 divided by 2 directly): Under De Moivre's law with limiting age omega, the complete expectation of life at age x equals (omega - x) / 2. Here e-degree-20 = (100 - 20) / 2 = 80 / 2 = 40 years. This closed-form result follows directly from the uniform distribution of future lifetime assumed under De Moivre's law.
  26. The correct answer is D (Exactly 19.5 years, computed as omega minus age minus 1 divided by 2): Under De Moivre's law, the curtate expectation of life is ex = (omega - x - 1) / 2. Here e60 = (100 - 60 - 1) / 2 = 39 / 2 = 19.5 years. The subtraction of 1 accounts for the discrete nature of curtate future lifetime compared to the continuous complete expectation formula.
  27. The correct answer is B (Approximately 0.020, derived correctly as 0.0004 multiplied by the given age): The force of mortality equals negative S prime of x divided by S of x. Differentiating S(x) = exp(-0.0002x^2) gives S'(x) = -0.0004x times S(x), so mu_x = 0.0004x. At age 50, mu_50 = 0.0004 times 50 = 0.02. This linear force of mortality corresponds to a Weibull-type survival model.
  28. The correct answer is C (Approximately 0.8452, dividing S(70) by S(50) using the given survival function): Here S(70) = sqrt(1 - 70/120) = sqrt(50/120) = 0.6455 and S(50) = sqrt(1 - 50/120) = sqrt(70/120) = 0.7638. Dividing these gives 20p50 = 0.6455 / 0.7638 = 0.8452. This reflects the survival probability under this generalized, non-uniform De Moivre-type mortality model.
  29. The correct answer is E (Exactly 88,000, multiplying the radix directly by the given survival function value): By the defining relationship lx = l0 times S(x), l45 = 100,000 times 0.88 = 88,000. This represents the expected number of survivors to exact age 45 out of the original cohort of 100,000 newborns. No rounding is needed since both given values are exact.
  30. The correct answer is D (Approximately 0.0889, dividing the difference between l60 and l65 by l50): By definition, n|mqx = (l(x+n) minus l(x+n+m)) divided by lx, so 10|5q50 = (l60 - l65) / l50 = (80,000 - 72,000) / 90,000 = 8,000 / 90,000 = 0.08889. This represents the probability that a life aged 50 survives to age 60 and then dies before reaching age 65.
  31. The correct answer is A (tpx = exp(-At) times exp[-B c^x (c^t - 1)/ln c], the full Makeham formula): Integrating the Makeham force of mortality separates into the constant A component and the Gompertz-type B c^x component, giving tpx = exp(-At) times exp[-B c^x (c^t - 1) / ln(c)]. This is the product of a simple exponential decay from the constant hazard and the standard Gompertz survival formula. Option E incorrectly omits the constant term's contribution entirely.
  32. The correct answer is E (Approximately 0.9277, applying the exponential formula directly with the given constant force): Under a constant force of mortality, tpx = exp(-mu times t). Here 5p = exp(-0.015 times 5) = exp(-0.075), which equals approximately 0.9277. This standard exponential survival result applies whenever the force of mortality is assumed constant over the relevant age interval.
  33. The correct answer is B (Approximately 0.0198, applying one minus the exponential of negative mu directly): Under a constant force of mortality, qx = 1 - exp(-mu). Here qx = 1 - exp(-0.02) = 1 - 0.980199 = 0.019801, which rounds to 0.0198. This exact exponential relationship, rather than the linear approximation qx approximately equal to mu, gives the correct one-year mortality probability.
  34. The correct answer is C (Approximately 0.9965, multiplying the two complementary one-year survival probabilities together directly): The two-year survival probability is 2p[45] = (1 - q[45]) times (1 - q[45]+1) = 0.9985 times 0.9980. Multiplying these gives approximately 0.996503, which rounds to 0.9965. This correctly combines both select mortality rates over the two-year period following selection at age 45.

Practice Test 20 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Z equals v to the power K_x plus one, paid at year end): the death benefit of a fully discrete whole life insurance is paid at the end of the year in which death occurs. Since K_x counts complete years survived, death happens during year K_x plus one, so the benefit of 1 is paid at time K_x plus one. Discounting that payment back to issue gives Z equals v raised to the power K_x plus one. The other options either omit the delay to year end or invert the exponent's sign.
  2. The correct answer is A (Y equals a-double-dot-angle at K_x plus one, the true number of payments): an annuity-due pays at times 0, 1, 2, up through the last year survived, so a life with curtate future lifetime K_x receives exactly K_x plus one payments. The annuity-certain value a-double-dot-angle evaluated at that same count gives the present value of those payments. Using K_x alone would omit the very last payment actually received. Options involving v to a power belong to the insurance random variable Z, not the annuity random variable Y.
  3. The correct answer is E (A_x equals 0.4444, since A_x equals one minus d times a-double-dot_x): the standard identity linking a whole life insurance and its matching whole life annuity-due is A_x equals one minus d times a-double-dot_x. Substituting d equals 1/21 and a-double-dot_x equals 11.6667 gives A_x equals one minus (1/21 times 11.6667), which equals one minus 0.5556, or 0.4444. This identity holds for any positive mortality and interest basis, not just special cases. The other options misapply the formula by multiplying instead of using the complement, or by treating one quantity as equal to the other.
  4. The correct answer is B (Z equals v to the K_x plus one power if K_x is less than n): an n-year term insurance pays only when death occurs within the first n years, meaning K_x is less than n. In that case the benefit is discounted exactly as in whole life insurance, giving v to the power K_x plus one. If K_x is n or greater, the life has survived past the term and no benefit is paid, so Z equals zero in that case. Options that pay on survival or that reverse the inequality describe a different or nonexistent benefit structure.
  5. The correct answer is D (The value equals 0.1080, discounting each year's death probability at its own timing): the probabilities of death in years one, two, and three from the table are 0.03, 0.04, and 0.05 respectively, found from the successive drops in the life table divided by l60. Discounting each at v, v squared, and v cubed and summing gives 0.0286 plus 0.0363 plus 0.0432, which totals 0.1080. This is the standard direct summation method for a term insurance actuarial present value. The other options confuse this figure with the pure endowment, the endowment insurance total, or an undiscounted sum.
  6. The correct answer is A (The factor equals 0.7602, discounting the 3-year survival probability of 0.88): the probability that (60) survives three years to age 63 is l63 divided by l60, which equals 88,000 divided by 100,000, or 0.88. Multiplying this survival probability by v cubed, the three-year discount factor at 5 percent, gives 0.8638 times 0.88, which equals 0.7602. This is the definition of a pure endowment, a benefit paid only on survival with nothing paid on earlier death. Option C omits the survival probability entirely, and option D confuses the pure endowment with the term insurance value.
  7. The correct answer is C (A_(60:3) equals 0.8682, the sum of the term insurance and pure endowment): an n-year endowment insurance is precisely the combination of an n-year term insurance and an n-year pure endowment, since exactly one of those two mutually exclusive outcomes must occur. Adding the given term insurance value of 0.1080 to the pure endowment factor of 0.7602 gives 0.8682. This decomposition works because death within the term and survival past the term are complementary events covering every possible outcome. Subtracting the two values, as in option A, has no actuarial meaning here.
  8. The correct answer is E (The value equals 2.7673, summing discounted payments weighted by survival probability): the payment at time 0 is certain and contributes 1, the payment at time 1 requires survival to age 61 with probability 0.97 and contributes v times 0.97, and the payment at time 2 requires survival to age 62 with probability 0.93 and contributes v squared times 0.93. Adding 1 plus 0.9238 plus 0.8435 gives 2.7673. This weighted sum is the defining feature that distinguishes a life annuity from an ordinary annuity-certain, which would ignore mortality entirely. Option D shows what the value would be with no mortality decrement applied.
  9. The correct answer is B (Recompute the entire present value formula using v squared throughout instead of v): the second moment of Z equals the expectation of v raised to twice the power K_x plus one, since squaring Z squares its exponent's base. This is algebraically identical to recomputing the ordinary actuarial present value formula with the discount factor v replaced by v squared everywhere it appears. This resulting quantity is conventionally denoted with a leading superscript 2, such as 2A_x. Simply squaring A_x, as in option A, gives the square of the first moment, not the true second moment needed for variance.
  10. The correct answer is D (The variance equals 0.0832, the second moment minus the square of A_x): variance is always the second moment minus the square of the first moment, or E of Z squared minus the square of E of Z. Here that is 0.2807 minus 0.4444 squared, which is 0.2807 minus 0.1975, giving 0.0832. Reporting either moment alone, as in options A and E, or their ratio, as in option B, does not correspond to any variance formula. This value can be used directly to find the standard deviation of the insurance benefit.
  11. The correct answer is E (The standard deviation equals 0.2884, the square root of the variance of Z): standard deviation is defined as the positive square root of variance, so taking the square root of 0.0832 gives approximately 0.2884. This is a straightforward final step once the variance itself has been computed correctly. Option A confuses variance with standard deviation directly, and option B applies the wrong operation, squaring instead of rooting. Option C repeats the mean value A_x rather than any measure of spread.
  12. The correct answer is C (The variance of Y equals 36.678, dividing Var(Z) by d squared): since Y equals one minus Z, all divided by d, and d is a constant, the variance rule for a linear transformation gives Var(Y) equals Var(Z) divided by d squared. With d equal to 1/21, d squared equals 1/441, so dividing 0.0832 by 1/441 is the same as multiplying by 441, giving 36.678. This large jump in magnitude reflects that dividing by the small constant d amplifies the spread substantially. Multiplying instead of dividing, as in option E, produces a far smaller and incorrect value.
  13. The correct answer is A (The value equals 1.9238, the certain first payment plus the discounted second payment): the payment at time 0 is certain regardless of mortality and contributes exactly 1. The payment at time 1 requires survival to age 61, which occurs with probability l61 divided by l60, or 0.97, and is discounted by v, contributing 0.9524 times 0.97, or 0.9238. Adding these gives 1.9238. Option B shows the value if mortality were ignored entirely, while option C incorrectly drops the certain first payment.
  14. The correct answer is D (A_(60:2) equals 0.9084, computed as one minus d times the annuity value): the identity a-double-dot equals one minus A, all divided by d, applies specifically to an endowment insurance paired with its matching temporary annuity-due of the same term. Rearranging gives A equals one minus d times a-double-dot, so here A equals one minus (1/21 times 1.9238), which is one minus 0.0916, or 0.9084. This relationship holds precisely because the endowment benefit and the annuity payments together account for every dollar the insurer expects to pay over the term. Dividing rather than multiplying, as in option E, produces a nonsensical result far larger than one.
  15. The correct answer is B (Z decreases as K_x increases, since a later benefit is discounted longer): because v is strictly between zero and one, raising v to a higher power produces a smaller number. As K_x increases, the exponent K_x plus one grows, so the discounted present value of a benefit paid further in the future is smaller. This means Z is a strictly decreasing function of K_x, with the largest possible value occurring when death happens in the very first year. Option A describes the opposite, incorrect direction of this relationship.
  16. The correct answer is C (Y increases with K_x until the payment cap is reached, then holds): each additional complete year survived adds one more annuity payment, so a-double-dot-angle at a larger argument value is strictly larger, up to the point where the number of payments reaches the cap of n. Once K_x plus one reaches or exceeds n, no further payments are possible regardless of how much longer the life survives, so Y remains constant at a-double-dot-angle-n. This behavior is the opposite of the insurance random variable Z, which decreases rather than increases with K_x. Option A incorrectly reverses this relationship.
  17. The correct answer is E (A_(x:n) equals the n-year term insurance value plus the n-year pure endowment factor): death within the n-year term and survival to time n are mutually exclusive and exhaust every possible outcome for the life. The endowment insurance benefit is therefore the sum of the payoff for dying early and the payoff for surviving the full term, so its actuarial present value equals the sum of the two component actuarial present values. This decomposition is one of the most frequently used identities in life contingencies calculations. Options that use division or ignore one component entirely do not reflect this additive relationship.
  18. The correct answer is A (The value equals 0.0649, discounting each year's death probability at its timing): the probability of death in year one is 0.03 and the probability of death in year two, given survival to year one, corresponds to a probability of 0.04 from age 60. Discounting these at v and v squared respectively and summing gives 0.0286 plus 0.0363, which totals 0.0649. This matches the same term insurance methodology used for the three-year case but truncated after two years. The other options confuse this figure with the endowment insurance or pure endowment values computed from the same table.
  19. The correct answer is D (The factor equals 0.8435, discounting the two-year survival probability of 0.93): the probability that (60) survives two years to age 62 is l62 divided by l60, which equals 93,000 divided by 100,000, or 0.93. Multiplying this survival probability by v squared, the two-year discount factor, gives 0.9070 times 0.93, which equals 0.8435. Option B applies discounting without the survival probability, while option C applies the survival probability without any discounting. Neither shortcut produces a valid actuarial present value.
  20. The correct answer is B (The variance equals 2A_x minus A_x squared, the second moment minus squared first moment): variance is always defined as the second moment of a random variable minus the square of its mean. Here the second moment is denoted 2A_x, using the leading superscript convention for the double force of interest calculation, and the mean is A_x. Subtracting in the correct order, second moment minus squared mean, gives a nonnegative variance as required. Reversing the subtraction order, as in option C, would produce a negative number, which is never a valid variance.
  21. The correct answer is A (The second moment equals 0.0974, discounting each squared payoff at double force): the double force of interest technique replaces v with v squared throughout the original term insurance formula. Using the same death probabilities of 0.03, 0.04, and 0.05, but discounting with v squared, v to the fourth, and v to the sixth power respectively, gives 0.0272 plus 0.0329 plus 0.0373, which sums to 0.0974. This is not the same as the ordinary term insurance value of 0.1080, nor its square, nor twice its value. This second moment is the essential ingredient needed to compute the variance of the term insurance benefit.
  22. The correct answer is D (The variance equals 0.0858, the second moment minus the square of the present value): applying the standard variance formula, the second moment 0.0974 minus the square of 0.1080, which is approximately 0.0117, gives 0.0858. This relatively large variance compared to the mean reflects the wide spread of possible timing outcomes for a term insurance benefit, since the payoff can be either zero or one of several discounted values. Simply reporting either moment alone, as in options A and B, skips the required subtraction step entirely. Adding the two quantities together, as in option E, has no meaning in this context.
  23. The correct answer is C (Variance needs the true second moment from v squared, not from squaring A): variance always requires the genuine second moment of the random variable, obtained by rebuilding the entire actuarial present value formula with v squared substituted for v throughout every term. Multiplying v squared by the square of A conflates the discount factor with the actuarial present value itself and does not represent the second moment of anything meaningful. This error persists regardless of the specific interest rate or mortality basis chosen, so options blaming rounding or a particular interest rate miss the underlying algebraic mistake. The same correct method applies equally to whole life and to any limited-term insurance.
  24. The correct answer is E (The variance of Y equals about 0.128, dividing Var(Z) by d squared): since the temporary annuity-due random variable Y equals one minus Z, all divided by d, the variance transformation rule gives Var(Y) equals Var(Z) divided by d squared. With d equal to 1/21, d squared equals roughly 0.00227, and dividing 0.00029 by that small number gives approximately 0.128. This large increase in magnitude, from a tiny insurance variance to a much bigger annuity variance, is typical for this transformation because dividing by a small squared constant amplifies spread substantially. Multiplying instead of dividing, as in option D, moves the answer in entirely the wrong direction.
  25. The correct answer is B (A_x equals 0.4000, since a higher rate lowers the discounted death benefit): recomputing the constant mortality formula for the actuarial present value with the higher interest rate produces a smaller discount factor v, which reduces the present value of every future death benefit payment. Carrying out the full recalculation at 6 percent interest with the same mortality basis gives exactly 0.4000. This confirms the general principle that, holding mortality fixed, actuarial present values of insurance benefits decrease as the interest rate increases. Option C incorrectly assumes present values always rise with interest rate, which is backwards for a payment received in the future.
  26. The correct answer is D (a-double-dot_x equals 10.600, computed as one minus A_x, all over d): rearranging the identity A_x equals one minus d times a-double-dot_x gives a-double-dot_x equals one minus A_x, all divided by d. Substituting one minus 0.4000, which is 0.6000, divided by 3/53, gives 0.6000 times 53 divided by 3, which equals 10.600. This value is naturally smaller than the 5 percent basis result of 11.667 given in option C, since a higher interest rate reduces the present value of every future annuity payment as well. Simply reporting the mortality rate's reciprocal, as in option E, ignores the interest and insurance information entirely.
  27. The correct answer is A (The variance equals 0.0559, the second moment minus the square of the present value): applying the standard variance formula, the second moment of 0.0601 minus the square of the ordinary present value 0.0649, which is approximately 0.0042, gives 0.0559. This is the correct variance for the shorter two-year term insurance on the same life and interest basis used elsewhere for the three-year version. Reporting either moment alone, as in options B and C, skips the necessary subtraction, and adding the two figures together, as in option E, produces a value with no actuarial interpretation.
  28. The correct answer is E (Term insurance variance can rise as the term lengthens, adding more uncertain timing): extending the term insurance from two years to three years adds another year in which death could occur, expanding the range of possible discounted payoffs the benefit could produce. This wider spread of possible outcomes is exactly what drives the increase in variance from 0.0559 to 0.0858 observed on this life table. This does not mean variance always rises with term length on every mortality basis, but it demonstrates that the relationship is not fixed or automatically decreasing as option A incorrectly claims. Variance clearly depends on more than just the interest rate or the first year's mortality alone.
  29. The correct answer is C (An endowment insurance, since the benefit must pay in every outcome to match): the derivation of this identity relies on the fact that the insurance benefit pays exactly 1 regardless of whether death occurs during the term or the life survives to the end of it, which is precisely the definition of an endowment insurance. A term insurance alone would pay nothing on survival, breaking the algebraic cancellation the identity depends on, and a pure endowment alone would pay nothing on early death, breaking it the other way. Only the endowment insurance guarantees a payment in every possible outcome, matching the annuity's own guaranteed structure of eventual full payment. This is why the identity is always stated specifically in terms of the endowment insurance value.
  30. The correct answer is B (K_x equals the curtate future lifetime, the whole number of years lived): K_x is defined as the greatest integer number of complete future years that (x) survives before dying, discarding any fractional part of the final year lived. This integer-valued random variable is what allows the discrete insurance and annuity present value random variables Z and Y to be expressed cleanly as functions of a single whole number. Option A instead describes the continuous future lifetime, often denoted T_x, which retains the fractional part rather than discarding it. K_x has nothing to do with a fixed terminal age or a mortality rate on its own.
  31. The correct answer is E (A_(60:3) equals 0.8682, computed as one minus d times the annuity value): rearranging the identity a-double-dot equals one minus A, all over d, gives A equals one minus d times a-double-dot. Substituting d equals 1/21 and a-double-dot equals 2.7673 gives one minus 0.1319, which equals 0.8682. This matches the value found earlier by directly summing the term insurance and pure endowment components, confirming the two computation methods agree exactly. Dividing rather than multiplying, as in option C, gives an unreasonably large number far outside any valid range for an insurance present value.
  32. The correct answer is B (Term insurance can equal zero on survival, endowment insurance always pays something positive): a term insurance pays nothing if the life survives beyond the n-year term, so its present value random variable Z can take the value zero. An endowment insurance, by contrast, pays 1 at time n even on survival, so its present value random variable is always positive, equal to at least v to the power n in every possible outcome. This structural difference is exactly why endowment insurance has a much smaller variance than term insurance covering the same period. Option C describes the reverse of the actual relationship.
  33. The correct answer is A (Y ranges from 1, on early death, up to a-double-dot-angle-n on full survival): the smallest possible number of annuity payments is exactly one, received when death occurs during the very first year, giving Y equal to 1. The largest possible value occurs when the life survives the full n years, receiving all n payments, giving Y equal to a-double-dot-angle-n. Between these two extremes, Y increases by one additional payment for each additional complete year survived, so it is never exactly zero and never decreases as K_x grows. Option D describes the exact opposite, incorrectly decreasing direction.
  34. The correct answer is D (The sum equals 0.9084, matching the endowment insurance value found by the annuity identity): adding the term insurance component of 0.0649 to the pure endowment component of 0.8435 gives exactly 0.9084. This matches the value obtained independently earlier through the annuity-based identity one minus d times a-double-dot, confirming that both computation routes agree. This cross-check is a useful way to catch arithmetic errors, since the additive decomposition and the annuity relationship should always produce identical results for the same life and interest basis. Reporting either component alone, as in options B and C, ignores the required combination of both outcomes.

Practice Test 21 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The value equals v to the n power if K_x is at least n, otherwise zero): a pure endowment pays 1 at a fixed time n only if the life survives that long, meaning K_x must be n or greater. In that survival case, the fixed payment of 1 at a fixed time n is discounted by the fixed factor v to the power n, and no benefit is paid at all if death occurs earlier. Since the payoff is always either zero or a single fixed discounted value, this random variable takes only two possible outcomes. Option B reverses the required inequality, describing a payment on early death rather than on survival.
  2. The correct answer is B (Substituting K_x plus one for n in a-double-dot-angle-n directly yields Y equals one minus Z, over d): the annuity-certain formula a-double-dot-angle-n equals one minus v to the power n, all divided by d, holds for any positive integer n. Replacing the fixed n with the random value K_x plus one, and recognizing that v to the power K_x plus one is exactly Z, transforms the expression directly into Y equals one minus Z, all over d. This algebraic identity holds for every individual outcome, not merely in expectation, which is why it can be used to translate variance results between the two random variables. Option E incorrectly claims the relationship is weaker than it actually is.
  3. The correct answer is D (The standard deviation equals 6.0562, the square root of the variance of Y): taking the positive square root of the variance 36.678 gives approximately 6.0562, the standard deviation of the annuity present value random variable. This measures the typical spread of possible present values around the mean annuity value of 11.667, reflecting the real uncertainty in how many payments a given life will actually receive. Option C confuses the mean itself with a measure of spread around that mean. Halving or squaring the variance, as in options E and B, are not valid operations for obtaining a standard deviation.
  4. The correct answer is A (A_x equals the n-year term insurance plus the endowment factor times A at x plus n): the whole life insurance benefit either pays out for a death occurring within the first n years, contributing the n-year term insurance value, or the life survives to age x plus n and the whole life benefit continues from that new starting point, discounted back by the n-year pure endowment factor. Multiplying the pure endowment factor by the whole life value at the new attained age x plus n and adding the term insurance component captures both possibilities exactly. This deferred insurance decomposition is a generalization of the term-plus-pure-endowment split used for endowment insurance. Ignoring either the term insurance piece or the deferred piece, as in options B and C, would misstate the full whole life benefit.
  5. The correct answer is E (The discount factor v squared should replace v everywhere in the original formula): doubling the force of interest delta means the new discount factor becomes e to the negative two delta, which is exactly the square of the original discount factor v equal to e to the negative delta. This is why the technique is described as substituting v squared for v throughout the actuarial present value formula, never a simple multiple such as 2v. This substitution correctly produces the true second moment of the underlying present value random variable when applied consistently. Options that describe doubling or halving v directly do not correspond to any meaningful transformation of the force of interest.
  6. The correct answer is C (The second moment equals 0.2807, rebuilding the present value formula with v squared): substituting v squared for v in the constant mortality whole life insurance formula and carrying the geometric series calculation through gives 0.2807. This is meaningfully larger than the square of the ordinary present value, 0.1975, which is why simply squaring A_x, as in option B, cannot substitute for genuinely recomputing the formula. This second moment feeds directly into the variance calculation, since variance equals this quantity minus the squared ordinary present value. Reporting the mortality rate or the plain discount factor, as in options D and E, ignores the actual calculation entirely.
  7. The correct answer is D (The factor equals 0.7602, matching the value found from the survival probability directly): since the endowment insurance value equals the term insurance value plus the pure endowment factor, rearranging gives the pure endowment factor equal to the endowment insurance value minus the term insurance value. Subtracting 0.1080 from 0.8682 gives 0.7602, exactly matching the value computed earlier directly from the three-year survival probability of 0.88 discounted at v cubed. This confirms the additive decomposition works correctly in both directions, whether building up to the endowment value or working backward from it. Adding the two given values instead of subtracting, as in option A, produces an impossible value exceeding one.
  8. The correct answer is B (A_x must always lie strictly between zero and one, given positive mortality and interest): the present value random variable Z equals v raised to a positive integer power, and since v itself lies strictly between zero and one whenever interest is positive, every possible value Z can take also lies strictly between zero and one. An expectation, being a weighted average of values that all lie strictly between zero and one, must itself lie strictly between zero and one. This bound holds regardless of the specific mortality table used, as long as interest and mortality are both genuinely positive. Options claiming A_x always equals a fixed number, such as one half or zero, ignore how it actually depends on the life's mortality and interest basis.
  9. The correct answer is E (a-double-dot_x must lie between one and the reciprocal of d, given positive mortality): the smallest possible value of the annuity random variable Y is 1, occurring on death in the first year, so the expected value must be at least 1. The largest possible value, the reciprocal of d, corresponds to an annuity-certain lasting forever, which no life annuity with genuinely positive mortality can ever fully reach or exceed in expectation. Since Y always lies strictly between these two bounds under positive mortality, a-double-dot_x, being its expectation, must also lie strictly within that same range. Option D incorrectly claims there is no upper limit at all, ignoring the reciprocal-of-d ceiling implied by the interest rate.
  10. The correct answer is A (The variance is extremely small, since the second moment and squared first moment nearly coincide): since the second moment 0.8252 and the square of the first moment, also about 0.8252, are so close together, subtracting one from the other in the variance formula leaves only a tiny positive remainder. This happens because a short two-year endowment insurance pays a similarly sized discounted amount whether death occurs in either year or survival is achieved, narrowing the spread of possible outcomes considerably. This is a general pattern for endowment insurance benefits, which tend to have much smaller variance than a comparable term insurance covering the same period. Interest rate information has already been fully incorporated into the given moment values, so option D is unnecessary.
  11. The correct answer is C (Endowment insurance pays a similarly discounted amount regardless of outcome, narrowing the spread): a term insurance either pays a discounted death benefit or pays exactly zero on survival, creating a wide gap between its two possible extreme outcomes. An endowment insurance instead pays a similarly sized amount close to the discount factor v to the power n whether death occurs late in the term or survival is achieved, since those two outcomes produce nearly the same discounted value. This narrower spread of possible payoffs directly translates into a much smaller variance for the endowment insurance benefit. Option A overstates this as a universal rule rather than the specific mechanism actually driving the difference here.
  12. The correct answer is E (The variance of Y equals about 26.37, dividing Var(Z) by d squared): applying the same variance transformation used for the 5 percent basis, Var(Y) equals Var(Z) divided by d squared. With d equal to 3/53, d squared is roughly 0.0032, and dividing 0.0845 by that small figure gives approximately 26.37. This value is smaller than the corresponding 36.678 found earlier at 5 percent interest, since the larger d at the higher interest rate reduces the amplifying effect of the division. Multiplying by d squared instead of dividing, as in option C, moves the result far too small to be correct.
  13. The correct answer is D (The variance equals 0.0845, the second moment minus the square of the present value): subtracting the square of A_x, which is 0.4000 squared or 0.1600, from the second moment of 0.2445, gives 0.0845. This is the correct variance of the whole life insurance benefit at the higher 6 percent interest rate, using the same constant mortality assumption applied earlier at 5 percent. Comparing this to the 5 percent variance of 0.0832 shows the variance actually rose slightly even as the mean present value fell from 0.4444 to 0.4000. Reporting either moment alone, as in options A and B, or their sum, as in option E, does not represent a valid variance calculation.
  14. The correct answer is C (A rising interest rate can lower the present value while still raising the variance): the actuarial present value A_x and its variance are computed by two structurally different formulas, so there is no guarantee they must move in the same direction as any single input changes. In this case, the higher interest rate reduces the discounted mean payoff, lowering A_x, while simultaneously widening the relative spread among the possible discounted outcomes enough to raise the variance slightly. This is a genuine, mathematically valid outcome rather than an error, and it illustrates why both quantities should be computed independently rather than assumed to track one another. Option A incorrectly assumes a fixed directional relationship that does not actually hold in general.
  15. The correct answer is A (The identity holds exactly for any valid mortality table and positive interest rate): the identity follows purely from the algebraic relationship Y equals one minus Z, all over d, taking expectations of both sides. Since this pathwise algebraic relationship between Z and Y holds regardless of the specific mortality table or interest rate chosen, the resulting identity between their expectations, A_x and a-double-dot_x, also holds universally and exactly, not just approximately. This makes the identity one of the most reliable cross-checks available when computing either quantity, since a mismatch between a computed A_x and a computed a-double-dot_x reveals an arithmetic error somewhere. Restricting it to a specific mortality assumption or interest rate, as in options B and C, is unnecessary and incorrect.
  16. The correct answer is B (For every outcome, Z equals one minus d times Y, since both derive from K_x): rearranging the pathwise identity Y equals one minus Z, all over d, directly gives Z equals one minus d times Y, which holds for every single possible outcome of K_x, not merely on average. This deeper relationship is what makes it possible to convert variance results between Z and Y using the simple scaling rule Var(Y) equals Var(Z) divided by d squared. Because the relationship holds pathwise rather than only in expectation, it applies regardless of the interest rate chosen, so option E incorrectly restricts it to a limiting special case. Reversing the sign or the roles of Z and Y, as in options C and D, misstates the actual algebraic connection between them.
  17. The correct answer is B (The annuity-due present value under this policy equals approximately 14.28 per unit benefit.): The annual discount rate is d = 0.05/1.05 = 1/21. Rearranging A_x = 1 - d times a-double-dot_x gives a-double-dot_45 = (1 - 0.32)/(1/21) = 0.68 times 21 = 14.28. This identity always holds for a fully discrete whole life insurance paired with a whole life annuity-due on the same life. The other listed values result from using the wrong discount rate or arithmetic slips.

  21. This closed-form result avoids integrating the exponential density directly. The remaining values arise from dividing by the wrong denominator or swapping mu and delta.

This follows because the endowment insurance decomposes exactly into these two mutually exclusive components. The remaining options either restate one of the given inputs or add instead of subtracting.
  28. The correct answer is D (The annuity-due present value under this policy equals approximately 5.56 per unit benefit.): With d = 0.055/1.055 = 0.052133, rearranging A_x:n = 1 - d times a-double-dot_x:n gives a-double-dot_62:8 = (1 - 0.71)/0.052133 = 0.29/0.052133 = 5.5629, which rounds to 5.56. This short 8-year temporary annuity-due naturally produces a smaller value than a whole life annuity-due on the same life would. The remaining options come from an incorrect discount rate or arithmetic slip.
  29. The correct answer is B (The standard deviation of the present value random variable equals approximately 0.1700.): The standard deviation is the square root of the variance, so SD(Z) = sqrt(0.0289) = 0.17. This computation requires only the definition of standard deviation once the variance has been established through the double-force-of-interest technique or another method. The other listed values restate the variance or come from an incorrect square root.
  30. The correct answer is E (The variance of the present value random variable Z equals approximately 16.38.): With d = 0.065/1.065 = 0.061033, d^2 = 0.0037250, so Var(Y) = 0.061/0.0037250 = 16.3757, which rounds to 16.38. This scaling relationship follows from Y = (1-Z)/d applied to the same whole life insurance and annuity-due pair. The other options use an incorrect power of d or an inconsistent rounding of the discount rate.
  31. The correct answer is A (The pure endowment component approaches zero and the endowment insurance converges to the whole life insurance A_x.): As n grows without bound, the probability of surviving n years from any fixed age approaches zero, so the pure endowment term nEx vanishes in the limit. Consequently the endowment insurance A_x:n, which equals the term insurance plus the pure endowment, converges to the term insurance evaluated over an unbounded horizon, which is exactly the whole life insurance A_x. The remaining statements reverse which component vanishes or confuse the insurance with the annuity-due.
  32. The correct answer is C (The standard deviation of the present value random variable equals approximately 0.1926.): First compute the variance using Var(Z) = 2A_x - (A_x)^2 = 0.11 - (0.27)^2 = 0.11 - 0.0729 = 0.0371. Taking the square root gives SD(Z) = sqrt(0.0371) = 0.1926. This two-step process combines the double-force-of-interest variance formula with the ordinary definition of standard deviation. The other listed values restate the variance, one of the given inputs, or an unrelated rounding.
  33. The correct answer is C (A_x equals about 0.2857, the correct ratio of qv to one minus the survival discount term): with constant q equals 0.02 and v equals 1 divided by 1.05, A_x equals qv divided by 1 minus (1 minus q)v. This gives 0.019048 divided by 0.066667, which equals 0.2857. The other choices misuse the formula by dropping the denominator or substituting the wrong quantities. This closed form arises because the future lifetime is geometric under constant mortality.
  34. The correct answer is A (Var(Z) equals about 0.0845, found from E of Z squared minus A_x squared correctly): here A_x equals 0.4000 exactly, and E of Z squared is computed by replacing v with v squared in the same formula, giving 0.244493. Subtracting A_x squared, 0.160000, from 0.244493 yields 0.084493. Options B, C, and D each stop at an intermediate quantity rather than completing the variance calculation. The double force trick of squaring the discount factor is essential for this step.

Practice Test 23 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (A-bar-x equals about 0.3750, the mortality force divided by the sum of both forces): under a constant force of mortality mu and constant force of interest delta, A-bar-x equals mu divided by mu plus delta. Substituting mu equals 0.03 and delta equals 0.05 gives 0.03 divided by 0.08, which equals 0.375. Option A inverts the roles of mu and delta, while E and C confuse the formula with unrelated arithmetic. This shortcut only holds because a constant force gives an exponentially distributed future lifetime.
  2. The correct answer is B (Var(Z) equals about 0.0898, correctly obtained after subtracting the squared present value from the doubled force result): A-bar-x equals 0.025 divided by 0.07, or 0.357143, while the present value at 2delta equals 0.025 divided by 0.115, or 0.217391. Subtracting the square of the first from the second gives 0.217391 minus 0.127551, which equals 0.089840. Choices A, C, and D each represent one piece of the calculation left incomplete. Doubling the force of interest inside the exponential is the standard shortcut for computing this variance.
  3. Substituting the given values gives 0.028302 times 0.357936 divided by 0.084906, which equals 0.1193. Option A is the whole life value for the same mortality and interest assumptions, while C is the pure endowment factor instead. Term insurance only pays if death occurs strictly within the stated period.
  4. This equals 0.613913 times 0.776318, which equals 0.4766. Option A uses only the interest discounting and ignores mortality, while C does the reverse. A pure endowment pays nothing at all if death occurs before the term ends.
  5. This additive relationship holds because the two components are defined on mutually exclusive and exhaustive events.
  6. The right side must first be discounted back one year at the stated 4% valuation interest rate before subtracting the starting policy value, since (tV+P) is the amount at the start of the year, not the end.
  3. The correct answer is E (Interest on the policy value plus the premium rate, minus mortality times the net amount at risk): Thiele's equation states that the reserve grows through interest credited on the existing reserve and through incoming premium income, while it is reduced by the mortality charge applied to the net amount at risk, giving dV/dt = interest on V(t) plus the premium rate minus mu times the net amount at risk. Reversing which term multiplies the mortality rate, or dropping the premium rate entirely, misstates the balance the equation expresses. This mirrors the discrete recursive relation in continuous time.
  4. The correct answer is C (The death benefit minus the policy value at that instant, b(t) minus V(t)): The net amount at risk is the extra amount the insurer must pay beyond the reserve already held if death occurs immediately, so it equals the death benefit minus the current policy value, b(t) minus V(t). This is the same net-amount-at-risk concept used in the discrete recursive formula, just expressed continuously. Reversing the subtraction or using division instead does not represent the insurer's actual extra exposure.
  5. The correct answer is E (The discrete recursive relation that projects policy values from one duration to the next): Thiele's differential equation is the continuous analogue of the discrete recursive relation for policy values, since both express how a reserve evolves period by period (or instant by instant) from interest, premium income, and mortality cost. The equivalence principle instead concerns setting the premium at issue, not the evolution of the reserve afterward. Confusing the two obscures why Thiele's equation takes a differential rather than an algebraic form.
  6. The correct answer is C (Zero, because the net premium reserve at issue is zero under the equivalence principle): Because the equivalence principle sets the expected present value of future premiums equal to the expected present value of future benefits at issue, the prospective policy value at time zero is exactly zero, which is the standard initial condition used when solving Thiele's differential equation. Treating V(0) as equal to the benefit or as negative misunderstands what the equivalence principle guarantees at the start of the contract. Thiele's equation applies from time zero onward, not only after the first year.
  7. The correct answer is E (It decreases, since the mortality term is subtracted more heavily from the growth rate): Since the mortality term mu_(x+t) times the net amount at risk is subtracted on the right-hand side of Thiele's equation, a larger force of mortality subtracts more from the growth rate, so dV/dt decreases (or becomes more negative) at that instant. Mortality does not increase the growth rate, and the interest and premium terms remain in the equation regardless of the mortality level. No offsetting change to the premium rate is required for this direct effect to occur.
  8. The correct answer is A (The expected present value of future net premiums, evaluated at policy issue): The equivalence principle is defined precisely as setting the expected present value of future benefits equal to the expected present value of future net premiums at the moment of issue, with no allowance for expenses in that equation. Gross premiums additionally include expense loadings and belong to a separate gross premium equation. Past accumulated premiums and total company surplus are retrospective or financial reporting concepts, not part of the equivalence principle itself.
  9. The correct answer is A (The insurer's expected future expenses associated with administering the policy): The gross premium equation adds the insurer's expected future expenses, such as commissions, per-policy costs, and percentage-of-premium loadings, to the benefit side of the equivalence relationship, which is exactly what separates it from the pure net premium equation. Changing the interest rate, mortality assumption, benefit amount, or premium period would produce a different net premium, not a gross premium, since those inputs can vary independently of whether expenses are included. Expenses are the defining addition that converts a net premium calculation into a gross premium calculation.
  10. The correct answer is B (The expected cost of the death benefit and the survival-weighted reserve carried forward): The recursive relation's right-hand side splits the year's outcome into the expected cost of paying the death benefit to those who die (mortality rate times benefit) and the reserve carried forward for those who survive, weighted by the survival probability, which together capture the year's mortality experience. Premium and interest appear on the left-hand side as the funds available at the start of the year, not the mortality experience itself. Expenses, commissions, and the gross-versus-net premium distinction play no role in this particular recursive relationship.
  11. The correct answer is B (They produce exactly the same policy value at every single duration): As long as premiums were originally set by the equivalence principle and the same assumptions are used throughout, the prospective and retrospective formulas are mathematically equivalent and produce identical policy values at every duration, not merely at issue. Neither method is systematically larger than the other, and the retrospective method can be applied at any duration, not only at the end of premium payment. Both methods rely on the same mortality and interest assumptions, so one is not exempt from mortality data while the other requires it.
  12. The correct answer is A (Future expected benefits minus future expected net premiums, from the valuation date forward): The prospective policy value is defined as the expected present value of future benefits minus the expected present value of future net premiums, viewed strictly from the valuation date forward. Reversing the order would make an in-force policy's reserve negative in the ordinary case, which contradicts how reserves behave for policies like whole life and endowment insurance. Past accumulated premiums minus past benefit costs instead describes the retrospective method, and the gross-minus-net premium difference is an unrelated expense-loading concept.

This premium is paid annually throughout the 25-year term while the insured remains alive.

  33. Substituting gives 100,000 times 0.5 minus 2,400 times 12, which equals 50,000 minus 28,800, or 21,200. This value represents the reserve the insurer must hold looking forward from time t.

Substituting gives 2,000 times 5 minus 100,000 times 0.04, equal to 6,000, divided by 0.5, which equals 12,000. The retrospective and prospective methods must agree at any duration under consistent assumptions.

Practice Test 29 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E ($661.22, the policy value one year later under the recursive relation): the recursive relation states that the reserve plus premium accumulated with interest equals the expected death benefit cost plus the survival probability times next year's reserve. Solving 848 minus 200, which equals 648, and dividing by p_{x+t} of 0.98 gives 661.22. A common error is forgetting to divide by the survival probability, which would leave the answer at 648.00 instead.
  2. The correct answer is A (delta times V(t), plus the premium rate, minus the benefit minus V(t) times the mortality force): Thiele's equation states that V prime of t equals delta times V(t) plus the premium rate minus the net amount at risk times the force of mortality. The net amount at risk is the benefit minus the current reserve, so it must be subtracted using that exact difference. Getting the sign or the subtraction order wrong, as in the other options, misstates how mortality reduces the reserve's growth rate.
  3. The correct answer is D ($1,000 per year, the net premium satisfying the equivalence principle for this term policy): the net premium equals the benefit times A^1_{x:15} divided by ä_{x:15}. Substituting gives 150,000 times 0.07 divided by 10.5, which equals 10,500 divided by 10.5, or 1,000. This premium is level and payable only while the policy remains in force during the 15 year term.
  4. The correct answer is C ($7,000 per year, the net premium satisfying the equivalence principle for this limited payment policy): for a limited payment whole life policy, the premium equals the benefit times A_x divided by the temporary annuity ä_{x:20}, since premiums stop after 20 years even though coverage continues for life. Substituting gives 200,000 times 0.4725 divided by 13.5, which equals 94,500 divided by 13.5, or 7,000. The shorter premium payment period raises the annual premium compared to paying for the whole of life.
  5. The correct answer is E ($6,500 per year, the net premium satisfying the equivalence principle for this endowment): the net premium equals the benefit times A_{x:10} divided by ä_{x:10}. Substituting gives 80,000 times 0.65 divided by 8, which equals 52,000 divided by 8, or 6,500. The endowment insurance factor already reflects both the death benefit and the survival benefit paid at the end of the 10 year term.
  6. Substituting gives 12G equals 40,500 plus 120 plus 180 plus 1.8G, so 10.2G equals 40,800 and G equals 4,000. This premium is larger than the corresponding net premium because it also funds the stated expenses.
  7. Substituting gives 100,000 times 0.6 minus 5,000 times 7, which equals 60,000 minus 35,000, or 25,000. This reflects the remaining obligation on the endowment contract from this duration forward.
  8. Substituting gives 2,500 times 4 minus 100,000 times 0.03, equal to 7,000, divided by 0.4, which equals 17,500. This method accumulates historical premium income net of the historical cost of insurance forward to duration t.

  10. Multiplying this net amount at risk by the force of mortality gives the expected instantaneous cost of insurance. This term reduces the growth rate of the reserve relative to pure interest accumulation on the existing balance.

  23. The correct answer is E (the mortality term vanishes and the reserve grows only from interest plus premium income): when the benefit equals the reserve, the net amount at risk of b(t) minus V(t) is zero, so the mortality term in Thiele's equation drops out entirely. What remains is V prime of t equals delta times V(t) plus the premium rate, an equation driven purely by interest accumulation and incoming premium. This special case arises, for example, in certain deposit type or investment linked contracts where the benefit tracks the account value.

Both formulas express the same underlying idea, that the reserve grows from interest and premium income but is reduced by the cost of providing the net amount at risk. This relationship shows that the discrete and continuous approaches to policy valuation are fundamentally consistent with one another.
